# Consideration of funding proposals - Addendum V

## Funding proposal package for FP257

### Summary

This addendum contains the following seven parts:
- a) A funding proposal titled "RE-GAIN: Scaling solutions for food loss in Africa";
- b) No-objection letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or fiscal position;
- c) Environmental and social report(s) disclosure;

This document is prepared and transmitted for consideration by the GCF Board at a Board meeting. It is published on the GCF public website in that context, which does not imply a personal opinion. Decisions taken in relation to this document and updates to it if any will be made publicly available in accordance with applicable GCF policies.

# NOTE TO ACCREDITED ENTITIES ON THE USE OF THE FUNDING PROPOSAL TEMPLATE

Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.

All proposals must be submitted on A4 paper (210 x 297 mm) and typed with the details provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected in the same format.

The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 35. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standards.

The recommended font is Arial, size 11.

Upon submission of the funding proposal, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website simultaneously with the submission to the Board, subject to the publication of information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. Accredited Entities are asked to fill in information on disclosure in section G.4.

fundingproposal@gcfund.org

A.20.Executing Entity Information

A.21.Executive summary
Sub-Saharan Africa is at a crossroads with an unprecedented opportunity and challenges for agricultural transformation, driven by the demands of a rapidly growing population of 1.5 billion and the pressures of a changing climate. The vicious cycle of poverty and environmental degradation in Africa is evident as extreme weather events have increased in frequency and intensity due to climate change, leading to climate shocks, which perpetuate persistent food insecurity and poverty. The effects of climate change are already being felt across all sectors of society, including agriculture, water resources, health, and energy — exacerbating the continent's food insecurity and climate vulnerability challenges.

The impacts of climate change have increased over the past decades in Africa, manifesting in more frequent and severe droughts, floods, heatwaves, pests outbreaks, landslides, desertification, and sandstorms. These extreme weather events have resulted in considerable economic losses for African countries, with agriculture being one of the most affected sectors.

African countries already face recurrent droughts that affect growing seasons, often leading to short production periods and low yields. In some cases, the yield can be reduced by up to 50%, while in others it may fall as much as 80%. The impact on crop yield in some countries could reach as much as 50% by 2030, and one crop revenues may fall by up to 90%. This is not only a problem for smallholder farmers but also for the food sector. However, it is not only by change in crop production but also by changes throughout the entire value chain, including post-harvest.

While there is a wealth of information on the observed and projected impacts of climate change on crop production pre-harvest (e.g., germination, tillage, cultivation, maturation, seed or grain quality, and most at all during the growing season), there are fewer studies that address the effects of climate change on harvesting, post-harvest handling and storage (e.g., threshing, sorting, drying, warehousing), and processing and marketing.

On the range of climate hazards that affect harvest and post-harvest value chains of cereal crops and legumes, scholarly publications and country experts pointed to variable, erratic, and unpredictable rainfall (including an increase in heavy rainfall events), high temperatures (extreme heat), and flooding as the priority hazards of concern. Literature and stakeholders also highlighted drought as an

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.1 PAGE 5

## Harvesting Processes: Shattering of the grain due to unusually heavy rains; challenges to field drying and storage; damage to stored harvest from flooding; pest and rodent infestation in the field due to high humidity; damage to stored harvest from flooding; pest and rodents.

### Post-Harvest Losses: Damage to harvested grains during transport, handling, and storage; from high temperatures and humidity; rotting, mold, and mycotoxin ( aflatoxin) contamination due to damage to storage facilities.

#### Processing, Transport, and Logistics: damaged or inaccessible road networks and transport infrastructure; damage to processing equipment and machinery; damage to grain quality from excessive rainfall and flooding; damage to or decay of grains/quality from high temperatures and humidity; disruption in the availability of mobility and access to markets due to extreme weather, impeding distribution and marketing.

8. In addition to the impacts of climate change on post-harvest food losses, such losses also contribute to greenhouse gas emissions.

9. As noted by the RE-GAIN programme notes above, the RE-GAIN programme will support the innovative adaptation of the harvest and post-harvest value chain in seven African countries — Burkina Faso, Ethiopia, Kenya, Malawi, Tanzania, Uganda, and Zambia—improving food security by addressing the availability of food through the use of Food Loss Reduction Solutions (FLRS) among smallholder farmers. In addition to increasing the amount of food available for consumption, FLRS will also increase the nutritional value of grains left behind after harvests leaving the farms, thereby increasing nutritional value, marketability and income potential, while limiting post-harvest losses. The RE-GAIN programme will support the implementation of these solutions by taking them across three primary pathways — farmer-centric, supplier-centric, and institutional support which will be implemented in collaboration with key stakeholders including governments and private sector partners in different countries. These interconnected solutions will address food loss under smallholder settings of Africa.

10. The first development pathway focuses on increasing demand and absorption capacity for FLRS among smallholder farmers by addressing the supply side activities through the promotion of climate change on post-harvest food loss, as well as on the availability of FLRS options on the market and their affordability. This will be coupled with training on the use of specific FLRS that have been identified for key crops in each country as well as on the effective use of weather information and advisories, while supporting the initial sale of surplus grains to local markets and other potential buyers at a fair price.

11. The second development pathway complements the demand-side activities by enhancing the supply and affordability of FLRS. The first output of this will support business development linked to the local supply of FLRS through the establishment of smallholder farmer groups, particularly MSMEs, local cooperatives and youth groups. Support to local businesses will increase the supply of FLRS in the market at a fair price for smallholder farmers, thereby increasing the potential of improved affordability. This will be complemented by the establishment of innovative financial mechanisms or instruments that will provide access to credit and other forms of financing for smallholder farmers and key stakeholders — including suppliers, agro-dealers, financial institutions, MSMEs and local farmers – to enable them to adopt FLRS technology, using climate co-pay contributions and conditional procurement to provide added security to the transactions.

12. Combined, the increased demand and the enhanced supply and affordability of FLRS stimulate the market; enabling suppliers to develop products and services that are available at a fair price. This will help in establishing a self-sustaining market that will operate beyond the project support. The integrated nature of these Outputs and the causal pathways reinforcing the market development are depicted in Figure 3 below.


---
**Page 8**
---

These first two development pathways will be underpinned and sustained by the third, which seeks to create an enabling environment for the uptake of FL-RS, which will not only mobilise increased investment in the project but also provide a platform for scaling up the adoption of FL-RS as a sustainable land management service. This will involve policy revisions and put in place enforcement mechanisms in each of the target countries to ensure that the quality standards are met and maintained over time, thereby ensuring the uptake of FL-RS quality — as well as creating platforms for the scaling and replication of successful FL-RS business models.

Overall, the resulting uptake of FL-RS at scale will reduce food loss, improve food quality and enhance local capacity, thereby enabling the long-term outcome of improved food and nutritional security. This will have a positive impact on the livelihoods of smallholder farmers by providing them with additional employment opportunities in the market. Moreover, the project will reduce GHG emission, both directly — through the reduced emissions from the wasted food itself — and indirectly by reducing land use change for agricultural expansion driven by the need to compensate for food losses.

# B. Project/Programme Information

## B.I. Climate context

### 14. Sub-Saharan Africa stands at a crossroads with an unprecedented opportunity and challenges for agricultural transformation, driven by the demands of a rapidly growing population of 1.5 billion and the pressures of climate change.

### 15. A great deal of attention has been paid to the direct impacts of climate change on crop cultivation, with extensive research on how climate variability affects crop yields, and management strategies to address these challenges. However, climate is influential throughout changes in crop production but only during the entire crop value chain, including harvesting.

### 16. The RE-GAIN programme will support the adoption of the harvest and post-harvest management practices for maize in Ethiopia, Eritrea, Malawi, Mozambique, Nigeria, Tanzania, Uganda, and Zambia; strengthening food security by addressing the climate change impacts on post-harvest food losses.

### 17. Climate change-driven (or exacerbated) food spoilage and contamination in post-production stages of the value chains compound the climate-change-driven productivity and yield losses during cultivation. This is especially true for maize, which is highly susceptible to moisture levels (warmer air holds more moisture), increased precipitation, more frequent or intense heavy rainfall events, flooding.

### 18. Climate change is a threat multiplier to crop value chains. Climate change impacts in the post-production value chain, which result in substantial post-harvest losses and reduce the output that farmers have left to sell, depress incomes and jeopardize livelihoods of smallholder farmers (IFPRI, 2002). This programme will address these challenges by supporting the adoption of best practices for reducing post-harvest losses.

### 19. In the face of climate change-driven post-harvest losses, farmers have an impetus to keep less of the reduced output for their domestic consumption and to offset the volume losses (during harvesting, field...

|---|---|---|

**Climate Risks for Post-Harvest Value Chains**

1. Climate change-driven (or exacerbated) food spoilage and contamination in post-production stages of the value chains compound the climate-change-driven productivity and yield losses during cultivation.
2. Climate change is a threat multiplier to crop value chains.

- Alabi, A., 2019. Impact of climate change on post-harvest value chain. International Journal of Agriculture Extension and Social Development, 4(6), pp.87–94.

**Impact of climate change on food loss**

22. The impact of climate change on post-harvest food loss has not been well studied, either in terms of observed impacts (i.e., research on historic food losses in the post-harvest value chain), with some studies suggesting that climate change may be a contributing factor to increased food losses in this value chain.

23. Thus, in the table below the observed impacts of climate change in the post-harvest value chain are captured simply as the level of climate-sensitivity of the grain/diet (drawn from peer reviewed literature on climate change and food security). The sensitivity of the post-harvest value chain to climate change is population based on national (and local) stakeholder guidance (including farmers and agricultural decision-makers) plus some additional sectoral expert judgment.

**Impact of climate change on food loss**

22. The impact of climate change on post-harvest food loss has not been well studied, either in terms of observed impacts (i.e., research on historic food losses in the post-harvest value chain), with some studies suggesting that climate change may be a contributing factor to increased food losses in this value chain.

Adaptive capacity – current capacity to anticipate or respond to the impact of climate change on food loss

24. At all seven RE-GAIN countries are recognized as having low adaptive capacity and low readiness for climate change impacts, in terms of their relative global standing. Per the ND-GAIN index*, based on scores for 192 countries, the rankings' ranking on adaptive capacity* and *readiness*: out of 192 countries – is presented in Table 3. The seven RE-GAIN countries are ranked from lowest to highest: Sierra Leone, Liberia, Côte d'Ivoire, Ghana, Nigeria, Uganda, and Kenya. These countries have low adaptive capacity and low readiness for climate change impacts. This was affirmed and validated through inputs in national and sub-national level workshops, at which the key enabling factors affecting adaptive capacity against climate change impacts were identified. Stakeholder inputs across all 14 crops on the seven countries points to consistent, common factors affecting adaptive capacity in all fourteen value chains (e.g., lack of access to information, limited financial resources, poor infrastructure, and weak institutions in all seven countries' agricultural value chains) and valuable (as it offers opportunity for RE-GAIN).

25. Across the board, the major factors of vulnerability that impede adaptive capacity, highlighted by stakeholders in all seven countries, are summarised as follows:

* **Infrastructure:** Reliance on climate-vulnerable (non-climate-resilient) storage and transport infrastructure, which is prone to damage from extreme weather conditions such as droughts and moisture, as well as during extreme weather conditions such as flooding and heavy rainfall events.
* **Finance:** Lack of access or limited access to credit reduces the ability for risk-management, and limits farmers' capacity to invest in climate-resilient technologies. Limited access to information and tools that would inform about markets reduces the ability to plan for volumes of storage, transport, and sale, with the result being reduced income.
* **Technology:** Reliance on traditional methods and practices rather than greater mechanization and specialization of crops and livestock production. This limits farmers' capacity to protect their harvested crop from the impacts of high temperatures, moisture, humidity, and exposure to flooding. Limited access to climate information services (e.g., early warning systems) reduces the ability to protect harvested crops from pests (including insects).
* **The lack of limited access to climate information services (early warning systems), storage capacity, and transport reduces ability to protect harvested crop from the impacts of high temperatures, moisture, humidity, and increases exposure to flooding and heavy rains.
* **Climate change-driven (or exacerbated) food spoilage and contamination post-production stages of the value chain. This results in a double-blow to farming communities from climatic factors such as rising temperatures, higher moisture levels, and increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as droughts, heavy rainfall events, flooding. Climate change is thus a threat-multiplicator to crop value chains. The impacts from climate change are exacerbated by the fact that smallholder farmers have limited access to credit and reduce the output that farmers have left to sell, depress income prospects and jeopardize livelihoods of smallholder farmers. This perpetuates vulnerability to climatic shocks and stresses and prolongs the cycle of persistent poverty.
* **Furthermore, in the face of climate change-driven post-harvest losses, farmers have an impetus to keep harvesting, field-drying, on-farm storage) by selling more of what remains in the market. This worsens food insecurity for households that already have marked levels of undernourishment (per FAO figures). All of these reduce adaptive capacity and limit the ability to adapt and ensure resilience of smallholder farmers in the seven countries.
* **In light of these findings, it is imperative for RE-GAN to adopt adaptation strategies to climate change through: improving access to information services and enable climate resilience in crops post-harvest value chains of smallholder farmers and communities.

Adaptation Needs and Climate Change Solutions

The impact of climate change on post-harvest food value chain stage is felt largely through exposure of the grain/seed to high temperatures, high moisture levels, direct contact with water (such as during heavy rainfall events), and damage caused by extreme weather events such as flooding. Additionally, high temperature can lead to increased germination rates, insect infestation, and disease susceptibility in fungi and parasitic agents. Thus, the most effective solutions for reducing climate change-induced food losses in the post-harvest stage are those that address multiple of these factors at once and reduce the overall risk hazard. This involves interventions that improve efficiency and speed of harvest and related actions (such as harvesting equipment, storage structures, and transport) and also include measures such as FLS which offer higher protection from climatic elements (barriers between the grain/seed and the external environment) and provide early warning and/or additional information on the levels or prevalence of the climate variables (e.g., measurement tools).

The mere provision of such FLS solutions by itself is not enough if it is not accompanied by capacity building to ensure that these solutions are effectively implemented, maintained, and scaled up where/wherewithal to maintain and augment such solutions in the future. To address the pressing need for broader adoption of FLS solutions, it will be necessary to develop a comprehensive strategy wherein it is necessary to raise awareness and build capacity to promote the adoption of Food Loss Reduction Solutions (FLRS) in the light of expected climate impacts. This needs to be complemented by strengthening the enabling environment through policy reforms, providing incentives for producers and service providers, increasing options of solutions available, and enabling practical application through policy dialogue and partnerships with governments, research institutions, financial institutions, Small, Medium and Enterprises (SMME), empowering them to invest in climate-friendly FLRS and increasing access to inputs from manufacturers, and suppliers of climate-adapted FL RS, fostering a robust end-to-end market ecosystem.

For this program, a key focus of attention should be placed on the strengthening of capacities of smallholder farmers and other post-harvest actors involved in harvesting, through providing physical assets alongside technical and institutional capacity building for actors across harvest and postharvest value chain. This will enable them to adopt climate-resilient practices and technologies that are based on approaches that leverage finance and build markets for food loss reduction strategies. By enhancing access to inputs from manufacturers and suppliers of climate-adapted FLRS, this will further encourage farmers to adopt FL RS products and services, thereby boosting their climate and economic resilience.

## Complementarity with ongoing and planned initiatives

According to the World Bank estimates, a one percent reduction in postharvest losses in Sub-Saharan Africa could save up to $40 billion each year, and most of the benefits would go directly to smallholder farmers. Moreover, food loss and waste depict an extremely inefficient use of resources and occur at all stages of production, processing, distribution, and consumption. The current inefficiency of water and fertilizer goes into the production of food that never reaches human mouths. Recovering the food that is lost during harvest and postharvest handling can help close the calorie gap in Africa while strengthening livelihoods and improving food security—without requiring any additional environmental cost.

The RE-GAIN Programme operates in a complex environment of ongoing initiatives, and has been described as a "complementary initiative". While the programme objectives are focused on the production side of the agricultural value chain area, there are also initiatives in each of the target countries targeting various aspects of the postharvest sector. The RE-GAIN team has worked to develop an overall understanding of the landscape of initiatives in the target countries and has developed working relations with many of these initiatives. The RE-GAIN team has been actively involved in the planning process, considered the ongoing work in each country and seeks to complement and collaborate with these initiatives to maximise the effectiveness of the programme. A list of the key initiatives in each of the target countries is presented in Table 10-2 of Annex 5.

B.2. a. Theory of change narrative and diagram

Goal Statement

33. If the capacity of the target countries and communities to respond to climate-triggered food losses is strengthened through improved and inclusive access to financing, promotion of context-specific and gender-sensitive solutions, and increased availability of appropriate technologies and equipment for investments, then smallholder farmers will have enhanced food security and livelihood resilience.

Barriers

34. Small barriers currently might impede the uptake of food loss-reduction solutions in Africa. While the specifics of these barriers might vary between countries and farming systems, six common barriers have been identified, cutting across several themes, including limited knowledge of the options available and capacity to implement them, lack of access to appropriate technologies and equipment for investments, and access to sustainable and inclusive finance opportunities, all encompassed in the broader theme of inadequate access to information.

Barriers 1. Limited indigenous access to information and solutions for smallholder farmers

35. While most existing knowledge bases are available on aspects of post-harvest losses on their food and income security, many do not have access to the information needed to adopt improved post-harvest practices that can reduce the magnitude of post-harvest losses. These knowledge gaps exist across the full range of pre- and post-harvest processes—including harvesting, handling, storage, transporting, and packaging of agricultural commodities—and are more typically significant for women farmers than men. In addition, while many smallholder farmers have access to extension services, these farmers, the knowledge gaps are fundamental, in that they lack the basic understanding of resilient post-harvest practices. This is because most extension services do not provide adequate training and a deeper understanding of the need for improved practices, or even the availability of food loss-reduction solutions. FLR can be an effective solution to reduce post-harvest losses; however, smallholder farmers often lack the capacity to implement them. These knowledge gaps are systemic in the agriculture sector of the target countries, with limited access to information and solutions for smallholder farmers. As a result, when FLR is implemented as a result, farmers lack access to the required information to adopt FLR. Overcoming this barrier requires a comprehensive approach that includes strengthening the capacity of smallholder farmers, as well as the establishment of an enabling environment in which information on FLR is maintained into agriculture sector policies and support services.

Barrier 2: Limited awareness of climate change and climate risks

36. All actors involved across the agriculture sector across its entire value chain—from production, through harvest, storage, processing and distribution. Despite this significant impact, many smallholder farmers do not fully understand climate change and the associated climate risks and the impacts on their livelihoods. This is partly due to the lack of awareness about available resilient solutions. Given that the impacts of climate change on the agricultural sector are expected to increase in the future, it is important for smallholder farmers to be aware of these changes and how they can be more severe. Although there is growing understanding among farmers of the impacts of climate change on production, this is less true when it comes to post-harvest losses. This is partly due to the fact that awareness is less prevalent for post-harvest losses. This is partly due to the links being less obvious to the smallholder farmer. For example, a lack of awareness about climate change and its impacts on storage. This lack of awareness perpetuates the information gaps noted in Barrier 1. Specifically, when a farmer does not understand the links between climate change and food loss, efforts to seek resilient solutions are often subdued. Moreover, a lack of awareness of potential future climate trajectories


---
**Page 21**
---

exacerbates the problem, as farmers are less likely to act early if they don't understand how the problem of climate change-induced food loss will increase over time.

**Barrier 3: Limited availability of appropriate harvest and post-harvest technology**

While a wide range of FL-RS options have been developed globally, penetration of such solutions into Africa is limited by lack of access to technologies and practices, low community engagement and insufficient demand and perceptions of risk from FL-RS providers. This is exacerbated by the fact that most FL-RS providers are not aware of the specific needs and barriers awareness in Barriers 1 and 2; there is currently limited demand for FL-RS in rural communities.

The lack of demand is further exacerbated by unavailability of working capital or access to affordable collateral, with many smallholder farmers having no suitable collateral event if they were able to adopt improved FL-RS.

Second, as a result of the limited market, suppliers have not demonstrated sufficient interest to develop and provide FL-RS in Africa. Further, without the economies of scale driven by sufficient demand, FL-RS providers are not incentivized to develop localized production, distribution networks and markets that would enable them to reach smallholder farmers with affordable products. This further hinders improving efficiency or affordability of the products.

Third, the development of local markets for FL-RS is further constrained by the lack of market pull for suppliers to invest in developing these markets. Without sufficient institutional institutions, FL-RS providers need to bear risk in setting up operations within, and providing services to, smallholder farming communities. Creating a viable business model that can be sustained over time requires significant investment and expertise to be developed, many of which require the sharing of risk between different stakeholders – including providing access to affordable collateral for farmers. This is further complicated by the need to provide FL-RS to smallholder farmers to secure sufficient income from their produce. Consequently, FL-RS suppliers often perceive smallholder farmers as high risk because of their general limited capacity to sustainably produce, process and market agricultural products.

The ultimate result of these challenges is that smallholder farms do not have easy access to affordable FL-RS. To overcome this barrier, a catalyst is required to initiate demand and establish a sustainable local value chain for FL-RS in Africa that can attract FL-RS providers.

**Barrier 4: Limited access to finance for FL-RS uptake**

As noted above, the market for FL-RS for smallholder farmers in Africa is influenced by the limited access to markets for smallholder farmers, working capital for MSMEs and access to finance for the purchasing of equipment and inputs. This is further exacerbated by a number of factors including: lack of access to banks or micro-finance institutions (MFIs) – several inter-related factors impede access to finance. First, MFIs are often reluctant to lend to smallholder farmers due to their limited capacity to produce and process agriculture under changing climate conditions) and limited access to producer collateral. The feasibility of using agricultural products as collateral is further complicated by the fact that many smallholders lack equipment to be able to repay the loan at the end of the season. However, the vast array of factors that impact the ability of farmers to repay loans can also lead to a situation where they are unable to repay their loan and processing can lead to further food losses before a farmer is ever able to use the FL-RS services. This could result in a vicious cycle where smallholder farmers are unable to access finance for FL-RS, due to a single extreme climate event. Consequently, FIs are often hesitant to provide agricultural loan products that target agri-focused MSMEs. In instances where financial products have been developed, the terms of the credit can be restrictive.

Second, and exacerbating the risk perceptions mentioned above, is the lack of or limited collateral and track record among smallholder farmers and the companies serving them, including youth groups, agro processors and input suppliers. This makes it difficult for FL-RS providers to secure affordable collateral from the borrowers, generally in the form of an immovable asset, such as land or property. However, many smallholder farmers do not have access to land that can be used as collateral due to either unsuitable collaterals or have already pledged their assets, restricting access to available finance products. For example, land tenure arrangements in rural areas of Africa are generally dominated by commercial


---
**Page 22**
---

### Barrier 4: Inadequate policy and regulatory frameworks to enable FL-RS investments

#### Development of national policies, strategies, frameworks and implementation plans aiming to address the barriers described above is a key element in enabling the mainstreaming of sustainable FL-RS in the agricultural sector. Despite this progress, there are still considerable gaps in the policy and regulatory environment across the target countries which impede the mainstreaming of sustainable FL-RS in the agricultural sector.

#### Second, policies generally lack coordination and harmonisation across sectors, limiting opportunities to address the vulnerability of food systems, associated food loss, or even exacerbated by poor infrastructure and/or weak institutions. The absence of a coherent policy framework for the integration of policies across these different sectors prevents collaborative action, perpetuating the challenges.

#### Third, where policies or regulatory frameworks are in place, they are often constrained to agricultural input supply chains, which can be seen as an obstacle to the development of FL-RS. This constraint extends to the focus of information and extension services, which are generally geared towards primary production rather than post-harvest activities. The lack of a coherent policy framework for the facilitation or favour of investments in storage and processing technologies for food in the smallholder system.

#### The undeveloped private sector in these countries require policy incentive mechanisms, for example input tax exemption that would enable reduction of cost of import and distribution of FS equipment are services. This is also an important consideration when assessing the potential for investment or expansion of FL-RS should be a key consideration in these countries.

### Barrier 5: Insufficient public sector capacity to steer investments in FL-RS

#### The lack of access to finance as described above is also restricted by Central Bank regulations and policies. These can limit the ability of commercial banks to assess the creditworthiness and value of guarantees for banks in reducing their reserve requirements. Addressing these policy barriers can unlock multiple benefits, including increased access to finance for smallholder farmers. The existence of warehouse receipt legislation and authorities are also an important factor to consider when assessing the viability of storage solutions. Without cash on hand at harvest, farmers will be hesitant to store their produce.

#### Actions to overcome the full range of barriers described above require a strong public sector to create the enabling environment and steer investments into FL-RS. The public sector plays an important role in


---
**Page 23**
---

steering network, not only by creating an enabling policy environment (see Barrier 5), providing context-specific extension and advisory services to smallholder farmers but also through its role in coordination and capacity building. The proposed project will contribute to food security in seven African countries where target countries currently have limited technical and institutional capacity to create the required enabling environment. This is partly rooted in human capacity constraints, as well as limitations in the technical capacity of those staff that are available to facilitate investments, technology adoption and partnerships. This includes public sector institutions, private sector actors, and civil society organizations (CSOs) who will be supported to develop sustainable business and market opportunities.

Proposed Solution

49. The proposed project will contribute to food security in seven African countries by promoting the widespread adoption of Food Loss Reduction Solutions (FL-RS) among smallholder farmers. In addition to increasing the amount of food available for consumption due to reduced losses, this approach will also improve the resilience and adaptability of smallholder farming systems, thereby enhancing their economic and income potential, while simultaneously reducing the GHG emissions associated with food loss. To achieve the project’s goals, it is proposed that a multi-stakeholder platform be established, which includes farmer-centric, and institutional support—which together will form the foundation of a sustainable and scalable market for FL-RS in the target countries. Interconnected solutions will be addressing food loss under smallholder setting of Africa.

FL-RS demand-side development to increase adoption of FL-RS by farmers

| FL-RS supply side development to improve access, affordability of FL-RS |

50. The first development pathway focuses on raising awareness and adoption rates for FL-RS among smallholder farming communities. Activities under this pathway will target overcoming Barriers 1 and 2, and raising awareness among farmers of the impacts of climate change on post-harvest food loss, as well as the need to adopt FL-RS in order to reduce these losses and adapt their livelihoods to cope with changing climate conditions. This awareness raising will be coupled with training on the use of specific FL-RS technologies, such as improved storage methods, cold chains, and other innovations that provide farmers with information and advisories, while supporting the initial transfer of suitable technologies to the farmers to catalyze adoption. The second development pathway focuses on increasing demand for FL-RS by building the demand and build the absorption capacity needed to achieve short-term Outcome 1: FL-RS adopted by smallholder farmers in seven African countries. This will be achieved through a number of activities, including improving market linkages between farmers and agri-value chain actors such as processors and aggregators, which will help to ensure that FL-RS are not only produced but also consumed. The benefits resulting from improved harvesting methods, post-harvest handling, storage, and processing is able to make it a suitable markets to maximize income creating sustainably in the whole value chain that will enable future scaling of and maintain demand for FL-RS.

**GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDS PROPOSAL V3.01 PAGE 22**

51. The second development pathway complements the demand-side activities by enhancing the supply and affordability of FL-RS (Outcome 2). This component involves two Outputs, one focused on business development support for smallholders and MSMEs to adopt FL-RS, and another focusing on complementary financial mechanisms for smallholders and MSMEs to enable the adoption of FL-RS. Activities under Output 2.1 will support business development linked to the local supply chain, both vertically and horizontally, through a combination of activities targeting smallholders, MSMEs, local cooperatives and youth groups. The business development support will include providing access to market intelligence, technical assistance, training, and capacity building for FL-RS adoption. Financial mechanisms established under Output 2.2. Support to local businesses will increase the supply of FL-RS in a way that is affordable and accessible to smallholders and MSMEs, thereby increasing their uptake and affordability. This will be complemented by activities under Output 2.2 aimed at establishing innovative financial mechanisms for FL-RS adoption. The project will establish a value chain across the FL-RS value chain to create accessible finance opportunities based on the needs of smallholder farmers and MSMEs. The project will also aim to engage key stakeholders — including suppliers, agro-dealers, financial institutions, MSMEs and local farmers — to ensure that they are able to access the benefits of FL-RS adoption. This will be achieved by supporting procurements under the system using GCF grants which will provide added security to the transactions, further enhancing the risk-sharing nature of the approach. Finance models will be adapted to fit the local context of each country and region, taking into account the specific needs of smallholders and MSMEs, the capacity of the local farmers, and the capacity of stakeholders to absorb risk. Where available, the development of financial mechanisms will also include insurance products available from local FIs as well as supporting the inclusion of FL-RS in climate-resilient input packages.

52. Combined, the increased demand from Outcome 1 and the enhanced supply and affordability of FL-RS facilitated by the project will stimulate the market, enabling suppliers to develop products and services that are more competitive and attractive to consumers. This will lead to a virtuous cycle of increasing demand and establishing a self-sufficient market that will operate beyond the project support. The integrated nature of these Outputs and the causal pathways reinforcing the market development are depicted in Figure 3 below.

The institutionalization of financial mechanisms and institutions will be enhanced to enable investments in FL-RS — specifically tackling Barriers 5 and 6. ## Quality — as well as creating platforms for the scaling and replication of successful FL-RS business models.

### Overall, the resulting uptake of FL-RS at scale will reduce food loss, improve food quality and enhance food safety. These benefits enable the long-term outcomes of improved food and nutritional security. This will have the co-benefit of increasing food safety, while the increased investment in FL-RS resulting from CO2 mitigation and GHG emission reductions will also lead to further reductions in direct and indirect GHG emissions, both directly — through the reduced emissions from the wasted food itself — and indirectly by reducing land use change for agricultural expansion driven by the need to compensate for food loss.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND-FUNDING PROGRAM V3.0 PAGE 24

## Theory of Change

### Goal/Intervention

- **Long-term Outcome:** Long-term outcomes are achieved through a combination of short-term and long-term interventions.
- **Co-benefits:** Co-benefits include increased resilience, improved livelihoods, and enhanced ecosystem services.

### Outcomes & Co-Benefits

#### Output 1: Smallholder Farmers
- Activity Set 1: Provide technical assistance to smallholder farmers in implementing climate-smart agriculture practices.
- Activity Set 2: Support the development of market access for smallholder farmers' products through value chain analysis and marketing strategies.

#### Output 2: Improved Livelihoods
- Activity Set 1: Provide training on climate-resilient agricultural practices to smallholder farmers.
- Activity Set 2: Facilitate access to financial services for smallholder farmers, including microfinance and insurance schemes.

### Barriers & Solutions

- **Barriers:** Lack of information dissemination, limited access to technology and inputs, and market access constraints.
- **Solutions:** Provide training on climate-resilient agricultural practices, facilitate access to financial services, and support the development of market access for smallholder farmers' products through value chain analysis and marketing strategies.

### Assumptions

1. The program's target group is mainly smallholder farmers globally who are in the transition phase.
2. Smallholder farmers have a willingness to adopt climate-smart agricultural practices.
3. There will be sufficient funding available to implement the program effectively.
4. Partnerships with local organizations and governments will facilitate the implementation of the program.

# B.3. Programme description

The programme description below outlines the overarching design of the programme’s approach as it applies to the seven target countries. The activities are presented thematically as they apply across the focal countries as a whole, with specific details on the nature of interventions for each individual country provided in the relevant Specific Appendices to Annex 2.

## Key crops and Site Selection

58. Key crops were identified by expert review of the National Agricultural Research System (NARS) and the National Designated Authority (NDA) of each target country, based on their national processes. Two crops per target country under AGRA’s portfolio were selected to ensure that a wide range of crops was available for the crafting and execution of appropriate interventions (see Table 5). Selected crops are representative of the specific agro-ecological diversity and aligned with the specific needs and strategic agricultural priorities in each country. In addition, these crops hold substantial importance to the livelihoods of smallholder farmers, particularly high rates of loss within the value chain. These crops are also grown by large parts of the population and a significant number of smallholder farmers engaged in the value chains.

59. The selection was informed by the agronomic and economic realities of each country and provide opportunities for targeted enhancement of food security and sustainable agricultural practices and improvement of livelihoods, while simultaneously contributing to national efforts to reduce contributions of these value chains to country’s GHG emissions, contributing to the NGDs of the target countries.

60. Within each country, a set of target regions have been identified as outlined in Table 5. Within these sites, the specific beneficiaries will be selected following a set of eligibility criteria, as presented in Section 5.2. The selection process will also take into account the presence of indigenous communities13, in locations where indigenous communities are present and where they face unique circumstances and challenges faced by Indigenous communities in these areas with continuous engagement to proactively include their participation in the RE-GAN programme.

## The first project component focuses on enhancing demand for, and adoption of FL-RS in the target provinces.

### Output 1: Smallholder farmers supported to adopt FL-RS

#### Activity 1.1: Conduct an awareness-raising activities targeting smallholder farming communities focused on post-harvest food loss management at both the value chain and policy level.

65. The limited awareness on post-harvest food loss management at both the value chain and policy level, coupled with a lack of knowledge about FL-RS, has been identified as one of the major barriers to the limited adoption of available FL-RS as well as inadequate integration of food loss reduction strategies into relevant policies and strategies by governments in Africa. This has, in turn, resulted in

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 27

#### 66. Establish a foundation for increasing demand for FL-RS, awareness and capacity building focused actions and activities will be undertaken in each of the target communities across the seven countries under this proposal. The focus is on reducing food loss and postharvest losses (FL-PHL) and associated food losses among farmers, local stakeholders in the agriculture value chain, and the public.

The awareness raising targets several key topics, as outlined below:

- Major drivers and causes of harvest and postharvest loss in the target locations, with emphasis on climate change adaptation
- Impact of postharvest losses on food security, income, economy, and the environment (incl. climate change) and the importance to reduce FL-PHL
- Available technologies and methods for reducing FL-PHL and the importance of these technologies and methods for harvesting, storage, etc.) to reduce harvest and postharvest losses and their potential impact on food security
- Role of different actors (local government, extension services, farmer organizations, agro-dealers, financial institutions, etc.) in addressing FL-PHL and their roles in reducing FL-PHL
- Cross-cutting themes: climate change awareness, climate smart agriculture, farm management, nutrition, gender equality, and food security

#### 67. The nature of awareness raising activities will be tailored to agricultural context of each country, accounting for the target crops, the availability of local information dissemination systems (including mass media such as radio, television, newspapers, etc.), the availability of extension services, farmer organizations, agro-dealers and other service providers, cultural considerations and local languages. The campaign will collaborate with local government, extension services, farmer organizations, build on existing outreach systems to maximize reach and participation. AGF will coordinate the awareness campaigns, overseeing planning and advising outreach strategies as needed.

**Activity 1.1.2:** Train smallholder farmers on improved crop management and resilient FL-RS.

#### 68. The training will be designed to address the capacities of smallholder farmers or improved crop management and storage techniques to reduce food losses and to maintain quality of produce, increase farmers' income by reducing losses and improving marketability, and improve supply of financial services and FL-RS to smallholder farmers. Training will be provided through a variety of channels including farmer field schools (FFS), example, NGOs, VEAIs) to facilitate training in target communities. Training will then be provided through various platforms such as radio, television, newspapers, etc., extension services, farmer organizations and Village-Based Advisors (VBAs), farmer cooperatives, industry associations, and digital platforms. The training will be conducted by trained facilitators and trainers.

Approximately 5,300 VBA will be engaged for training, each targeting 200 to 250 farmers, resulting in a total of approximately 1,060,000 farmers trained.

#### 69. The nature and content of the training will be tailored to each group and demand as outlined below. Training will include baseline topics, including an introduction to the FE-GRAIN programme and the core concepts of FL-RS, followed by a series of modules on specific topics such as crop management and leveraging material from the awareness campaign (Activity 1.1.1). The sequencing of the training will also, where feasible, follow Activity 1.5.3 to enable integration of the equipment demonstrations (Activity 1.1.3) into the training sessions.

The training will be conducted in a series of modules, lectures and practicals, with content not tailored to the local context of each community. A list of potential training topics has been identified, which will be finalized at the beginning of the first year of implementation once the communities are identified.


- Major triggers and incidences of food loss and the impact they have on food security and health
- Identification of postharvest losses during harvesting and transport
- Methods to reduce contamination of produce by mycotoxins and other diseases at harvest and storage
- Use of available weather forecast data.

### Activity 1.1.3: Train agricultural traders and processors on complementary services for FL-RS
71. Building on the training of farmers, the programme will provide additional training to agricultural traders and processors in order to ensure that they have a good understanding of FL-RSs and how to maximise the effectiveness of FL-RSs. This training will include the same basic concepts, including an introduction to the FL-RSs, their benefits, and the importance of post-harvest losses (PHL) on the impact of climate change. Beyond that, the training will include best bet approaches to introduce FL-RS to smallholder farmers and the types of initial support required to enhance demand, tailored to the processing and storage methods used by traders and processors.

- improved quality management and adherence to quality standards;

- the responsible use of storage protectants;
- farmer aggregation methods and systems and associated risks of food loss;
- improving postharvest losses through whole grain processing; and value addition;
- supplier management;
- effective record keeping;
- access to finance and credit.

## Activity 1.1.5: Conduct scouting missions to identify and engage manufacturers of FL-RS equipment

Under this activity, AGRA will develop an inventory and characterization of FL-RS equipment manufacturers and agro-dealers that will be engaged in developing/strengthening local markets and enhancing the capacity of farmers to adopt FL-RS (see Table 1.1.5-1 for a list of manufacturers under Component 2). By mapping the market and building networks of local manufacturers and regional distributors, AGRA will identify those with the most potential to scale up their production/scalability potential. To reach the end of RS to remote and rural areas, the programme will also engage agro-dealers in these areas. This will ensure that there is a network of local agro-dealers in those target areas, thereby ensuring availability of solutions, and their accessibility by farmers. The mapping exercise under this activity will be conducted through the development or strengthening of platforms that will bring together manufacturers and other stakeholders to facilitate exchange of goods and services.

### Activity 1.1.6: Conduct demonstrations of locally-appropriate FL-RS

The programme will facilitate active demonstrations of short-listed FL-RS to target communities. This activity will be sequenced after and will build on the relationships developed under Activity 1.1.5, partnering with manufacturers and agro-dealers to demonstrate their products and services in the field. The solutions through the programme supported out as an incentive for vendors to engage in demonstrations of their products and services. Demonstrations will be conducted by local farmers or RS demonstration that will be established either at the agro-dealers' property or on-farm in partnership with the agro-dealer. These demonstrations will be designed to showcase the benefits of FL-RS to farmers to the suppliers of the products. Where possible, demonstrations will be done in conjunction with the training under Activity 1.1.2 to improve efficiencies and ensure full connectivity between the theoretical knowledge gained from the training and practical application on-farm. The demonstrations will also include loss-change climate nexus will be designed taking into account local context. They will take the form of a combination of field visits, farm tours, workshops, and other forms of engagement such as innovation hubs, and by agro-dealers, targeting farmer and youth groups, aggregators and processors. The programme will ensure that at least 20 demonstrations are conducted in each target district per year. At least two demonstrations will be conducted for each modality of demonstrations and trainings. At least 2 demonstrations will be done in each target district per pilot project.

The activity will engage broader initiatives that aim to increase the consumption of whole grains by developing markets such as schools so that not only a put mechanism for wheatgrass but also a demand creation mechanism is created for future markets. ### Output 1.2: Improved market linkages between agri-value chains

The proliferation of unstructured market systems is one of the major causes of post-harvest losses, with poor handling practices and lack of infrastructure leading to substantial risk for produce during the post-harvest phase of production. The project will therefore aim to facilitate the development of more structured relationships between producers, value-adding agents and off-takers. By structuring the linkages on the premise of increased food quality linked to improved post-harvest handling, this will reduce the risk of spoilage and loss during storage and transport.

Moreover, the improved market linkages will help de-risk potential external investments in IV, RLS, enhanced infrastructure and other value chain development activities.

### Activity 1.2.1: Provide technical assistance to support the structuring of value chains and coordination between market actors

The technical assistance under this activity will facilitate market bridges between smallholders, MSMEs and larger actors such as retailers, processors and exporters – using the consortia model as tested and proven by AGRA. Other opportunities such as institutional markets will also be explored on this project.

### Activity 1.2.2: Mapping of existing value chains

The process will start with an assessment of the existing market in each target community, drawing on and expanding the initial market assessment conducted during preparation of the RE-GAIN programme. The assessment will include a mapping of the existing value chain actors and their roles within the local level, including potential institutional markets (e.g., schools, hospitals, prisons) that are not yet fully exploited by smallholder farmers.

The existing value chains will be mapped, drawing connections between various actors to identify opportunities for filling gaps. Based on the stakeholder mapping, the programme will provide technical assistance to locate and promote opportunities for market linkages with other actors in the chain. The project will specifically consider existing constraints faced by women in the sector and identify and promote opportunities that can help them.

### Activity 1.2.3: Clear action plans for structuring value chains

This activity will culminate in clear action plans for structuring value chains for target crops in each community, including an outline of business development opportunities (see Output 1.2.1) to fill gaps in the existing market and identify new actors that can be engaged through improved coordination mechanisms and supporting the establishment and/or strengthening of cooperatives where needed to facilitate improved coordination between actors.

### Activity 1.2.1: Provide business development support and market intelligence

#### 87. Once service providers are identified and enrolled on the programme, a rigorous assessment of their capacity and skills will be conducted, before organizing intensive "boot camps" to train these entities on the following areas: (i) the design and implementation of financial services for the full life cycle of the FLS service provision; including: i) service management and process optimization; ii) the safe, effective, efficient, and sustainable delivery of FLS services; iii) the development of quality standards for post-harvest services; and iv) the management of the fee-for-service model. #### Output 2.2: Financial mechanisms established to support smallholders and MSMEs adopt FLSRS

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.01 PAGE 23

92. One of the key transformative approaches in the RE-GAIN programme is the introduction of innovative financial mechanisms that will strategically use GCF grants as catalysts to develop the market for FL-RS.

The approach taken in financial model design is focused on mitigating the current market dynamics and challenges, including low demand, lack of supply chain infrastructure, and limited access to finance. The programme will also introduce additional activities in the programme to respond to some of the key challenges identified and the gaps in the market that need to be addressed. These interventions are designed to address these challenges and support the growth and development of the market, as a whole. In addition to the core financial mechanisms, the programme will support several other pathways to financing FL-RS including the use of a FL-RS bond, the establishment of a FL-RS credit facility, and structuring prefinancing partnership arrangements that include FFLRS.

Activity 2.2.1 Facilitate the development and deployment of catalytic procurement models for FL-RS logistics services in collaboration with key stakeholders and end-users.

93. Under this Activity, RE-GAIN will implement two financial models split into two distinct categories: interventions at a smallholder farmer level; and interventions at a youth group/co-operative level. An overview of each model is provided below. Further details on the specific implementation modalities, including the roles of actors and eligibility criteria - provided in Annex 2: Feasibility Study. In all instances, the models draw on the same core financial mechanism as described above (i.e., a pre-financing facility) but will be designed to tailor the models to the local context. The models leverage partnerships between these actors to share risk and responsibility for the development of robust markets.

94. Interventions at a smallholder farmer level

Model 1 provides the local supply of FL-RS interventions by using conditional preconditions to effectively subsidise smallholder farmer-level (referred to hereinafter as 'smart subsidies'). As its base case, the model will provide a subsidy for the purchase of two tons of FL-RS per year at a fixed subsidy rate by using GCF funds to procure one ton for every two tons1 procured and sold by an agro-dealer. The subsidy is passed down to the smallholder farmers as a discount on the purchase price.

The model will be implemented in three phases:

- In phase 1, the programme will focus on smallholder farmers who have access to the end-users – i.e., for FL-RS such as tarps and plastic sheets, metal and plastic slats, and hermetic bags. The subsidy will be provided through a two-tiered approach: a local agro-dealer will receive the subsidy released once proof of eligibility by eligible smallholder farmers has been obtained. The instruction for release of funds will be made by AGRA once the implementation unit is satisfied that the conditions have been met and the subsidy can be released.

- In phase 2, the programme will focus on smallholder farmers who do not yet have access to end-users but are willing to pass through an FI. It should be noted that no GCF funds will be transferred to a financial institution as the end-user is not yet in place.
- To stimulate production and manufacturer capacity by placing pre-order of FL-RS, however, maintaining control over risk through a conditional release of funds to the manufacturer; and
- To mobilize demand for FL-RS. The subsidy provided at this stage will drive additional demand and facilitate knowledge sharing about the benefits of interventions.

### 104. Should the suppliers, agro-dealers or farmers require additional finance for their role in the system, they will be able to approach local financial institutions that already exist all in the target countries. For example if a farmer is unable to meet the demand of the market (i.e., "supply-side demand") and/or the "market demand", they can enter into a loan agreement with an IFI to cover the additional liquidity needs for providing the supply. The same applies to agro-dealers who may need financing in order to provide the supply themselves will be outside of the scope of the RE-GAIN Programme and will not include AGA. The order placed by the supplier or farmer will be paid out of their own funds, but no RE-GAIN Programme funds will be used to lend to the supplier or to make payments to IFIs.

### 105. This model creates a win-win scenario for all actors, with the manufacturer ultimately receiving the full value of the FL-RS provided, the agro-dealer making income from their markup, and the farmers receiving a higher price for their produce. The RE-GAIN Programme will provide financial support to the farmer with reduced risk, eventually bringing down the cost of FL-RS in the local market, enabling the smart utilization of the resource.

### Interactions at a cooperative or SME level

#### 107. Model 2 will use GCF grants to provide a unique approach to unlocking higher value items and food loss reducing equipment to farmer collectives or small to medium sized enterprises. The term "youth groups" is used here as an example of the type of group that could be supported by this model, but it should be noted that the model will also apply to other cooperatives, including women's groups and local MSMEs. The aim of Model 2 is for the main contract/procurement agreement with suppliers to youth groups and informal networks within the scope.

#### Enhancing creditworthiness - through repurchase assurances from the suppliers that lower the loss given default

#### Reducing borrowing costs - Through a combination of the lowered credit risk (as per above) and subsidies provided by GCF, the cost of financing for MSMEs will be reduced. This makes loans more affordable and thus accessible to youth groups who provide services to smallholder farmers.

### At the heart of Model 2 is the engagement of local youth groups that will be supported to act as service providers to smallholder farmers in their communities. The youth groups will be provided with a range of equipment including mechanical crop-treath harvesters (preferably solar-powered), moisture meters and communal storage structures. The establishment of these service operations will be supported through the business development services provided by GCF, which will include training on how to manage the equipment and provide a reliable service** However it is recognised that despite the proposed capacity development activities some youth groups may not have sufficient experience in running a business and therefore will need to work with formalised MSMEs to partner with local youth groups; with the established MSMEs taking the lead role in the management of the service operations. The youth group will be responsible for the management of the arrangement. The MSMEs will recruit and work with informal youth groups from the outset, providing them with training on how to manage the equipment and provide a reliable service. The MSME-supervised approach: the informal youth groups will have the opportunity to build a track record, demonstrate their ability to deliver services and gain experience for future opportunities. This approach leverages several key concepts to unlock the targeted benefits:

- **Collectives** provide benefits to smallholder farmers in economies of scale through cost sharing and increased bargaining power with off-takers. These should promote further profitability and therefore additional demand for FL-RS.
- **Post-harvest handling will increase the quality and quantity of agricultural produce, allowing smallholder farmers to capture more value, hence increased incomes.

### For example, to finance working capital, additional processing time or inputs that are required to satisfy the additional demand for FL-RS. Financial institutions will be encouraged to provide loans to MSMEs in order to unlock the growth and innovation that can bring to the process.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.03 PAGE 27

## Step 13: On receipt of the 70% payment from the FI, the supplier delivers the equipment to the youth group and reports on delivery to AGRA's RE-GAIN PII.

### Step 14: On receipt of the delivery report from the supplier, AGRA will release the 30% co-payment from the FLO towards the initial purchase agreement. At this stage, the youth group will have control over the use of the equipment, but ownership of the asset remains with the supplier and the loan is still outstanding.

### Step 5: The youth group, now in possession of the equipment, provides an FL-RS service to local farmers. Support under Output 2.1 will ensure that the youth groups are adequately capacitated to operate the equipment effectively.

### Step 6: The producer farmers pay the youth group for the FL-RS service, with the youth group collecting income from multiple farmers; thereby distributing the cost of the equipment across multiple beneficiaries.

### Step 7: The youth groups use the income from the services to make repayments to the FLO on the loan. On average, this will result in a lower repayment burden through RE-GAIN reduction, as compared with a scenario where a 10% loan would have been required, thereby decreasing the loan loss given default.

### Step 8: At the end of the agreed loan period, the FI concludes the transaction and reports on the outcome to AGRA's RE-GAIN PII.

## Step 9: The conclusion of the transaction will lead to one of two possible outcomes, as outlined below:

- **Outcome 1:** Market development was successful, indicated by the youth group operating an FL-RS service, enabling the full repayment of the loan. Under this scenario, the ownership of the asset will be transferred from the supplier to the youth group at no cost and the transaction will be considered a success under the initial agreement, without the costs of servicing the loan.

- **Outcome 2:** Market development was unsuccessful, indicated by the failure of the youth group to make repayments on time. In this scenario, the supplier's repurchase assurance is triggered, through which the supplier buys back the asset (account for depreciation). The value of the equipment will be reduced by the amount of outstanding loan plus any remaining interest and transaction fees. Should the repurchase value exceed the outstanding loan amount, with any remaining balance, it will be transferred back to the youth group to compensate for any payments made before default.

## 120. Alternative variations to these models may be introduced depending on the local context and the nature of the FL-RS service provided by the youth group. In all cases, GCF grants will be used to make a co-payment on the equipment on behalf of the beneficiary youth group or MSMEs; therefore, there is no additional cost to the transaction and de-risking the transaction for the supplier or its involved in the agreement.

### Activity 2.2.3: Structure prefinancing partnership arrangements that include FL-RS

122. To further unlock financing for accessing FL-RS, the RE-GAIN programme will provide technical assistance through its FUI, CGI and ADRA staff to key value chain players — for example of takers, input suppliers, traders, processors, etc. — in order to help them access financial services from banks or other institutions that will denier the provision of finance. Assistance will involve convening key actors (through workshops/meetings) on how to access these financial services and how to structure prefinancing arrangements for FL-RS financial products based on risk sharing and risk reducing arrangements Some of these products are under development in Kenya, Tanzania and Malawi and have been successfully implemented in several of its focus countries including Burkina Faso, Tanzania and Malawi.

### Component 3: Enabling environment to ensure sustainability of the FL-RS market

#### Outcome 3c: Strengthened enabling environment for the uptake of FL-RS

123. The impacts of climate change on food loss in Africa is aggravated by the lack of an inclusive policy environment that supports the development and adoption of FL-RS technologies. Key policy gaps include the limited: i) availability of pan-African platforms on climate change that could facilitate action to minimize post-harvest losses and that are supported by evidence and responding to African needs and priorities; ii) capacity building for institutions, including financial institutions, to provide credit and support SME investments; iii) weak regulatory and enforcement mechanism that would have reduced food loss in Africa. The RE-GAIN programme will address these gaps through: i) strengthening the entrepreneurial skills of youth in minimizing food losses In response to the challenges, Component 3 of the RE-GAIN programme will focus on three key areas: a) building capacity for institutions to provide RS at scale, working within the existing policy environment where needed and supporting change where possible; b) enhancing capacity of national institutions to enable investments in FL-RS.

#### Outcome 3.1: Enhanced capacity of national institutions to enable investments in FL-RS

124. To increase the sustainability and scalability of the positive impact created by the improved demand and supply of FL-RS, and improved access to financing and established market images RE-GAIN programme will work with key stakeholders including governments, financial institutions, input suppliers, etc., including tax exemptions/incentives certification and standards for FL-RS quality and promote successful FL-RS business models for scaling-up and replication.

#### Activity 2.3.1: Strengthening the enabling environment for FL-RS organizations

125. All actors involved in the FL-RS value chain, both central and local will be crucial The programme will align with other projects and programmes to leverage synergies utilize existing laws and policies on FL reduction, SME promotion and smallholder support ensure effective and efficient programmatic delivery and maximize impact.

#### Outcome 3.2: Effective stakeholder engagement is essential and will involve raising awareness, providing programme information and ensuring inclusivity for women youth minority groups and all value chain actors A variety of tools and approaches will be used to engage stakeholders in the design implementation and mobilization through stakeholder engagement meetings Additionally, ensuring the availability of quality FL-RS data and access to finance is vital in supporting long-term communication.

## Activity 3.1.1: Support the revision of policies in support of enabling FLRS investments, including tax exemptions and/or reduced import duties, certification and standards for FLRS quality

### B

**127. Under this Activity, AGRA will collaborate with government entities to identify critical policy gaps related to food loss, and revise and update key policies which are not responding to the current and future food loss challenges in FLRS. This will include working closely with relevant ministries of agriculture and other agencies to review existing regulations and policies and proposing amendments to facilitate the development of a conducive environment for investments in post-harvest infrastructure and services. AGRA will also introduce/strengthen national policies that provide financial incentives such as tax exemptions, subsidies, or reduced import duties, and/or certification and standards for FLRS quality, including private sector investments**. Evidence generated and lessons learned in FLRS from target countries and counties will be used to develop capacity, influence policy and promote best practices at scale.

### 128. AGRA will also actively participate in policy advisory initiatives, working with stakeholders to ensure that policies are aligned with the national development plans and strategies of the respective countries. In addition to support for policy revision, AGRA will support local entities in the implementation of these policies by providing technical assistance and capacity building where they are currently not fully enforced or supported. These actions will result in a supportive policy environment that enables the successful implementation of the RE-GEN programme and widespread adoption of FLRS solutions.

### Activity 3.1.2: Support institutional capacity building of key local entities to support the scaling up of FLRS

**129. Through this activity, AGRA will provide technical assistance to local and national government entities engaged in crop value chains (directly or indirectly) to build their institutional capacity to support the scaling up of FLRS solutions. This will be achieved through the establishment of formal partnerships with local government through the establishment of formal partnerships and facilitating regular meetings and consultations on issues related to the development, implementation and monitoring of national priorities. These partnerships will be complemented by technical assistance, including guidelines, targeting tools and technology delivery mechanisms, to develop the analytical capacity of partner institutions on the topic of food loss in relation to climate conditions and the impact of FLRS in reducing losses, as well as the metrics to be tracked and reported on in relation to the NDG — as well as for the scenario-based projections of future impacts.**

### 130. RE-GEN will also work with governments and other development partners to promote private sector investment in post-harvest infrastructure, including storage facilities, processing units and cold chains, as well as in the development of value-added products. This will be achieved through the identification of processes for FLRS products and services, ensuring they meet required levels of reliability and efficiency. Support will also be provided to governments and other development partners to promote private sector services to monitor food loss in relation to climate conditions and the impact of FLRS in reducing losses, as well as building and strengthening of operational and financial management systems.

### 131. Finally, AGRA will support other interventions that can synergize with others playing in building institutional capacity of governments and the private sector in FLRS, which may include enhancing outreach efforts to smallholder farmers dealing with climate-related loss under changing climates.

#### Activity 3.1.3: Develop a business case for investments in FLRS, including value chain services provision and financing to promote successful FLRS business models for scaling-up and replication

**A key step in unlocking investments and creating sustainable markets for FLRS in Africa revolves around the development of a business case that will provide a clear rationale for investment. This will involve financiers, agri-dealers, service providers and farmers. AGRA will, therefore, research and compile case studies of successful FLRS initiatives implemented through the programme, as well as other best practices from elsewhere in the region and beyond. The findings from this research and analysis will form the foundation of the business case for FLRS, detailing best practice and benefits of**

**Note: RE-GEN will advocate at Government level to create a more enabling environment for FLRS but will not directly cover the financing on tax incentives or subsidies.**
