**Activity 3.1.4: Implement a comprehensive monitoring, evaluation and learning system for FL-RS**

133. The programme will develop a robust monitoring, evaluation and learning (MEL) framework that includes clear performance indicators, data collection methods, and regular reporting schedules. This system will be used to track progress against the project’s objectives, assess the effectiveness of different components of programme activities, assess the impact of interventions, and identify areas for improvement. The MEL framework will also include a process for collecting feedback from stakeholders on their experience with the programme, conducting periodic evaluations, and gathering feedback from stakeholders. These insights will be used to make informed adjustments to the programme, ensuring that it remains relevant and effective.

134. Through the implementation of the RE-GAN initiative, farmers will possess knowledge sharing, improve their livelihoods through continuous learning, and collaborate more among themselves. This will be supported through the activities under Components 1 and 2 to encourage the formation of farmer groups, share best practices and collectively address challenges related to post-harvest management. The knowledge gained from this initiative will be shared with other stakeholders in the region, including the successful models across Africa and beyond. In the spirit of south-south cooperation, the programme will also seek to disseminate lessons learned through the RE-GAN initiative to other countries.

This will include developing joint action plans, sharing resources, and coordinating activities to maximize impact and avoid duplication of efforts.


**B.4 | Integration arrangements**

**PSAA Applicant**

135. AGRA – an African-led institution with a vision of contributing to a food system-inspired inclusive agricultural transformation across Africa, of reducing hunger, improving nutrition, and adapting to climate change. The programme will be implemented in seven focus countries (Ethiopia, Kenya, Malawi, Mozambique, Nigeria, Tanzania, Uganda) where there is potential for significant impact on smallholder farmers’ livelihoods, increased incomes, better livelihoods, and improved food security will serve as the PSAA Applicant for this project.

136. AGRA was founded in 2006, bringing 18 years of experience in a varied sector related to food security, including seeds systems, sustainable farming, policy and state capacity and inclusive markets and trade. (These areas are critical components of the programme’s strategy and will be addressed through several key cross-cutting themes particularly climate change, nutrition and gender responsiveness.)

137. The organisation operates in 12 countries, employing over 300 staff, and managing an average annual budget of $50 million. AGRA is a member of the Alliance for a Green Revolution in Africa (AGRA), which is a partnership between CGIAR and five other donor bodies (BMGF, Rockefeller Foundation, DFID, USAID, and SIDA). The organisation has been working with public and private partners to support 12 countries in Africa to create and sustain Inclusive Agri-Food Systems (IAFS) that are inclusive of smallholder farmers.

138. As a continental institution in agriculture and food systems, AGRA is well placed to undertake the planned activities through its widespread infrastructure and partnerships on the continent, having strong AGRA country offices in all seven focus countries. The programme will be implemented under the African Smallholder Food Systems (ASFS) and Agribusiness Deal Room, where AGRA brings on board connections with governments, private sector actors, and other stakeholders to facilitate key actors, knowledge and resources, which are fundamental to take food loss innovation to scale.

139. AGRA is known as a trusted broker by the governments it works with and which will enable it to ensure the programme’s success in the seven focus countries. The programme will be implemented through the ASFS and Agribusiness Deal Room, where AGRA brings on board connections with governments, private sector actors, and other stakeholders to facilitate key actors, knowledge and resources, which are fundamental to take food loss innovation to scale.

AGRA's ongoing projects across the continent as leverage, AGRA will drive a paradigm shift for building the resilience of smallholder farmers and agricultural communities in the seven focus countries and across Africa. The programme will be implemented through the African Smallholder Food Systems (ASFS) and Agribusiness Deal Room, where AGRA brings on board connections with governments, private sector actors, and other stakeholders to facilitate key actors, knowledge and resources, which are fundamental to take food loss innovation to scale.

## Executing Entity (EE)

The project will be executed directly by AGRA through Programme Implementation Unit (PIU). Through the PIU, key resources including finance, Grant Management and Procurement Officers who will provide financial and administrative management, overseeing financial, contractual compliance, procurement and grant administration. The PIU will also manage human resource planning and quality assurance; supervise programme monitoring, evaluation and reporting; ensure timely communication of project progress to all partners; facilitate partnerships with other development partners; and ensure smooth communication flow across all programme partners. This executing role will be located at AGRA’s Nairobi-based headquarters, and AGRA’s country offices, and will report to the AGRA senior leadership.

## The EE is responsible for:

* Execution of the project.
* Procurement activities specifically (major procurement and contracting)
* Facilitating partnerships,
* Managing and monitoring results
* Annual reporting by country offices to the PIU

AGRA deploys a diverse set of delivery models to deliver its country and institutional strategy. It offers services through its expert staff at the headquarters in Nairobi, at the East, Southern and West Africa regional offices as well as at country offices. AGRA staff work with downstream partners and local organizations to implement specific components of a contracted programme area with the aim to improve agricultural productivity and food security. The delivery models are designed to reflect the nature of its interventions. AGRA provides Technical Assistance (TA) in the form of short- to medium-term expertise, capacity building and training for government officials, extension workers, farmers and other stakeholders. TA is provided through a range of mechanisms including individual assignments that require skilled expertise. AGRA is a convener (brings stakeholders together around a shared goal), an enabler (provides resources and support) and a facilitator (bridges the gap between different actors and stakeholders within the agriculture and food systems sector). AGRA utilizes advocacy and partnerships as well as key tools for change. The specific delivery models will be determined at the implementation stage and will depend on each country context.

## Responsible Units

The EE team at the Nairobi HQ will be supported by AGRA country offices in each of the seven target countries who will serve as responsible units. These units will support on-the-ground coordination and implementation, with roles being mandated for specific outputs/activities.

## AGR will establish a Programme Advisory Group (PAG) made up of senior representatives from AGR's Integrated Natural Resource Management (INRM) unit that will serve as the starting point to guide innovative, impact scale and adaptive thought leadership to shape the partnership at the continental level. AGR environmental advisory body will meet on quarterly as part of the PIM meetings.

### Programme Implementation

#### 16. A central Programme Implementation Unit (PIU) will be established at AGR's Nairobi headquarters to oversee implementation of the entire programme across all seven countries. This unit will report to the PAG and consist of three main groups: a Programme Management Unit (PMU); and a Technical Expert Group (TEG), as described below.

##### *Programme Management Unit*

The PIU will establish a management unit that will be functional for the entire duration and be responsible for day-to-day implementation of the project. The PMU will oversee overall management, implementation and general technical direction of the entire programme, ensuring an integrated vision among all TEGs. The PMU will consist of: i) Programme Manager; ii) Project Finance Officer; iii) Procurement Officer; iv) Project Analyst; v) M&E Officer. The PMU will be based at AGR's Nairobi headquarters with in-country support from responsible units in the country offices.

##### *Technical Expert Group (TEG)*

The TEG, also situated within the Nairobi Headquarters, will provide expertise to assist the PMU in the technical implementation of the RE-GAIN programme. The TEG will include several full-time positions and part-time positions as follows: i) Technical Advisor – Gender Equality, Women's Empowerment, Inclusive Finance and GIS; ii) Technical Advisor – Extension and Value Chain Development. These sub-sectors are grouped under the following thematic areas: a) Sustainable Livelihoods and Food Security; b) Inclusive Markets and Finance; c) Land – Sustainable Farming, Distribution and Youth in Extension; d) Technical Advisor – Livelihood Resilience and Climate Adaptation; e) Head M&E; and f) Technical Advisor – Waste Reduction Analysis.

##### *Country-level Implementation Unit*

The PIU will be assisted in project implementation within each target country by a country-level implementation unit (CIU) which will be established in each of the AGR country offices* and will be comprised of core staff from the relevant departments. The CIU will consist of a Country Director, who reports directly to the PIU, as well as providing regular reports to the relevant Project Steering Committee (PSC). Specific responsibilities of the CIU for each project output are outlined in the first row of Table 6 below.

### Programme Steering Committee

At the country level, the programme will be implemented under the overall guidance of a Programme Steering Committee (PSC) co-chaired by a representative of the NDA and AGR country managers*. The PSC will include representatives of other key government departments and agencies, the project team leader, and representatives from the relevant Departments of Agriculture. The PSC will meet with the Departments for Land Resources Conservation, Crop Development, Agriculture Extension Services and Agroforestry to discuss progress on the project in each country; review progress against targets set out in the project in-country; address project issues as raised by the advisory group; iii) review the project programme work plan and budget (AWP); iv) assess whether the project is being implemented satisfactorily and within the approved project framework; v) review and approve annual work plan and budget (AWPB) and provide necessary strategic guidance for its implementation; vi) approve the annual budget (ABD) and provide necessary strategic guidance for its implementation; vii) provide advice for subsequent work plans to build on achievements and address any shortcomings; and viii) provide ad

## Implementation Arrangements

A focus of each of the implementation arrangements is on flow of funds, reporting lines, and reporting lines as they apply to each individual country. A focused overview of the implementation arrangements at the regional level is presented in Figure 9.
156. Africa's food insecurity challenge has been exacerbated by climate change. Sub-Saharan Africa stands at a crossroads between the need for economic transformation and the imperative for agricultural transformation, driven by the demands of a rapidly growing population of 1.2 billion and the pressures of a changing climate. The continent faces significant development challenges including food insecurity, resource degradation, poverty, gender inequality, and social exclusion.


---
**Page 49**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.03 | PAGE 47

**B**

evident in low crop productivity, deforestation, land degradation, conflict, migration, and vulnerability to climate change which perpetuate persistent food insecurity and poverty. These challenges are being exacerbated by climate change, which is impacting the full spectrum of food production system. Most vulnerable are smallholder farmers who are already struggling with limited access to resources resulting from increases in pests and diseases, unpredictable rainfall pattern, increased humidity, and changing temperature regimes. The impacts of climate change on agriculture are expected to be severe across Africa, where the capacity to adapt and respond to a changing climate is weak. Climate finance support from the global community is therefore essential for African countries to build resilience against climate change.

### 157. Low productivity, aggravated by harvest and postharvest loss, has been the major driver of climate vulnerability as farming is expanding to fragile ecosystems, wetlands and mountains, causing deforestation, land degradation and drying of water resources, exposing farmers to extreme events.

### In response to these direct climate change threats on food security, the RE-Green programme aims to enhance resilience in agriculture through the implementation of Climate Smart Agriculture (CSA) interventions. The CGIAR-FU RS in seven African countries, AGRA, as the lead agency, is requesting GCF grant funds to support the roll out of the RE-Green programme in these countries. This will be achieved by strengthening the capacity for financial and market access. The rationale for GCF concessional support – beyond the basic climate change adaptation needs - has been presented below.

### Barriers to public-private sector alliances

### 159. Experience shows that countries rarely invest in climate change adaptation, less so in reducing food loss. This is largely due to the constrained fiscal space in the public sector, which limits opportunity for investment in climate change adaptation and mitigation initiatives. The situation has been further compounded by the extremely high levels of external debt, which prevents governments access to local financial markets. In addition, the lack of a robust institutional framework for climate change resulting high levels of external dependency, the target countries are reliant on external funding and support for climate change adaptation initiatives. However, International aid and climate finance flows may be insufficient in the future if they do not provide sufficient incentives for private sector investment in adaptation and mitigation strategies.

### Looking to the private sector as an alternative is a challenge because secure finance from conventional market-based interventions is lacking unless demand market is created and appropriate market linkages are put in place. The return on investment for such programmes is often seen as too low, especially when compared with other more profitable investments. In addition, the lack of climate change adaptation initiatives is viewed by the market as untested, with the absence of a track record of best practices. This has been compounded by the fact that resource-poor smallholder farmers may not be willing to invest in the FL-RS within their own farms because they are not sure whether these technologies will work or not. These technologies are unaffordable and farmers need a quasi-guarantee about the viability of these solutions before investing.

### 161. The stakeholder mapping AGRA conducted during the country strategy development, existing interventions and support focus mainly on last mile delivery, with few actors willing or able to invest in improving the underlying challenges that would facilitate agriculture and food systems transformation. Addressing this gap and a potential solution is to create demand for climate change adaptation technologies markets includedly, the right level of awareness and adoption is created and access to finance is de-clenched.

### Building the necessary capacity to develop and implement effective adaptation plans and investments requires substantial investments in education, training, and institutional strengthening, which, although catalysed by GCF support, will not be sufficient. Additional resources are therefore required if it is necessary to initiate a paradigm shift in the sector, supporting Africa-led solutions to tackle national priorities and investment plans.

### Consideration of alternative funding models.


---
**Page 50**
---

163\. In the process of designing the RE-GAIN programme, numerous other alternative finance models have been considered. Each option has some merit, each faces considerable barriers that prevent these models from working on their own. These alternative methods are summarised below and have been factored into the design of the RE-GAIN programme.

### Farmers pay cash for the high-tech technologies and for using the FL-RS services if they have savings or other alternative sources of income. This is often not the case in most contexts and countries.
- Farmers would need to save money, which is difficult given that many are small-scale service providers; these MSMEs have the liquidity to give credit to their customers. This is often not the case in most contexts and countries.

### Farmers pay cash for the high-tech technologies and for using the FL-RS services if they have savings or other alternative sources of income. This is often not the case in most contexts and countries.
- Farmers would need to save money, which is difficult given that many are small-scale service providers; these MSMEs have the liquidity to give credit to their customers. This is often not the case in most contexts and countries.

### Service providers offering FL-RS could get equipment financing through wealthy individuals, family and friends or other informal sources of finance. However, this is difficult for many farmers, both for the lender and borrowers, with several barriers currently preventing access to finance.
- Innovation in this area would be needed to make this a viable option.

### While credit options are possible, borrowers would need to offset the risk for lenders through co-ordination and guarantees.
- This option is further hindered by the high interest rates in some countries (particularly those with high inflation) and the lack of collateral security in rural contexts (documentation, transport).

### There exist risks for lenders (first time borrowings and then lack of repayment) as the financing of FL-RS on food crops have been minimal till date.
- The costs for lenders (loan origination, assessment and monitoring) are higher than for other sectors and lend to risk.

### There is also a risk to potential borrowers (market risk, profitability risk), as the business case for many of these technologies is not yet clear. This is particularly true in low-income rural areas where small-scale producers.
- External services (for example the provision of information on weather and harvesting practices) could be paid for with Government budget. However, most Governments are constrained in their budgets and there may be a need to seek additional funding from other sources such as donors or private investors.

### The government can provide some support to FL-RS service providers in terms of subsidies of the FL-RS; however this is limited, and they might not be able to pay 100% of the investment costs for creating assets. In addition, the cost savings on the margin profits do not make up for that investment. Private sector does invest in the activities that give them a return on their investment and that might not be on products sold in rural areas to low-income small-scale farmers.

**Rationale for concessional funding**

AGRA is requesting concessional finance in the form of grants for the RE-GAIN programme. The need for such support is based on the following considerations:

- The programme will not support communities with strategic conditional procurements to catalyse a paradigm shift in the post-harvest value chain.
- The programme will not be able to provide sufficient financial resources to cover the costs of intervention, their suitability for concessional finance, and how the programme will transfer the benefits of concessional funding to small-scale farmers.

### Do-risk first time users to invest in new technology that remains unproven in their context. The "pay for two, get one free" type of investment will make sure the concessional funding end up in farmers pockets.
- Reduce the cost of distributing FL-RS to rural areas where demand is not yet confirmed (the concessional funding will be used to cover this cost).
- Reduce the risk for FL-RS service providers to invest in equipment when the business case is not yet proven. The programme will not provide sufficient financial resources and will not be with on-going debts in case the equipment is not able to operate at profitable scale.
- Do-risk investments in awareness creation by sellers and distributors of FL-RS (extension, demonstration). The cost of awareness creation does not necessarily translate in adoption of FL-RS and profit for the sellers of FL-RS.

165. The level of concessionality transferred to the end user through the 'smart-subsidies' will depend on a number of factors which differ per country and per FL-RS. The less developed the local context is or any of these enabling factors, the greater the subsidy relative to the co-contribution form the recipient. These factors include:

- Is the FL-RS technology proven to be profitable in financial terms for all actors: farmers, agro-processors, etc?
- Is the distribution network of FL-RS already in place and reaching all areas where the FL-RS is needed?
- Are farmers already aware of the FL-RS and ready to adopt? Is there a ready market?
- Are manufacturers capable of forecasting demand and preference stock of FL-RS where the subsidy will be applied?
- Does the Government have the knowledge and tools to provide the FL-RS information in time and with sufficient accuracy to help farmers harvest in the right time using the right practices?

### B.6. Exit strategy

166. Long-term sustainability is at the core of the programme design, with GCF investments seen as catalysts of change within local systems. A key aspect of this GCF intervention is having an exit strategy through the development and strengthening of market-based approaches to ensure long-term sustainability of investments in FL-RS under Component 2. This approach to sustainability is centred on market development, which will be achieved by creating a conducive environment for private sector investment rates — working through the private sector to foster innovation and commercial opportunities. To establish such an enabling environment, the programme will focus on three key areas: (i) building public-private partnerships; (ii) establishing a conducive regulatory framework; and (iii) developing market-oriented demand-driven approaches of awareness, demonstrations, and market development under Outcome 1). The programme will also create a sector or supply chain, and develop enabling policies, strategies, and relationships and commitments over time with the target communities and expand to spits over areas beyond initial GCF investment period. Strengthening a market-led system is particularly important in the context of FL-RS as it is a technology that requires significant upfront investments. These will ensure that the farmers and the supporting value chain actors continue to see value in the decision to invest in FL-RS, and that they are able to sustain their investment over time. This will be enabled by public policy and institutions will ensure continued promotion of FL-RS through extension systems beyond the initial GCF investment period. The programme will also seek to build on the lessons learned from the long-term adaptation support provided through the environmental and social co-benefits. Such lessons include the importance of a conducive regulatory environment, as well as the need for strong enabling public policy and institutions will ensure continued promotion of FL-RS beyond the project lifespan.

167. The level of investment will vary depending on at post-harvest stage is that by demonstrating a positive track record of direct investments in rural MSMEs and farmer organisation, as well as by funding financial intermediaries for the creation of enabling conditions for private sector investment in FL-RS. This will enable the private sector to focus on the agriculture sector through the design and development of appropriate products, thereby creating a conducive environment for private sector investment in FL-RS, which enhances opportunities for continued investments in FL-RS beyond the initial subsidised finance modes.

C.3. Capacity building and technology development transfer

C.3.1 Does GCF funding finance capacity building activities? Yes ☐ No ☐

C.3.2 Does GCF funding finance technology development? Yes ☐ No ☐

Capacity development is a key aspect of the programme design, cutting across all three project components. Under Component 1, capacity development will focus on training smallholder farmers on the use of FL-RS and other related technologies (USD 0.5M). The capacity building will also include complementary services for FL-RS (USD 1.4M). The capacity of extension workers and agro-dealers will also be strengthened through training, including training support to farmers, traders and processors, with specific training of trainers programme (USD 29 M).

Under Component 2, similar capacity development support will be provided to train new FL-RS service providers and other key stakeholders in the value chain. This includes training for extension workers and business development support and market intelligence (USD 1.8M), as well as capacity and market development for all supply side market actors (USD 1.8M).

Under Component 3, the project will provide additional regional capacity development support to key local entities to support the scaling of successful FL-RS models (USD 2.6M).

In terms of technology, the project will not develop new technologies but will invest in technology transfers for existing FL-RS. This will be approached primarily through the development of innovative financing mechanisms and business models for FL-RS service providers and other key stakeholders, through partnerships with financial service providers, agro-dealers, MSMEs, youth groups, and smallholder farmers (USD 32.7M).

D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

### D.1 Impact potential

#### Adaptation Impact

174. The proposed programme will yield significant adaptation benefits for vulnerable smallholder farmers, their families, and communities across the seven target countries. At the core of these benefits is the expected reduction in climate change-induced food losses that are increasingly being experienced across Africa. Such reductions would be particularly beneficial to those smallholder farmers who are already highly vulnerable communities, mainly by enhancing the absorption capacity of smallholder farmers to seasonal climate variability. In addition, reduced food loss through improved management practices at a household level, reduced food loss will directly contribute to food security in the target communities — thereby contributing to GCF ARA 1.

While climate change is known to impact the full spectrum of food systems – from production through to consumption and waste – this project focuses on reducing food losses. This would result in a more resilient and somewhat under-served, component of food security. Reduced food loss through improved management practices at a household level will directly contribute to food security in the target communities by enhancing farm-level profitability. Additionally, reduction of food loss also leads to preserving the integrity of natural ecosystems that further has a positive impact on the resilience of land. The impacts of this project are expected to be felt across all seven countries and will contribute to the broader adaptation for agricultural resilience within each target country, filling a key gap in the broader landscape of food security.

However, it is important to note that while the proposed programme focuses primarily on primary production, the expected impacts on food security would remain even when evoked in isolation.


175. The nature of the approach – targeting smallholder farmers, with particular focus on women, youth groups, and vulnerable communities – contributes to GCF ARA 1. In addition to the increased food security, these benefits will also contribute to poverty reduction by increasing farm-level profitability and enhancing access to developing local markets and building up farmer cooperatives, women and youth groups, and MSMEs. The project will also contribute to GCF ARA 2 through the development of a suite of climate-resilient sustainable systems that will leverage partnerships with financial institutions and private sector actors to achieve significant investment beyond the proposed project.


176. Through this programme, an estimated total of 2,823 million people across the seven countries, distributed across the even target countries as shown in Table 7. Further details on the methodology for determining beneficiary numbers are presented in Supplementary Annex 1: Adaptation Impact Estimation.


#### D.2 Paradigm shift potential

177. The proposed programme has been designed to elicit a paradigm shift in the harvest and post-harvest development pathways for the seven target countries and continually in Africa. At the core of this shift is the use of GCF grants as a catalyst for change, stimulating markets and mobilising investment at scale. This approach will lean on AGRA’s convening power in Africa, drawing together key actors across the


---
**Page 57**
---

# Potential for scaling up and replication

The nature of the programme approach is such that it will stimulate local supply of affordable FL-RS options that will enable the scaling of interventions beyond the scope of the GCF investment. Specifically, the demonstration effect of the project will be amplified by the fact that the initial investments in farmer support under Outcome 2.1, will catalyse the establishment or reinforce strong local supply chains that will bring FL-RS to smallholder farmers at affordable rates. A key feature of this model is the notion of technology and service providers as enablers. For example, AGRA Africa’s AgriConnect platform will demonstrate and scale innovations that enable smallholder farmers to participate in both input and output markets. The programme will also work with a range of actors including input suppliers, commercial aggregators, business development service providers and financial institutions. This collaboration will be supported by the GCF investment through Outcome 1.2. These technology networks that connect farmers to market. The FL-RS solutions will be disseminated at scale by integrating them into existing supply chains and value chains. The programme will also work with governments on the demand-oriented actions of awareness, demonstration and market development under Outcome 1. The GCF investment will therefore have a catalytic effect in creating the enabling environment that will enable the scaling of interventions to the target communities beyond initial GCF investment.

In addition to the scaling potential noted above, the programme is structured such that the approaches to scaling up FL-RS are not limited by geographical boundaries and can be replicated across Africa. The project will have which when combined with the convening power of AGRA Africa will attract additional investments in the region. Component 3 of the project will establish an enabling environment for sustainable FL-RS markets (see below). This will include a range of activities to support the development of market infrastructure, collaboration with continental and regional bodies and national governments, including African regional organisations such as the East African Community, to initiate the replication of the programme’s innovative models for scaling FL-RS across the region.

## Potential for knowledge sharing and learning

The innovations, especially on innovative financing and business models within the programme have the potential to be scaled up beyond the project. The programme will also work with farmers to improve their food losses and thus increasing their productivity and their resilience to climate shocks. Activities under Outcome 2.1 will capture these lessons and develop knowledge products to share best practices at the regional level. This knowledge sharing framework will be fundamental to the replication potential described above.

## Contribution to the regulatory framework and creation of enabling environment

Through the partnerships that will be established under the programme, finance and business models, the project will focus on national governments in partner countries to help them realise their commitments to facilitate the scaling up of FL-RS. This will include support for policy development by governments to enact forward-thinking policies and regulations that seek to integrate climate-resilient FL-RS into national agricultural strategies; lobbying for the establishment of new incentives, and the update of certifications and standards for FL-RS.

#### Environmental co-benefits

183. Programme activities to reduce post-harvest food loss will have several environmental co-benefits:

- **Reduced land degradation and improved water use efficiency:** Reducing post-harvest food loss will increase the net production of agricultural land, thereby reducing area of land and amount of water needed for irrigation. This will help in improving the overall productivity of land and water use in the agriculture sector, thereby reducing the impact of agriculture on land and water resources.
- **Reduced greenhouse gas emissions:** The reduction in post-harvest food loss will reduce the need to compensate for food loss. The resulting reduction in the rate of land use change will help preserve natural ecosystems that act as carbon sinks, thus reducing greenhouse gas emissions.
- **Decreased in net use of fertilizers and pesticides:** The improved net-productivity of agriculture will reduce the need for additional fertilizer application, as well as the overall energy demand, particularly related to regrowing of lost food.
- **Reduced environmental impacts of improper disposal of spoiled food:** For example the release of air pollutants when waste is burned, or the production of methane (see mitigation benefits in Annex 1).
- **Increased regreening and improved land use:** Reduced expansion of farming will release more land to reforest and improve degraded lands.
- **Reduced loss of biodiversity:** Land expansion into forests and fragile areas is the major cause of biodiversity loss on the continent, which is threatening the livelihoods of current and future generations.


---
**Page 59**
---

# Mitigation Impact

Through the process of reducing food losses and creating capacity of key players in sustainable FL RS solutions, the proposed programme will contribute to reducing greenhouse gas emissions: contributing to each country’s NDCs. The emission reductions are achieved through two primary pathways. The first, and most significant pathway is the reduction of post-harvest loss (without considering land expansion). The second pathway is the reduction of food losses as a driver of land use change linked to compensatory agricultural expansion. Combining these pathways results in an estimated total emission reduction of 117,279 tCO2e over 10 years. Details on the emission reductions methodology are provided in Annex 22, with a country-specific breakdown provided in Table 16.

## Socioeconomic and health co-benefits

In targeting the livelihoods of highly vulnerable smallholder farming communities, the proposed programme will yield multiple socioeconomic co-benefits. Some of these co-benefits arise as a direct result of the core component of improved post-harvest practices, while others arise as a result of the actions to develop the FL RS market in target communities.

- Increased food quality and safety: Improved post-harvest processes that reduce food waste will also improve food quality and safety. This will help to reduce the risk of foodborne illnesses and other risks associated with contaminated food.
- Improved public health: Reduced food waste will enhance public health systems through reduced incidence of nutrition-related illness.
- Job creation: By working with local suppliers and agro-dealers, while also supporting the business development initiatives of smallholder farmers, this programme can generate employment opportunities that extend beyond the initial investment. This will particularly benefit women who are often excluded from other agricultural value chains due to their gender and youth integration, thereby leading to development outcomes that are gender sensitive.
- Market stability: Reduced food loss can help stabilize food prices by ensuring a more consistent supply. This can protect both producers and consumers from price volatility.

## Gender-sensitive development benefit

The proposed programme has been designed with gender sensitivity at its core, with each component targeting women in the FL RS value chain and only ever equal rights participation of women, but also actively empowering women within the FL RS value chain. This includes business development opportunities directly targeted at women’s groups and women-led enterprises, recognizing them as key actors in the market and supporting their capacity to access markets and resources. The gender-sensitive mechanism of the project which primarily focuses on targeting farmer groups provide a platform that are safe spaces for gender and youth integration and therefore leads to development outcomes that are gender sensitive.

### D.4. Needs of recipient

RE-GAIN targets seven African countries that are all recognised as being highly vulnerable to climate change, which impacts all sectors of society. Of particular concern is the highly vulnerable agriculture

# CC Strategies and Policies

## Burkina Faso’s National Sustainable Development Policy (PNDS)
Burkina Faso’s National Sustainable Development Policy (PNDS) includes a key focus on reducing post-harvest losses through the promotion of agricultural entrepreneurship and the development of value chains for sustainable agricultural practices. This is complemented by the National Economic and Social Development Plan 2018-2023, which focuses on increasing productivity and reducing harvesting and post-harvest losses as part of the strategy to restore security and peace, strengthen national resilience, and transform the country into a food surplus nation.

## The National Climate Change Adaptation Plan (PNCA)
The National Climate Change Adaptation Plan (PNCA) also addresses the need for resilience in agricultural systems amid climate variability and change, undergoing adaptive agricultural practices and sustainable methods. The PNCA includes a focus on reducing post-harvest losses through the promotion of value chains that address these efforts, providing strategic guidance to the agricultural sector to reduce post-harvest losses and improve food security.

## Ethiopia’s Agricultural Transformation Agenda
Ethiopia’s Agricultural Transformation Agenda focuses on improving productivity and reducing post-harvest losses through the implementation of the National Agricultural Investment Plan (2018) and the Working Strategy for Strengthening Ethiopia’s Teff Value Chain (2015). This also includes a focus on reducing post-harvest losses and enhancing value chains.

## Kenya’s National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy
Kenya’s National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy focuses primarily at post-harvest, numerous national policies and strategies identify agriculture as a key sector for sustainable and climate-resilient development. This includes the Ten-Year Development Plan (2016-2025), the Kenya Vision 2030, the National Agriculture Sector Development Strategy, the National Economic Strategy which focus on agriculture and private sector investment as a driver to reach middle-income status by 2030, and the National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy. These strategies are also laid out in the National Agricultural Investment Plan (2018) and the Agricultural Sector Policy and Investment Framework (2017-2026). The Kenya’s National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy (2015-2030) targets significant GHG reductions by 2030. The National Adaptation Plan (2017) focuses on agriculture and rural development, with the aim of reducing poverty and increasing food security. The Agriculture Sector Policy (2017–2030) integrates best practices for effective extension services, and the Nutrition Sensitive Agriculture Development (2015-2030) aims to enhance nutrition in agricultural programs.

## Kenya’s National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy
Kenya is committed to achieving middle-income status by 2030 through focusing on modernizing agriculture and achieving a 10% annual economic growth rate. The National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy (2015-2030) focuses on addressing agricultural emissions and enhancing productivity and resilience. Kenya’s Agricultural Sector Development Strategy (2017–2030) aims to increase the share of smallholder farmers in commercial production and post-harvest loss reduction. Kenya’s National Determination Contribution (2020-2030) seeks to reduce GHG emissions by 45% below business as usual, with a focus on agriculture. The National Adaptation Plan (2015-2030) enhances climate resilience towards Vision 2030, promoting climate-smart agricultural practices and sustainable land management. The Kenya’s Climate Smart Agriculture Investment Framework (2018–2027) provides guidance for innovative and transformative initiatives, focusing on productivity, resilience, and emissions reduction.

## Financial Structure – adequacy, reasonableness and return on investment

### Affordability to access finance, market access, financial literacy, and consumer awareness are key challenges for smallholder farmers seeking to adopt practices that reduce food loss in the agricultural value chain. The project will address these challenges through a multi-pronged programmatic approach that combines demand-side, supply-side, and enabling environment components.

### The combination of both financial and non-financial approaches effectively ensures market failures related to the adoption of climate-smart agriculture (CSA) practices are addressed in the value chain.

### The two key financial models—1) catalytic grants for smallholder farmers and 2) catalytic grants for agricultural MSMEs—is one concessional funding to incentivize private sector manufacturers and financial institutions (FiIs) to:

- Provide commercial solutions that are within the affordability envelope of farmers while remaining commercially viable to private sector suppliers.
- Provide tailored financial products that are affordable to farmers, while mitigating credit risk to financial institutions.


---
**Page 64**
---

196\. By focusing on a 5-year period, the program aims to demonstrate long-term market viability to suppliers (both physical and financial), build the necessary credit history required to access financial products, and encourage private sector investment.

197\. Non-financial interventions drive adoption of these solutions from farmers by focusing on building capacity and awareness through training, educational materials, and village-based agent (VBA) demonstrations. Adoption is also encouraged by creating linkages between farmers and buyers, which are critical to creating linkages between private sector manufacturers, MSMEs and Small hold farmers.

198\. This combined approach of addressing both supply and demand side constraints is expected to mobilize the necessary investments in the seven target RE-GAM countries over a 5-year period, with an initial investment of US$ 105 million. Given the program cost of US$ 105 million this represents a catalytic effect or return on investment (ROI) of approximately 24 times the original investment across each of the seven countries. This excludes the long-term impact of the program’s ability to create new markets for food loss reduction solutions, which will benefit both buyers and suppliers (manufacturers and FIs), which will catalyze new private sector markets while addressing food-loss and associated climate-related challenges.

### Economic Efficiency and effectiveness of the proposed project/programme and return

199\. The proposed programme is expected to generate a total investment of US$ 105 million expected to mobilize additional investments in adaptation outcomes. By reducing post-harvest food losses, the programme contributes directly to increased food availability, with co-benefits of reduced greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions and improved health outcomes through reduced aflatoxin exposure.

200\. An economic analysis for the programme was conducted using a cost-benefit analysis approach, which is based on a 10-year time horizon. The analysis used discount rates as presented in Annex 3. The analyses weighed up the total programme costs against the benefits of the programme over this period. The results show that the proposed programme would be beneficial with the total programme investment generating an economic Net Present Value (NPV) of US$ 250 million, which represents a return on investment (ROI) of approximately 24 times the original investment of around 56%. On this basis, there is a clear economic case for implementing the proposed programme. Additional benefits are also expected from the co-benefits associated with reduced GHG emissions and other assumptions, including around the choice of discount rate, the shadow price of carbon, and the proportion of the total programme costs that would be covered by private sector investments in addition to public interventions. The results of these analyses are presented in Annex 3 and show that, at a programme level, the above summary statistics are robust to changes in the input values.

201\. At a country-level, the economic analysis shows that the highest net benefits are experienced in Burkina Faso and Ethiopia and can be explained, at least in part, by the choice of crops used to illustrate the impacts of interventions, and which have comparatively higher prices; the relatively high prevalence of aflatoxin contamination in Burkina Faso.

### Application of best practices

202\. An evaluation of proposed physical food loss reduction solutions (FL-RS) was conducted across all seven target RE-GAM countries to identify those with the highest potential to reduce post-harvest food losses and associated climate-related challenges. The results from this evaluation, which were presented in Annex 7, show that each of the seven countries provided critical nuances including advantages, disadvantages, and barriers for use.

particularly for smallholder farmers, while a detailed assessment of best practices was used to underpin the assessment.

In addition to the above-mentioned prioritizations focusing on climate resilience, the final selection of projects considered additional prioritization factors to ensure the success of the RE-GAIN programme and achieve lasting systemic changes in all target countries. These included: developed solution's estimated cost-effectiveness; alignment with national agricultural policies; the potential for scaling up; the use of the solutions; current level of awareness of the farmers about the solution’s proper use and maintenance; and the availability of local resources to support the implementation.

Based on the above-mentioned analysis, basket of options approach was selected for the RE-GAIN programme FL-RS adaptation strategy – adopting bespoke combinations of FL-RS tailored to the context of each target country. The basket includes: (i) improved crop varieties (e.g., drought-tolerant and/or stress-tolerant; preferably solar-powered); combined with moisture meters for monitoring the level of moisture in the target crops, and communal storage structures, with the FL-RS lens on the use of individual farm-level storage solutions such as jerry cans, plastic drums, or other locally available materials (e.g., plastic bags, sacks) used for storage of the crops; and storage protectants and control agents, preferably biological origin.

AGRA’s last mile delivery model through training and supporting VIBAs (village-based advisors) and ag-dealers in the target countries, who are largely in place to take the FL-RS solutions to farmers and their families. ## Core Indicator E: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to strengthening national policies and frameworks for low-carbon development in a country-driven manner

### Development of national policies, strategies, and action plans aimed at addressing the agricultural sector and promoting sustainable land management (SLM) as a priority focus for many of the African governments

The RE-GUN programmes will support the development of local markets and support to farmers (e.g., bio-cost calculation and standards for FL-RS quality), and promote innovative financial mechanisms that can unlock local markets for FL-RS. The RE-GUN programmes will incentivise establishment of sustainable FL-RS in the agricultural sector. This is achieved through:

First, there is limited or no use of land degradation neutrality (LDN) indicators in the formulation of policies and strategies on post-harvest loss reduction, soil conservation, and carbon sequestration and harmonisation across sub-sectors and countries. Second, there are weak regulatory frameworks for sustainable agriculture sector. Third, where policies or regulations exist, they have not continued to contribute agricultural input innovation (e.g., improved seeds) — with no subsidy on post-harvest technologies and services. Fourth, there is a focus of information and extension services, which are limited in terms of post-harvest production, and hence so to post-harvest processing and storage. Fifth, there is limited investment in storage and processing technology innovation.

### Core Indicator E: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to the deployment, dissemination, demonstration, transfer and innovation

The development of FL-RS in the target countries is extremely limited, particularly in terms of the availability of markets due to an extremely limited penetration of suppliers and service providers. There are no innovative financial mechanisms that can unlock local markets for FL-RS.

The RE-GUN programmes will support the development of local markets and support to farmers (e.g., bio-cost calculation and standards for FL-RS quality), and promote innovative financial mechanisms that can unlock local markets for FL-RS. The RE-GUN programmes will incentivise establishment of sustainable FL-RS in the agricultural sector. This is achieved through:

First, there is limited or no use of land degradation neutrality (LDN) indicators in the formulation of policies and strategies on post-harvest loss reduction, soil conservation, and carbon sequestration and harmonisation across sub-sectors and countries. Second, there are weak regulatory frameworks for sustainable agriculture sector. Third, where policies or regulations exist, they have not continued to contribute agricultural input innovation (e.g., improved seeds) — with no subsidy on post-harvest technologies and services. Fourth, there is a focus of information and extension services, which are limited in terms of post-harvest production, and hence so to post-harvest processing and storage. Fifth, there is limited investment in storage and processing technology innovation.

### Core Indicator E: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to the deployment, dissemination, demonstration, transfer and innovation

The development of FL-RS in the target countries is extremely limited, particularly in terms of the availability of markets due to an extremely limited penetration of suppliers and service providers. There are no innovative financial mechanisms that can unlock local markets for FL-RS.

### Core Indicator E: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to strengthening national policies and frameworks for low-carbon development in a country-driven manner

The RE-GUN programmes will support the development of local markets and support to farmers (e.g., bio-cost calculation and standards for FL-RS quality

## Core indicator D: Degree to which local markets contribute to market development at the sectoral, local or national level

### Local markets for FL RS are primarily among smallholder farming communities.
Limited access to markets and infrastructure constraints demand FL RS, which in turn increases their value and price. This problem is exacerbated by a lack of investment in rural infrastructure that can catalyse investment in local market development.

## Activity 1.1.5: Conduct scouting missions to identify and engage market actors for FL RS equipment.

### Objectives:
- Host scouting mission in each target country and surrounding areas.
- Identify potential market actors, including smallholder farmers, traders, and processors.
- Develop a stakeholder map, identifying key players at various stages of the value chain.

## Activity 1.1.6: Conduct demonstrations of locally appropriate FL RS equipment.

### Objectives:
- Demonstrate at least one physical and one non-physical demonstration in each target country per year for five consecutive years.
- Engage project partners in target countries to develop a capacity building plan.
- Conduct demonstrations of non-localized technology.
- Develop a stakeholder map, identifying key players at various stages of the value chain.

## Activity 1.1.7: Provide technical assistance to food processors to facilitate a shift towards white-grain flour production.

### Objectives:
- Demonstrate awareness raising and capacity development in each target country.
- Conduct process with smallholders and potential buyers such as retailers, processors, and traders.
- Develop a stakeholder map, identifying key players at various stages of the value chain.

## Activity 1.1.8: Provide technical assistance to support the establishment of market linkages and coordination between market actors.

### Objectives:
- Conduct a market assessment (including identifying gaps) in each target country.
- Host technical workshops in each target country for capacity building.
- Value chain map and gap analysis for each target country.
- At least one technical workshop per year in each target community.

## Activity 1.1.9: Provide technical assistance to smallholders, MSMEs, and potential buyers such as retailers, processors, and traders.

### Objectives:
- Conduct a market assessment (including identifying gaps) in each target country.
- Host technical workshops in each target country for capacity building.
- Value chain map and gap analysis for each target country.
- At least one technical workshop per year in each target community.

- A financial model per local partner in each community, with at least one based on model 1 or 2 and one based on model 3.

## Capacity building
- At least one training workshop targeting MSMEs and cooperative service providers.
- At least two workshops per year on the development of partnerships.

## Market intelligence reports
- Disburse subsidies based on market intelligence reports.
- Briefing papers on the integration of FLRS into RE-GAN programmes.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0: PAID IN E

| Activity 2.3.2: Structure pre-financing arrangements that include FL-RS |
| --- |

To further unlock credit for accessing FL-RS, the RE-GAM programme will provide technical assistance to key value chain players — for example off-takers, input providers, and traders — to develop business cases and financial models with their preferred financing partners who will derisk the provision of finance. Assistance will also be provided in developing a robust data collection system, including a platform, and training/capacity development that will help mainstream FL-RS into existing lending platforms. This will enable banks to offer additional credit for financing access to FL-RS. The model, which is widely promoted by the GCF, will provide an opportunity for financial institutions to diversify their loan portfolios.

### Activity 2.3.1: Support the revision of policies in support of enabling FL-RS investments, including harmonization of certification and standards for FL-RS quality

AGRA will collaborate with government entities to review and update key policies that are critical to the successful implementation of the RE-GAM programme within national contexts. This will include engaging relevant government ministries and agencies to provide technical assistance in areas such as policy development, capacity building, the development of local markets and support for farmers for the uptake of FL-RS. This will be done through a combination of training workshops, policy briefs, and other evidence generated and lesson learned in FL-RS from target communities and countries.

AGRA will also actively participate in policy advocacy initiatives, working with key stakeholders to ensure that policies are aligned with the RE-GAM programme and support the uptake of FL-RS but are currently not fully enforced or supported. These activities include providing technical assistance for the development of national plans for implementation of the RE-GAM programme and widespread adoption of FL-RS globally.

AGRA will provide technical assistance to local and national government entities in developing policies that will enable the successful implementation of the RE-GAM programme, including policy briefs involving central and local government through the establishment of a national steering committee. This will ensure alignment of the RE-GAM programme with national and regional development priorities and support for farmers for the uptake of FL-RS from target communities and countries.

AGRA will also provide technical assistance at district level to build institutional capacity in developing policies that will enable the successful implementation of the RE-GAM programme, including guidelines, targeting tools and technology delivery mechanisms, to develop plans for the scaling up of successful FL-RS models.

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements

206. The data structure for RE-GAIN has been developed to be in line with AGRA's existing M&E structure, to ensure a cohesive approach, as well as with GCF’s policy requirements such as Monitoring and Accountability Framework and Integrated Results Management Framework. It has also been designed to integrate the baseline data (Outcome KPIs and Output KPIs above) and the broader programme performance, respectively. A baseline survey will be conducted during the inception phase of the project to collect the baseline data for all indicators applicable based on the roles alongside the baseline results as part of the inception report. The performance against the Outcome and Output KPIs will be monitored by the M&E officer with oversight from the AGRA M&E Team and Board. The GCF-supported project KPIs will be monitored during the implementation phase, while the broader programme performance monitoring of the programme over the long-term. The responsibilities of the M&E team will include:

- Data collection and performance measurement.
  - The M&E officer will work closely with each project intermediary, to aggregate data on the non-financial indicators from the intermediaries and from the intermediaries. This will include data to support the development of site-specific baselines; Economic activity: Market share; Productivity: Yield per hectare; Income: Household income; and Livelihoods: Number of households economically aggregated by intermediaries during implementation relating to production, sales volumes and value added.

- Periodic site visits will be conducted to assess and verify the results presented by the intermediaries.
  - When necessary and deemed necessary, external consultants may support the data collection and measurement process.

207. The monitoring and reporting on the Outcome and Output KPIs will be integrated into the broader performance monitoring system for AGRA. AGRA has a comprehensive MRV system that extends from grantees, to country, region, corporate level review and verification processes supported by an online Monitoring Reporting Verification (MRV) System. AGRA will also implement a Data Quality Assurance (DQA) mechanism to ensure quality performance data are collected and reported. AGRA will integrate RE-GAIN into the MRV system for reporting on the Outcome KPIs and Output KPIs, which will be reviewed by the AGRA Board, in collaboration with the AGRA M&E Team, during the programme’s inception and will continue to be updated as the programme progresses. The AGRA Board and M&E Team will work with the project intermediaries to ensure that the data are accurate, relevant indicators and results to assess the effectiveness of the M&E system and amend it where appropriate.

Evaluation

208. Performance will be independently evaluated against the Outcome and Output KPIs at the mid- and final stages of the project implementation period, specifically in years 3 and 5, as per GCF evaluation requirements. The evaluations will be conducted by an independent evaluation specialist, under the oversight of AGRA.

Financial Reporting

209. AGRA will manage its finances and produce semi-annual reports that summarizes material developments of these. AGRA will develop valuation processes and procedures for the underlying portfolio investments and ensure these are done in accordance with Accounting Standards Codification (ASC) 820-10 on a semi-annual basis on June 30th and December 31st.

# F. Risk Assessment and Management

## Selected Risk Factor 1

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Low | Medium |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

### Description

Lack of programme ownership by beneficiaries and target governments leads to weak uptake or sustainability of FLRS.

### Mitigation Measures

Detailed stakeholder engagement has been conducted throughout the programme design stage, and will continue throughout implementation. This will ensure that the project is relevant for the needs of the target countries. This will also help to ensure that the probability of this risk remains low.

## Selected Risk Factor 2

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Medium | High |
| Technical and operational | Medium | High |

### Description

Financial crisis caused by unstable macro-economic variables impacts the market development and increases the risk profile of financial institutions.

### Mitigation Measures

The financing framework has been built on a foundation of a detailed analysis of local markets and economic fundamentals, ensuring that the mechanisms are robust to withstand fluctuations in the macro-economic situation. Multiple sources of funding have also been identified which will provide an additional buffer against market volatility and its impact on the effectiveness of the programme.

## Selected Risk Factor 3

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Medium | High |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

### Description

Political instability in some target countries leads to disruptions to the implementation of project interventions, or non-compliance with contractual obligations.

### Mitigation Measures

Given the low probability, AGRA will increase engagement with national stakeholders to ensure investments and processes are not disrupted, reducing the potential impact of political instability on programme outcomes.

## Selected Risk Factor 4

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Low | Medium |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

### Description

Lack of willingness from end users to adopt FLRS reduces market transformation potential and long-term impacts of the project.

### Mitigation Measures

The AGRA team will engage in extensive awareness campaigns and capacity development to ensure that farmers fully understand the threats posed by climate change on post-harvest loss, the benefits of FLRS for reducing losses, and how they can use the technology. This will increase the willingness of end users to adopt investments which increases availability and affordability of FLRS; these actions will reduce the probability of this risk low.

## Selected Risk Factor 5

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Low | Medium |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

### Description

Credit default and end beneficiary failure to repay the loans and meet their contractual obligations may reduce the project’s financial sustainability and erode stakeholder confidence.

### Mitigation Measures

The risk will be mitigated by ensuring that AGRA has a good understanding of its customers and the market within which they operate. The financial models have also been designed with multiple levels of defence, including small subsidies to end users in case of default. This will ensure that the project is financially sustainable and able to withstand disruption in value chains and capacity development of farmers and MSMEs. Assessments will also be conducted by AGRA to ensure that all credit providers provide procedures and processes on account of ARRL.

## Selected Risk Factor 6

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Low | Medium |
| Description | Low | Medium |

### Description

Legal disputes may impede the implementation of project interventions in individual countries.

### Mitigation Measures

The legal team will deploy a strong legal department and internal controls to ensure that the activities align with regulations in each country, ensuring the project can be implemented within the programme timeline.

G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1. GCF Policies and social risk assessment

#### Risk Categorisation

210. The programme is assigned a Category B risk rating as per the GCF Revised Environmental and Social Framework (ESF). The rationale for the risk rating is as follows:
- The Programme will target established smallholder farmers and MSMEs and as such will not be associated with adverse land use change.
- The Programme is designed to facilitate the adoption of FL-RS solutions by smallholder farmers and MSMEs. These solutions are manufactured, transported and installed in-country and are not directly involved in the manufacturing, transportation and/or installation of the FL-RS solutions;
- The E&SD risks associated with the programme will be assessed at the country level for each of the Programme in each scope country, the contextual circumstances of the existing smallholder farmers who participate in the interventions and the co-country partners chosen to facilitate the interventions; and
- The risk assessment conducted on the Programme highlighted limited adverse E&SD risks and a reach of 10% of the population. These risks are considered manageable and will be addressed through actionable mitigation measures (see Programme E&SD Risk Management Framework).

#### Programme E&SD Risk Assessment

211. In order to meet the GCF E&SD due diligence requirements expected on a Category B type project, AGRA has conducted an overall programme level E&SD risk assessment for each of the scope countries and each in-scope country and focus crops and an intervention level E&SD risk assessment taking into account the specific Programme activities. The key E&SD risks stemming from these assessments are summarised as follows:

##### Overall Programme E&SD Risks

212. In AGRA procures the services of local NGOs and/or private sector actors to support the building of the capacity of smallholder farmers and MSMEs to build the connections between manufacturers, agro-dealers, and the end recipients such as smallholder farmers and MSMEs). Repudiational risks may exist associated with the Programme partners chosen due to poor E&SD performance and/or reputation.

213. AGRA will also partner with local or international manufacturers of FL-RS technologies for inclusion into the Programme. Repudiational risks associated with manufacturers due to E&SD related sub-standard labour and working conditions including potential for poor occupational health and safety practices;
- Labour protection control measures and waste management; and/or
- Road transport health and safety risks associated with transporters (internal/external) of FL-RS technologies.

214. In AGRA will partner with locally available financial institutions to provide financing at preferential rates to either manufacturers or smallholder farmers / MSMEs. Repudiational risks associated with financial institutions include the risk that they may not be able to meet their obligations (especially those related to microfinance institutions) which may lead to smallholder farmer indebtedness.

#### Contextual E&SD Risks

215. If conflict areas are identified in certain regions in Burkina Faso and Ethiopia where the Programme is implemented, this could lead to challenges in meeting Programme objectives including potential health, safety and security risks to Programme personnel.


---
**Page 83**
---

216. i) Depending on the country, maturity and oversight of labour regulations, reputational risks associated with labour and working conditions may exist on the target smallholder farms and/or MSMEs. These may include the presence of child and/or forced labour practices on these farms, poor health and safety practices, and non-compliance with labour laws. The programme will align with the requirements of international standards such as those set out in the ILO and IFC PS 2.

ii) AGRA conducted a desktop Indigenous Peoples (IPs) screening and vulnerability assessment of IPs across all focus countries including Ethiopia, Kenya, Malawi, Tanzania, Uganda, and Zambia. However, potential IPs were identified in specific regions within Burkina Faso, Eritrea, Ethiopia, Kenya, Tanzania, and Uganda. The programme will work with IPs to ensure that their rights are respected during the implementation of IPs, affect access to land or natural resources, nor will the Programme interventions be likely to include activities that could negatively impact IPs. Where IPs are identified as being affected by the proposed activities, they will be implemented. However, the identification of the IPs underscores the need for careful and proactive consideration of any risk that might arise during programme implementation and requires tailored approaches at scale. The programme will work with IPs in a manner consistent with their rights and interests. In addition to this regard, the Programme will proactively work with IPs and their representatives to ensure that the proposed activities are not likely to have an adverse impact on IPs. Where IPs are identified as being affected by the proposed activities, they will be implemented. However, the identification of the IPs underscores the need for careful and proactive consideration of any risk that might arise during programme implementation and requires tailored approaches at scale. The programme will work with IPs in a manner consistent with their rights and interests.

218. i) The Programme includes the introduction and promotion of novel FL-RS technologies. The innovative technology introduced to small holder farmers and/or MSMEs may lead to unintended health, safety and/or environmental risks if not adequately trained on their correct use and/or waste disposal.

ii) AGRA promotes NGOs to create awareness and demonstrate the FL-RS in collaboration with communities. However, some of these NGOs may have limited technical competencies or may not have adequate technical competencies to provide training on unintended health, safety and/or environmental risks associated with the FL-RS technologies. NGOs may deliver training that is not culturally appropriate or inclusive and/or based from an inclusion and gender perspective.

Programme E&S Risk Management Framework

220. AGRA has in place a centralised Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) that will be applicable to all initiatives initiated in AGRA’s focus countries. The ESMS is designed to ensure that potential environmental, social and health risks associated with the proposed activities are identified where communities and the environment are enhanced. It follows the guidance of IFC Performance Standard 1 and includes a risk management framework for assessing, managing and mitigating E&S risks at country level.

In addition, AGRA has developed a Programme E&S Action Plan (ESAP), which provides a framework for E&S action across the entire RE-GAIN Programme. Once the specific communities and beneficiaries have been identified, the ESAP will be used to identify potential environmental, social and health risks associated with impacts associated with the individual country, region, crop and combination of FL-RS applicable to the specific intervention envisaged. The Programme ESAP sets out mitigation measures for each of the risks identified in the ESAP.

222. Full details on Programme E&S risk identification and mitigations are provided in Annex 6 of the Funding Proposal.
