### G.2. Gender assessment and action plan

#### 223. To ensure that RE-GAIN Programme is designed, financed, implemented and monitored in a gender-sensitive and socially inclusive manner, a comprehensive Gender Assessment and Gender and Inclusion Action Plan has been developed (Annex 8). These outputs comply with the GCF’s Gender Policy, as well as its Gender and Inclusion Strategy, ESMS Minimum Safeguarding of Vulnerable Persons and Youth Policy.

#### RE-GAIN's Gender Assessment considers the various factors (including those exacerbated by climate change) that enable and/or impede gender equality progress at a national level. The assessment identifies opportunities for women and youth to contribute to inclusive resilience building within post-harvest value chains, as well as their role in decision-making processes. It also informs the Action Plan, which defines activities, indicators and targets specific to women and youth in their capacities as smallholders, cooperative members, ESMSs and FLR rights holders.

#### 225. The Gender Assessment is a country-led exercise carried out on a country-by-country basis on the key contextual factors that enable and/or impede gender equality progress at a national level, and it draws from 28 national and local stakeholder engagements spread evenly across the seven countries.

#### Using these information sources to examine the disparities (and underlying drivers) between women, men and youth within the context of agriculture and climate change in each of RE-GAIN's countries, several clear recommendations are made on how to address gender equality gaps. The ability for financial institutions to finance for women and youth is a fundamental enabler for increased and more equitable participation in FLR rights and post-harvest value chains. Therefore, it is critical that inclusive and diverse finance options and advisory services are made available to these groups. This includes the need for financial service providers to be aware of gender-specific practices or preferences; and on financial management and marketing – including options available, budgeting, pricing, etc.

#### The design of any financial product or service must be in a gender-sensitive and highly context specific manner. This must be coupled with sensitive outreach strategies that are tailored to the needs of women and youth. In addition, access to financial, technical, infrastructural and capacity building resources. The deliberate and targeted appointment of extension service delivery partners is fundamental to achieving many of these recommendations. These partners should have a demonstrated track record in working with women and youth and other marginalized groups. It will be important that the RE-GAIN Programme has a dedicated Gender Expert on FLR's needed to create sustainable shifts in the way women and youth engage in, and benefit from, FLR rights and post-harvest value chains. This will ensure that gender equality is mainstreamed into inclusive, enforceable FLR policies. These recommendations, together with RE-GAIN’s overarching goal of increasing the number of women and youth who are able to participate in FLR rights, will lead to both quantitative and qualitative targets over the five-year period. Implementing the measures set out in this Action Plan will require a significant amount of resources, including financial and human resource support. A dedicated RE-GAIN Gender Expert will oversee the delivery and M&E of the Gender and Inclusion Action Plan, and ensure continued coordination with other processes, including application of the GCF’s ESMSs and Safeguarding policies and ESMS.

#### The Gender Assessment has been budgeted for the full five years, along with related expenses required to deliver all activities.


### G.3. Financial management and procurement

#### 227. RE-GAIN Programme will use a financial management system designed to ensure accountability, transparency, and efficient use of resources. Below is an overview of AGRANA's financial management structure, including financial monitoring systems, financial accounting, auditing, and disbursement


---
**Page 85**
---

# Financial Monitoring Systems

AGRA transitioned from Navision to an integrated system - Oracle Crystal. It employs robust financial monitoring systems to oversee the management and utilization of funds.

## 1. Financial Monitoring Systems

### Budget Monitoring:
AGRA uses a budgetary control system that monitors expenditures against approved budgets. This allows for real-time tracking of financial performance and ensures that any variances are identified early.

### Financial Reporting:
AGRA's financial monitoring includes regular financial reporting. These reports provide detailed information on the status of grants, ensuring transparency and enabling informed decisions. The reports are typically produced on a quarterly basis and include budget variance analysis, fund utilization reports, and financial forecasts.

### Grant Management System (AMS):
All grant disbursements are processed through an Oracle system. AGRA uses a dedicated grant management system-AMS. This system tracks grant disbursements, monitors compliance with reporting requirements, and ensures that grantees are using the funds as intended.

## 2. Financial Accounting

AGRA's financial accounting practices are designed to ensure accuracy, consistency, and compliance with international accounting standards. The key elements include:

### Accrual and Modified Cash Accounting:
AGRA uses both accrual and modified cash accounting systems. Under accrual accounting, revenue is recognized when it is earned and some costs its only when cash is exchanged as this is a requirement for some of our donors. This method provides a more accurate picture of financial performance.

### Internal Controls:
AGRA has a set of internal controls in place to prevent and detect errors or fraud. These controls include segregation of duties, authorization of transactions, and reconciliation of accounts.

### Financial Software:
AGRA uses Oracle financial management software to record and manage its accounting activities, including accounts payable, accounts receivable, general ledger, and fixed assets management.

## 3. Auditing

AGRA places significant emphasis on auditing to ensure the integrity of its financial management. The audit process includes:

### Internal Audits:
AGRA conducts regular internal audits to assess the effectiveness of its internal controls, compliance with policies and procedures, and the accuracy of financial records. The internal audit is subject to oversight by an Audit Committee.

### External Audits:
In addition to internal audits, AGRA is subject to annual external audits conducted by independent auditors. These audits are designed to ensure that the organization complies with relevant regulations, and the effectiveness of its financial management systems. The external audit reports are shared with stakeholders, including donors and board members, to ensure transparency.

### Audit Committee:
AGRA has an Audit Committee that oversees both internal and external audits. This committee reviews the results of these audits and takes corrective actions, and making recommendations to the Board on matters related to financial governance.


---
**Page 86**
---

# 4. Disbursement Structure and Methods

## 231. AGRA’s disbursement structure is designed to ensure that funds are distributed efficiently and in accordance with donor agreements and organizational objectives. The key elements include:

### Procurement: 
AGRA provides support through the procurement of various partner organizations/Implementing agencies, including governments, research institutions, and NGOs. AGRA ensures that all procurements are conducted in a manner consistent with its procurement policy.

### Direct Payments:
For projects and non-project costs, AGRA makes direct payments to vendors or service providers. These payments are processed through AGRA’s financial management system.

## 232. AGRA has a fully resourced procurement unit with clear policies and procedures that meet best practices. The procurement policy and procedures manual are approved by the board and clearly outlines the relationship between the board and the approval process.

### Key steps in the procurement process:

- **Planning and Budgeting:** Initiated by user units through annual budgeting and procurement planning.
- **Vendor Management:** Handled by the procurement unit and includes vendor due diligence, prequalification, registration and performance management.
- **Request for Proposals (RFP):** Issued via AGRA’s automated SCM management system. This includes evaluation of proposals/quotations by the Technical Evaluation Panels, negotiations and review of agreements with the requesting units, and approval of the agreements by the board or the board per the approval matrix.

### Contracting:
Involves drafting of contracts, sharing with proposed consultants/vendors and signing of the agreements. This is also done through AGRA’s automated SCM management system.
- **Contract Management:** Includes management of all contracts by the requesting units, vendor payments, and performance management.

## 234. For the implementation of the financial models under Oduro 2.0, escrow accounts will be used to hold funds where conditions have been met in these instances. AGRA will open an account either at the bank of the supplier, at the partner financial institution, or at its own bank (depending on the specific agreement). The account will be held until all conditions are met and the agreement is signed by AGRA; it is a signatory. Depending on the agreements reached, the other parties (suppliers, financiers) will be included as co-signers as needed. Funds can only be released if the trigger in the agreement has materialized.


---

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.01 PAGE 85

#### Governance and Oversight

225. The roles and responsibilities are clearly outlined in the procurement policy and procedures manual. The President is responsible for reviewing and approving the policies. The President appoints the Procurement Committee comprising of nine members including the Chief Risk Officer, Secretary, and Chairperson who are responsible for providing oversight to ensure that the procurement system is fair, transparent, competitive, and cost-effective. Internal Audit Function provides independent review and assurance on all aspects of the procurement process.

#### Procurement Risk Assessment and management

236. AGRA has an elaborate Risk Management Framework that is overseen by the Enterprise Risk Management Unit. AGRA delivers procurement risk as the possibility that an event may impact its ability to achieve its objectives. The objective of the risk management framework for procurement activities involves identifying, assessing, measuring, and managing procurement risks to ensure they remain within acceptable limits. The integrated procurement approach aims to enhance transparency, accountability, and stakeholder confidence in the procurement process. The procurement unit and other units have risk registers that are updated on quarterly basis.

#### Other policies

237. AGRA has several other policies that complement the procurement policy. These include Human Resources Policy, Code of Conduct Policy, The Whistle-blower Policy, Anti-Fraud Policy, and Anti-Money Laundering and Counter Terrorism Financing policy. To gain further insight into our procurement procedures, please refer to the procurement policy document embedded below.

Dear Madam, Sir,

We refer to the programme titled RE-GAIN: Scaling Solutions for Food Loss in Africa under the Project-Specific Assessment Approach ("PSAA") submitted by AGRA to us on 3rd September 2024.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of the Ministry of Green Economy and Environment (MoGE), the National Designated Authority for Zambia. Pursuant to the GCF Board decision B-08/15, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, in my capacity as representative of the National Designated Authority, I hereby communicate our no objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal.

The RE-GAIN Food Loss Reduction Programme offers a comprehensive solution that aligns with Zambia's climate priorities. By minimizing food waste, the programme contributes to mitigating climate change through reduced greenhouse gas emissions. Additionally, it supports adapting to climate change by ensuring food security and promoting resilient agricultural practices.


---
**Page 102**
---

This approach is in line with Zambia’s national climate action plan, which prioritizes sustainable agriculture, food security, and climate resilience. The RE-GAPS Programme's focus on reducing food loss enhances agricultural efficiency, reduces reliance on external inputs, and strengthens local food systems, thereby contributing to these key objectives.

By communicating our no-objection, it is implied that:

(a) The government of the Republic of Zambia has no objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal;

(b) The programme as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with the national priorities and the National Strategy for Economic Development (NSED);

(c) In accordance with the GCF’s environmental and social safeguards, the programme as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.

We also confirm that our national process for ascertaining no-objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.

# Section 1. Secretariat’s assessment of the applicant under the project-specific assessment approach

The document has been assessed against the GCF accreditation standards. The result of the entity capacity assessment and the Secretariat's endorsement and recommendations for accreditation using the project-specific assessment approach (PSA) are as follows:

## Overall capacity
- **High**
  - The entity has a well-developed financial management system; a well-functioning control framework, including risk management;
  - The entity is not involved in money laundering or financing of terrorism and other prohibited purposes.
  - The entity has sufficient scope to manage environmental and social gender risks and impacts with a low likelihood of negative impact on the environment.

## Fiduciary functions
- **Basic fiduciary standard**
  - Specialized fiduciary standard for project management

## Maximum environmental and social risk category
- **Limited adverse risk (category B)**

## Conditions
- N/A

The Secretariat has augmented its capacity by using external experts in undertaking the entity capacity assessment review. A summary is presented below.

### I. Entity capacity assessment summary

#### 1. Introduction and institutional assessment

##### 1.1 Basic information about the project and the entity

AGRA (formerly the Alliance for a Green Revolution in Africa) established in 2006 by the Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation, is an international organization, a pan-African institution headquartered in Nairobi, Kenya. AGRA’s vision is to contribute to a food-system-inspired nations adapted to climate change. AGRA's strategic objectives include empowering and building the resilience of smallholder farmers and supporting the development of inclusive markets for agricultural products. The Secretariat has assessed AGRA against the capacity capability of African countries to sustain agricultural transformation. AGRA collaborates with both public


---
**Page 109**
---

### Methodology

AGRA passed the Climate Investment Committee underwriting step (now known as OC2) on 1 October 2021, which was the first of two material events (the other a financial check) on 17 September 2021 using the combined application form and material request. Two further review rounds took place from September to November 2021. The external review team (ERT) reviewed AGRA’s capacity against the GCF accreditation standards, including its policies, procedures, capacities, track record and human resources against the GCF accreditation standards.

#### Reference check interviews were conducted with AGRA's two existing donor partners: the German Development Bank Kreditanstalt für Wiederaufbau (KfW), which has supported AGRA since 2019. The referees were selected on the basis of their earlier contributions to AGRA’s work and their knowledge of AGRA, its role in the CGIAR for Inclusive Agricultural Transformation in Africa, and their mandate as public government donors. KfW was also asked about any potential conflicts of interest or other capacities of AGRA that had been identified during the long-term donor relationship.

In addition to the desk review, the ERT conducted a site visit to AGRA's headquarters (HQ) in Nairobi, Kenya. The HQ staff were interviewed as part of the desk review process and engaged in interviews and presentations across all areas of the assessment. A small group of HQ staff also participated in a focus group discussion, which provided further evidence of the capacity and procedures within AGRA.

The applicant was assessed in accordance with the GCF policies and standards to the extent applicable.


---
**Page 110**
---

### Contribution to the mandate of the GCF

#### 1.3 Contribution to the mandate of the GCF

* The RE-GAIN funding proposal outlines AGRA's contributions to the GCF focus areas: forests and land; livelihoods of people and communities; and health, food and water security.
* This document provides an overview of how AGRA is contributing to these focus areas by scaling up and replicating innovative practices in the harvest and post-harvest value chain across seven African countries. In this document, we highlight the RE-GAIN funding proposal as a means for AGRA to help farmers reduce food losses through the widespread adoption of food loss-reduction solutions (FL-RS) in the proposed region.
* The document also provides an overview of how AGRA is contributing to the GCF focus areas by scaling up and replicating innovative practices in the harvest and post-harvest value chain across seven African countries. In this document, we highlight the RE-GAIN funding proposal as a means for AGRA to help farmers reduce food losses through the widespread adoption of food loss-reduction solutions (FL-RS) in the proposed region.
* The document also provides an overview of how AGRA is contributing to the GCF focus areas by scaling up and replicating innovative practices in the harvest and post-harvest value chain across seven African countries. In this document, we highlight the RE-GAIN funding proposal as a means for AGRA to help farmers reduce food losses through the widespread adoption of food loss-reduction solutions (FL-RS) in the proposed region.

#### 1.4 Track record and implementation of similar projects

* As evidence of its track record, AGRA referenced four earlier or ongoing interventions that demonstrate its capacity to manage and deliver similar sized programmes in the proposed region.
* The four reference interventions are: the Agripreneurs Programme (USD $20 million), which is implemented by the International Finance Corporation (IFC) and the World Bank; the Agripreneurs Programme (USD $15.6 million); the Strengthening Regenerative Agriculture in Kenya programme (USD $38.4 million); and the Agripreneurs Programme (USD $20 million).
* The four reference interventions are: the Agripreneurs Programme (USD $20 million), which is implemented by the International Finance Corporation (IFC) and the World Bank; the Agripreneurs Programme (USD $15.6 million); the Strengthening Regenerative Agriculture in Kenya programme (USD $38.4 million); and the Agripreneurs Programme (USD $20 million).
* The four reference interventions are: the Agripreneurs Programme (USD $20 million), which is implemented by the International Finance Corporation (IFC) and the World Bank; the Agripreneurs Programme (USD $15.6 million); the Strengthening Regenerative Agriculture in Kenya programme (USD $38.4 million); and the Agripreneurs Programme (USD $20 million).

#### 1.5 Legal status, registration, permits and licenses

* AGRA was registered in 2006 in the United States of America as a non-profit organization under the US Internal Revenue Code (para. 501(c)(3)).


---
**Page 111**
---

19. AGRA has established country offices in 12 African countries and is physically headquartered in Nairobi, Kenya. In Kenya, AGRA operates and is registered with the Registrar of Companies as a branch of the AGRA US registration under section 366 of the Companies Act, Cap 480 of the Laws of Kenya. The country office will be subject to the same financial management as governed by the Kenyan Companies Act. AGRA will be both the accredited entity (AE) as the contractual party with GCF and the executing entity (EE) of the RE-GAIN programme through the programmatic arrangement.
19. AGRA has established legal entities in six countries (Ghana, Kenya, Mali, Mozambique, Rwanda and Tanzania). In these countries, AGRA is responsible for financial management as well as ensuring legal compliance. The country offices are extensions of AGRA and operate under the supervision of AGRA HQ. In countries where AGRA is not registered, administrative operations will be carried out through host country agreements.
16. In addition, several African governments (Burkina Faso, Ghana, Kenya, Malawi, Mali, Rwanda and Tanzania) have granted operational privileges and exemptions through host country agreements.

## II. Accreditation assessment

### 2.1 Basic fiduciary standards

#### 2.3.1 Key administrative and financial capacities

18. AGRA's global board of directors includes members from the Rockefeller Foundation, the Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation and independent directors, supported by board committees dedicated to oversight of strategy, finance, human resources, legal affairs, communications and programme, whose task is to oversee key functions. In addition, AGRA has established five oversight committees: audit committee, remuneration committee, nominations committee, data protection and safeguarding. Procedures for appointment, termination and remuneration are defined.
18. AGRA's organizational structure is documented, outlining responsibilities and reporting lines. The process for setting objectives involves a five-year strategy cycle and annual reviews, with key performance indicators (KPIs) set by the board of directors and reviewed by the programme.
24. Monitoring and reporting mechanisms are detailed in a quarterly management review, with assigned roles and reporting frequencies. External evaluations are conducted at closure of the budget year and every three years thereafter.
19. AGRA's financial statements are prepared as per the United States Generally Accepted Accounting Principles, and include detailed statements of financial position, performance, cash flows, operating results and changes in equity for each fiscal year.

### Specialized fiduciary standards

#### Project management

AGRA has structured its operations around thematic areas or business lines to achieve its mission of transforming agriculture in Africa. Under the purview of the Vice-President – Center of Technical Expertise, AGRA has thematic units aligned with the business lines for policy and state support, research and innovation, value chain development, market access and competitiveness, inclusive markets, trade and finance. Under the purview of the Vice-President - Programs Delivery, AGRA has a number of program delivery offices, as well as units for private sector partnerships, gender, youth and inclusiveness.

AGRA implements projects through a variety of instruments and partners, including grants, loans, equity investments, public-private partnerships, joint ventures with financial institutions, extension service providers and so on. All projects and interventions are aligned with broader strategies and plans at the country level as well as AGRA’s own strategic plan. Primary implementing partners who execute interventions contained in the AGRA strategy. AGRA has a comprehensive project management framework called the MEF (Management, Evaluation and Learning) framework and a corresponding set of policies, procedures, tools and responsibilities to execute the MEF framework.

AGRA has a clear monitoring, evaluation and learning (ME&L) framework and a comprehensive set of policies, procedures, tools and responsibilities to execute the ME&L framework. AGRA’s overall corporate strategy is underpinned by a number of specific theories of change. The Director of Strategy & Monitoring & Evaluation and Learning reports directly to the Vice-President – Center of Technical Expertise. There are regional MEF officers for West Africa and for Southern and East Africa, as well as in-country MEF officers in eight countries.

AGRA has a comprehensive set of policies, procedures, tools and responsibilities to execute the ME&L framework throughout the project cycle. These are fiduciary policies for quality control and oversight mechanisms to monitor budgets and risks in project implementation phase. AGRA has broad grant-programme-wide fiduciary policies and MEF capacity with defined procedures to adhere to established policies and guidelines.

#### Grant award and/or funding allocation mechanisms

AGRA has not applied for accreditation for the specialized fiduciary standard for grant award and/or funding allocation mechanism under the RE-AGRA special grants in the proposed RE-AGRA programme, but the field-level implementation partners will be selected and contracted through a competitive process. The same set of policies, procedures, tools and responsibilities and many of the established grant-making practices and policies will be applied in the RE-AGRA programme.

# Section 2. Secretariat's assessment of FP257

## Proposal name:
RE-GAIN: Scaling solutions for food loss in Africa

## Project-specific assessment approach applicant:
AGRA

## Country/(ies):
Burkina Faso, Ethiopia, Kenya, Malawi, Uganda, United Republic of Tanzania, Zambia

## Project/programme size:
Medium

### I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat
The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remark:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| As the first project targeting food loss mitigation within the GEF portfolio, this initiative holds significant potential for transferability and replication. The adoption of food loss-reduction solutions by engaging multiple stakeholders on the supply side, with a strong focus on marketing enhancement and active private sector participation is expected to be successful. | Potential implementation risks given the complex nature of the programme, including several contexts affected by fragile states and conflict zones. The implementation arrangements, including the legal terms, include adaptive management provisions for mitigating some of these risks. |
| Six out of the seven targeted countries are least developed countries, with limited public and private sector capacity to deliver. Significant numbers of beneficiaries will be reached, considering farmers and micro, small and medium-sized enterprises (MSMEs) will be supported through a multi-country approach. The adoption of food loss-reduction solutions and improved practices is expected. | A potential risk factor for model 2 is the inability of beneficiaries to access finance or credit facilities due to their size, which could lead to a reduced number of beneficiaries. To address this issue, it is recommended that food loss-reduction solutions deployment models that have multiple layers of de-risking, including those that provide private sector and business development support for MSMEs. |

As the first multi-country programme with a direct access entry under the project-specific assessment approach, the GEF Secretariat originates efforts inculcating a culture of learning by leveraging its sectoral expertise, local presence and proven track record - have been deployed in the development of the RE-GAIN initiative.


---
**Page 119**
---

## Summary of the Secretariat's assessment

### Programme background

Many African countries face current droughts that alter growing seasons, often shortening the period for rainfed agriculture and limiting the viability of farming in marginal areas. Projections indicate that crop yields in some countries could decline by up to 50 per cent by 2030, and net revenues from crops may fall as much as 90 per cent by 2100. An additional challenge is the increasing competition for land use between agriculture and other uses such as urbanization or infrastructure development. This will not only reduce crop production but also through changes to the crop value chain, including post-harvest losses and food security.

To address these challenges, the programme aims to build the climate resilience and adaptive capacity of smallholder farmers by promoting the wide-scale adoption of FL-RS, and thereby increasing their income and food security. The programme will increase the ability of, and enabling environment support for wide-scale adoption of these solutions. These activities are aligned with the priorities set out in the Green Climate Fund (GCF) 2014–2027: Target 9 (new direct access entities target), Target 4 (food systems), Target 9 (adaptation and capacity building), and Target 10 (local private sector early-stage ventures and SME innovation).

The total financing for this proposed programme is USD 105 million with the total GCF funding amount of USD 83.27 million, which will be provided through a grant co-financed by AGRA. The project is rated as ESS category B. The programme will directly benefit 2.6 million vulnerable smallholder farmers in seven countries (representing approximately 10 per cent or the regional population) across seven countries: Burkina Faso, Ethiopia, Kenya, Malawi, Uganda, United Republic of Tanzania and Zambia.

The project is a large-scale cross-country initiative in the African region, supporting a direct access entity (regional). The PSAA applicant, AGRA, serving as both the AE and the RE, has extensive experience in agricultural development with over 20 years of professional. AGRA's experience and proven track record make it well positioned to lead this large-scale PSAA initiative, with its extensive network including partnerships with governments, civil society organizations, and civil society organizations in the agriculture sector.

This funding proposal was developed with support provided by the Project Preparation Facility.


### Component-by-component analysis

**Component 1: Increased adoption of FL-RS by smallholder farmers (total cost: USD 43.1 million; GCF cost: USD 22.73 million)**

- The programme incorporates several technical strategies, particularly in its comprehensive approach to increasing the adoption of FL-RS by smallholder farmers. By integrating multiple strategies such as awareness raising, training, technology transfer and market linkages, the programme effectively addresses the demand side of FL-RS adoption.


---
**Page 120**
---

## Outcome 1: Strengthening the programme’s potential impact

The extensive engagement strategy further strengthens the programme’s potential impact. By leveraging village-based partners and local institutions, the project aims to reach over a million smallholder farmers, ensuring widespread awareness and participation across seven countries.

### Component A: Enhanced outreach and market access

- **Outcome 1a**: The programme leverages its market-oriented approach by structuring agriculture value chains and fostering linkages between smallholder farmers and cooperatives, MSEs and potential buyers. This ensures that the adoption of FL-RS is not only a technical solution but also a commercial one.

### Component B: Enhanced supply and affordability of FL-RS

- **Outcome 1b**: The programme leverages a holistic approach to overcoming supply-side barriers to the adoption of FL-RS by introducing innovative financial mechanisms. Through a dual approach that combines capacity building for smallholder farmers, cooperatives, MSEs, and other stakeholders with the development of local value chains, the programme enhances the capacity of local MSEs.

### Component C: Strengthened enabling environment

- **Outcome 1c**: The programme strengthens its enabling environment by providing targeted training on operations and maintenance. This ensures that smallholder farmers are equipped to effectively use FL-RS while also creating a sustainable system that extends the programme’s benefits beyond its five-year duration.


---
**Page 121**
---

### III. Assessment against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential
**Scale: High**

The proposed programme delivers transformative adaptation benefits by addressing the vulnerabilities of smallholder farmers and communities across seven African countries. By reducing food losses exacerbated by climate change, the programme strengthens food security and enhances resilience to future shocks for vulnerable groups including women and MSMEs, empowering them through capacity-building, market development and social cohesion.

These efforts directly support the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and indirectly support national adaptation objectives at both the regional and the national level.

The programme also complements traditional agricultural adaptation strategies by addressing climate change impacts on food security. By preserving harvest crops, it alleviates pressure on land and water resources, promoting environmental sustainability while mitigating the adverse effects of climate change.

Through catalytic grants and partnerships with the private sector, the programme creates a foundation for sustainable markets and attracts additional investments, ensuring benefits are sustained over time. This approach amplifies its impact, positioning it as a model for regional and global food security initiatives.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential
**Scale: High**

By leveraging CGI grants, the programme catalyses a paradigm shift in post-harvest development pathways through market stimulation, private sector engagement and scalable FL-RS adoption. It promotes climate-resilient agricultural practices that build resilient agricultural ecosystems by empowering smallholder farmers with accessible, climate-smart food-loss reduction systems. It integrates multi-stakeholder engagement and processes fast-time delivery of climate-smart solutions to ensure long-term sustainability.

These efforts create robust linkages between farmers, markets and financing mechanisms.

The programme's design means there is potential for significant scaling and replication. Strengthening local supply chains, fostering private sector innovations and embedding successful models in national policies can be achieved through partnerships with regional climate institutions amplifying its impact, facilitating adaptation across Africa. Complementing CGI’s efforts to scale up FL-RS adoption, the programme supports innovative financial models to influence broader strategies.


---
**Page 122**
---

### Sustainable development potential

**Scale: High**

- The proposed programme extends its impact far beyond reducing food losses by delivering a range of co-benefits that advance global goals (SDGs 2, 5, 8, 13), environmental conservation and socioeconomic well-being. Its integrated design fosters systemic change at the local, national and international levels.
- The programme generates substantial environmental co-benefits by reducing the need for crop protection chemicals, which are major drivers of deforestation, soil erosion, desertification, land degradation and protects ecosystems. Efficient agricultural practices decrease dependency on fertilizers and pesticides, reducing the agricultural environmental footprint. In addition, monitoring and reporting systems will be established to track progress towards global climate goals.
- The programme also contributes significantly to food security and safety by reducing contamination risks, improving nutrition and lowering food-borne illnesses, which positively impacts public health systems. It generates employment through FL-SR value chains, creating opportunities for women and youth in rural areas. The programme will reduce the price volatility of agricultural produce, benefiting both producers and consumers, while providing more predictable income and affordable food.
- Designed with gender sensitivity at its core, the programme ensures women's equal participation and leadership, fostering equity and leveraging their pivotal role in agriculture. Inclusive value chains and market access strategies ensure growth integration ensures equitable benefits, strengthening social and economic resilience.

### Needs of the recipient

**Scale: Medium to high**

The target countries rely heavily on agriculture, a sector that employs 40-80 per cent of their populations and contributes 20–40 per cent of their gross domestic product. However, climate change is exacerbating existing challenges in this sector, including reduced crop productivity, economic instability, exacerbated by low adaptive capacities and limited institutional readiness. The programme will address these challenges through a range of interventions to reverse food losses and build resilience in post-harvest systems crucial yet underutilized data on the agricultural value chain.
- Despite significant investments in improving production systems, post-harvest processes remain neglected, leaving smallholder farmers – the backbone of these economies –


---
**Page 123**
---

particularly vulnerable to losses from spoilage, contamination and inefficiencies. RE-GAIN addresses these gaps by introducing FL-RS and improving storage, processing and market access.

* The programme is uniquely tailored to local contexts, working with community-level suppliers, agro-dealers and MSMEs to integrate FL-RS sustainably into existing systems. This localized approach ensures that the programme responds effectively to the financial and institutional constraints of national governments, whose limited resources are often concentrated in urban areas.

* A core strength of RE-GAIN lies in its focus on empowering vulnerable populations, particularly smallholder farmers, women and youth. These groups face significant barriers to adopting FL-RS technologies due to lack of access to inputs, credit, extension services and market opportunities. The programme addresses these barriers by providing affordable FL-RS, building market infrastructure and creating targeted business development opportunities for marginalized groups. This inclusive approach promotes equitable development and enhances community cohesion.

### 3.5 Country ownership
Scale: High

* The RE-GAIN programme exemplifies a model of country-driven, context-specific development, reflecting a strong commitment to aligning interventions with national priorities while fostering local ownership. Through close collaboration with national governments, regulatory authorities, ministries and stakeholders, AGRA has developed project concepts and solutions that address the unique challenges faced by each country. This approach involves extensive bilateral government engagements, stakeholder workshops and iterative feedback loops. As a result, the programme ensures that interventions are tailored to specific contexts, addressing issues such as value chain development, MSME support, financial systems and food security. A participatory design process involving key stakeholders at all levels—farmers, agro-dealers, processors, traders, communities, governments and non-governmental entities identified critical gaps and shaped interventions that directly address the realities of smallholder farmers. This process continues throughout project implementation to ensure alignment with evolving local contexts.

* The programme also leverages AGRA's expertise and its established presence in the target countries. Drawing from five-year country strategies, comprehensive gap analyses and strategic partnerships, the programme builds on AGRA’s foundation to deliver systemic change. Collaborative efforts between national governments, regulatory authorities, private sector actors and development organizations ensure that interventions are sustainable over time.

### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness
Scale: Medium

* The financial structure exemplifies a strategic and innovative approach to addressing the barriers faced by smallholder farmers in adopting FL-RS while ensuring long-term market sustainability. By integrating catalytic grants, concessional funding and capacity-building initiatives, the programme creates a robust framework that addresses critical challenges on both the supply and the demand side of the agricultural value chain.

# Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

## Environmental and social safeguards

### ESS 1: Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts

ESS 1 has adopted an ESM that includes policies, procedures, tools, and guidance for any activities or interventions it will finance. Therefore, the assessment of ESS risks is a requirement for all projects and grantees in each country. The broad risks identified comprise contextual risks (conflict and child labour), biodiversity, air emissions, water management, road safety), Indigenous Peoples-related risk, soil conservation and management; water management; pesticide management; biodiversity; air emissions; occupational health and safety (including child labour) and general environmental and social risks.

### ESS 2: Labour and working conditions

ESS 2 has broadly identified the potential labour risks for all projects and grantees in each country. These include child labour and forced labour, and occupational health and safety. Considering the programme will be implemented in various countries, a country-level analysis on child labour in the agricultural sector should be conducted.

### ESS 3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention

ESS 3 details the procedures and tools that will be used to assess interventions, as well as specific pollution risks for grantees during implementation. It further provides guidance on how to integrate resource efficiency and pollution prevention into environmental and social management plans to address pollution and promote sustainable use of resources.

### ESS 4: Community health, safety and security

ESS has identified potential community health and safety risks, including effects on ecosystem services and risk of exposure to pesticides and other chemicals. It provides guidance for developing comprehensive environmental and social management plans to address community health and safety issues.


---
**Page 125**
---

### ESS 5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement

AGRA has stated that the GCF-funded projects will not be associated with any land acquisitions. This should be part of the Exclusion List, stating that no programme activities will result in land acquisition.

#### ESMS

The ESMS provides a detailed environmental and social management plan which will provide additional details on potential risks/impact and mitigation measures on the programme.

#### ESS 6: Biodiversity conservation and GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy

AGRA undertook an assessment on Indigenous Peoples and concluded that the project interventions in five of the seven countries where it operates are likely to result in displacement, loss of access to land or natural resources, or alter the physical or social context of the local areas where Indigenous People live. The ESMS includes due diligence and resettlement measures for these activities.

#### ESS 8: Cultural heritage

AGRA has provided due diligence and screening procedures for identifying impacts on cultural heritage and determining whether risk management measures are required to mitigate any adverse effects resulting from project implementation.

#### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESS 7 on Indigenous Peoples in the projects

There are pastoral communities that may be affected by the project activities. The ESMS has provided an Indigenous People plan framework that it has adopted for compliance with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Project. Project exclusion ensures that no activity results in the infringement of the rights of Indigenous Peoples and that free, prior, and informed consent is secured for the activities that require it; and the activities exclude land acquisition and resettlement measures.

#### Sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH)

AGRA has zero tolerance of SEAH which is covered in the AGRA human resources and administration policy. In ethics policy and procedures, there are specific guidelines on how to identify potential SEAH risks for the programme such as incidents involving staff or programme partners. The ESMS includes a risk management plan that provides mitigation measures for SEAH risks. Mitigation measures to manage these risks include training on the AGRA safeguarding framework and programme-specific policies regarding awareness training on SEAH and gender-based violence awareness, mitigating SEAH risk engagement into staff performance reviews and programme planning; and SEAH awareness sessions in sub-programme meetings. The ESMS also includes a plan for regular inter-departmental dialogues to address power dynamics and promote positive masculinity as well as including more balanced recruitment practices and providing support services to survivors of SEAH. Furthermore, the applicant will ensure that access-to-grievance mechanisms for SEAH are in place prior to mobilization. The application includes safeguarding officers at the country level for undertaking SEAH assessments and training.

### Gender policy

The AGRA provides a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Updated Gender Policy.

### Risks

#### 4.3 Risks

##### 4.3.1 Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

###### 4.3.1.1 GCF is providing a grant of USD 75.62 million to build smallholder farmers' climate resilience and increase their adoption of climate-smart agriculture practices.

The programme is co-financed with USD 90.38 million from AGRA, leveraging a total project cost of USD 105 million. This programme covers seven countries in the Africa region. This is the first multi-country GCF-supported project in this region.

###### 4.3.1.2 Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme (medium risk)

The PSA applicant, AGRA, is carrying out the AFE/ER role for this project. AGRA has an established track record in implementing agriculture projects in the region as well as extensive partnerships with governments, local agribusinesses and civil society organizations.

##### 4.3.2 Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)

The proposed procurement models (model 1 and 2) under component 2 have an innovative nature while retaining certain risks.

##### 4.3.3 Misalignment risk:


---
**Page 127**
---

There is a misalignment in the timing of the distribution of economic benefits between the local smallholder farmers (e.g., manufacturer, agro-inputs) and smallholder farmers. Service providers benefit from the grant immediately upon the completed agreement for the sale of agricultural inputs or equipment. Smallholder farmers may not receive their share until they have access to the equipment or service provided using the equipment. This disparity creates a risk that GCF concessionary rates may be eroded owing to:

(Model 1) Inadequate market systems on pricing: The memorandum of understanding to be signed between AGRA and the supplier lacks mechanisms to monitor the resale value of the equipment, which is critical for ensuring transparency. This sense of oversight may dilute GCF concessionality and result in farmers not benefiting from the intended affordability.

(Model 2) Youth groups managing the equipment as a business venture may default if they lack sufficient capacity or face economic challenges;

(Model 2) Premature exit risk: Youth groups may choose to exit the business prematurely, resulting in the loss of equipment and/or services that are fully realized by farmers; and

(Model 2) Market risks: The manufacturer that provides the repurchase insurance may not cover the repurchase value if it does not cover the total outstanding loan, for example, if the resale value has depreciated due to the market situation.

To mitigate these risks, the project should incorporate:

A provision in the memorandum of understanding with the relevant smallholder farmers by specifying the monitoring/spot check/reporting mechanisms in models 1 and 2;

Incentives for youth groups to prioritize long-term capital creation over short-term economic gains;

Mechanisms ensuring the accessibility and affordability of equipment for local farmers; and

Repayment provisions for the residual value of the grant if the business is terminated early, such as second lien pledges on equipment or negative covenants to restrict funds to similar initiatives; and

An agreement with financial institutions and suppliers agree on clear depreciation terms for the repurchase value and the risk-sharing ratio and the establishment of regular maintenance requirements to preserve equipment value.

### Co-financing risk (medium)

The co-financing commitment from AGRA amounts to USD 29.3 million, representing 27.5% per cent of the project cost. While AGRA's track record and capacity provide confidence in its ability to deliver, delays or shortfalls in co-financing disbursement could affect the timely implementation of activities and reduce the overall impact.

### Compliance risk (low)

The programme’s proposed activities are associated with a relatively low inherent risk of money laundering/terrorist financing (ML/TF).

The PSAA applicant has assessed ML/TF and other prohibited practices risks as low-medium in line with the GCF’s standardised risk matrix assessment, which indicated the likelihood to be rare and the potential impact to be major. Mitigating measures include grantee screening and pre-boarding due diligence as part of organisational capacity


---
**Page 128**
---

### 4.4 Fiduciary

125. The RE-GAIN programme will be implemented by AGRA both as the AE and the EE. AGRA will leverage its extensive network of country offices and partnerships across seven African countries to ensure effective implementation. The financial management and compliance unit within AGRA will oversee programme activities, financial management and compliance with GCF fiduciary requirements. This unit will provide technical assistance for budget preparation; supervise programme monitoring, evaluation and reporting; ensure timely realisation of all payments; monitor the performance of country offices and partners in implementing programmes; and ensure smooth communication flow across all programme partners. This executing role will be fulfilled by AGRA’s Nairobi-based HQ and AGRA’s country offices, and the EE will report to the AGRA senior leadership team.

126. A central programme steering committee, co-chaired by AGRA and representatives of the national governments in each implementing country, will oversee programme progress and project performance annually. The committee will ensure that programme activities are consistent with GCF objectives and national development goals, reinforcing accountability and governance mechanisms.

127. AGRA will establish a robust financial management framework, utilising its internal financial management system to ensure accurate reporting of all expenditures. This system will be maintained for accurate financial reporting. AGRA’s financial management system aligns with international standards, ensuring compliance with GCF fiduciary requirements.

### 4.5 Results monitoring and reporting

109. As detailed in the theory of change, three interconnected programme outcomes will contribute to achieving longer-term adaptation outcomes for food and nutrition security and livelihoods resilience. Improved access to climate information services is a co-benefit. The theory of change has appropriately articulated the goal statement to focus on the adoption of improved agricultural technologies, improving the resilience of farmers, RMES and rural communities to climate shocks.

110. The programme’s logical framework follows the GCF Integrated Results Management Framework (IRMF) for reporting against the three main outputs. Each output is linked to a specific UCF result area: livelihoods of people and communities; and health, food and water security. The enabling conditions are linked to the UCF result areas and will be measured in accordance with the requirements and allow for comprehensive results monitoring. The project-specific indicators will be used as part of the programme’s performance measurement framework to ensure performance is adequately measured. The measurement of a few of the programme-specific indicators will be informed by the baseline survey to be undertaken by the AE at the inception stage.

112. The monitoring and evaluation of the programme will leverage the AE's existing M&E framework, which has been developed in line with the GCF Evaluation Policy. As part of the funding proposal package describes how the measurement and reporting against the logical framework will be conducted. The implementation monitoring will be undertaken at the country level by the AE and will include a mid-term review and an end-of-programme performance report in accordance with the GCF Monitoring and Accountability Framework. The programme will also undergo an independent final evaluation through independent evaluators in line with the GCF Evaluation Policy.

### 4.6 Legal assessment

113. This funding proposal is being presented under the PSFA if the funding proposal is approved by the Board, the GCF Secretariat and the applicant will negotiate a funded activity agreement (FAA) with the AE.

114. The applicant has provided a legal certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the programme.

115. The proposed programme will be implemented in seven host countries. The GCF has signed bilateral agreements with six of these countries, namely Burkina Faso and Zambia. In the case of the remaining five countries, namely Ethiopia, Kenya, Malawi, Uganda and United Republic of Tanzania, GCF is not provided with privileges

and immunities. This means that, among other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in these countries, which risks need to be further assessed. Moreover, the ability of GCF to undertake redress activities and/or investigations in such countries may be hindered due to the lack of legal capacity.

Therefore, it is recommended that the Board consider whether disbursements of GCF proceeds should be made only after GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in these countries. ## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

1. This funding proposal for a medium-sized public sector adaptation programme in environmental and social safeguards category B is submitted by AGRA through the GCF project-specific assessment approach (PSA). The RE-GAIN programme will operate over five years in seven African countries: Burkina Faso, Ethiopia, Kenya, Malawi, Uganda, United Republic of Tanzania, Zambia (see figure 1). AGRA, a continental institution with a strong presence across these countries, has been identified as the implementing entity for the programme, which will be executed through the AGRA Programme Operational Unit and utilising the existing institutional arrangements and capacities of the host country, allowing close working relationships with governments, partners and other stakeholders.

## Impact potential

Scale: High

### 1.1 Impact potential

The programme will target two of the GCF results areas for adaptation, aiming to enhance the resilience of (a) the most vulnerable people and communities and (b) health and well-being, and food and water security, in the seven participating countries.


---
**Page 134**
---

1. Climate change: The funding proposal highlights the vulnerability of all seven countries to climate change, with negative impacts experienced and anticipated—not only on crop production and yields but also on volumes of crops that remain of sufficient quality for consumption or sale. Data from the World Bank Climate Change Knowledge Portal, with temperature indicators (such as annual mean temperatures) increasing at a rate of 0.15°C per decade since 1960, shows an increasing trend over time, and the rate of increase accelerating in recent decades. Historical rainfall trends display greater variability, and for some countries a measure of this is the number of consecutive dry days, which has increased significantly. The frequency of intense rainfall events is not shown as a key climate variable.

2. Impact on agriculture: The proposal notes that projections indicate future increases in temperature (such as annual mean precipitation), the number of consecutive wet days, and—as a corollary—the number of consecutive dry days suggest a projected rise in rainfall intensity and frequency for most countries. For example, data from Zambia, where projections display a slight drying signal consistent with regional trends for southern Africa, shows that climate change is expected to increase temperatures, as are increasing heat extremes. AGRA acknowledges that the impact of climate change on post-harvest losses has not been well studied in the region and that there is limited evidence to suggest that [it] the range of climate hazards that affect harvest and post-harvest value chains of smallholder farmers is likely to increase, with a greater frequency of variable, erratic, and unpredictable rainfall (including an increase in heavy rainfall events), high temperatures (extreme heat), and flooding as the priority hazards of concern.

3. Adaptation strategies: The proposal outlines adaptation strategies that will provide benefits for vulnerable smallholder farmers and their households across the seven countries through reducing climate-related risks to food security and livelihoods, with a particular focus on resilience to climate change by improving management at harvest and post-harvest stages, maintaining higher levels of food availability for consumption and sale than would be the case without the GCF adaptation strategy. The proposed strategies will also contribute significantly to the broader adaptation strategies for agricultural resilience within each target country, which tends to be a key component in national climate change action plans (NDCs) or other members of state, aiming to increase the resilience of rural livelihoods through investing in developing and scaling up climate-resilient agricultural practices, technologies, and MSMEs, leveraging partnerships with financial institutions and private sector actors to mobilize significant additional resources.

4. Through these results, the programme aims to directly benefit over 2.6 million people and indirectly benefit a further 2.7 million people distributed across the seven target countries as shown in Table 1 (see supplementary annex I: to the funding proposal). The 35 priority regions, shown in detail in map 1 of this document, are those that have been identified by each country as most vulnerable to climate hazards. Smallholder farmers adopting new climate-resilient practices with FL-RS will be direct beneficiaries, experiencing improved food and nutrition security as well as increased food quality and quantity. Farmers who adopt the technology will also experience improved livelihoods as a result of increased business through the programme subsidy and access to markets for their produce. Farmers in the same regions who have access to affordable and good-quality grains and legumes will improve as a result of the programme, with smallholder farmers acting as buyers and processors, who will benefit from improved market linkages.

5. At GCF outcome level (see funding proposal annex II:), the programme will measure the number of climate-resilient households of farmers with FL-RS technology that strengthens climate change resilience, the number of people with new climate-resilient livelihood options, and the number of vulnerable farm households with improved food security.


---
**Page 135**
---

using farmer surveys exploring the Food Insecurity Experience Scale. Since the innovations and livelihood options in question are predefined and relate to specific F-RI-S, these key indicators should deliver credible tracking of results. Although food losses before and after programme implementation will be monitored, the primary focus for measuring the impact of producing meeting quality standards will measure the percentage of grain rejected due to quality concerns at the time of harvest and during storage. This is a critical indicator in this context.

Overall, it can be anticipated that food loss reductions will be significant, at least during the programme implementation period (i.e., while the subsidies are in operation), improving resilience against future shocks through increased crop sales and enhanced business incomes along the value chains for technical solutions.


## 1.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium

Paradigm shift relates to the degree to which the proposed programme can successfully catalyse impact beyond the F-RI one-off investment of USD 754 million over five years, together with the USD 300 million in co-financing from other donors. The GEF and its partners have gone into designing the programme in a manner that intends to elicit a paradigm shift in the harvest and storage practices of smallholder farmers across the region, particularly continentally in Africa. A clear focus on achieving scale and sustainability is embedded in the programme design. However, it should be noted that there are several potential barriers to scaling up F-RI-S and through the work with governments on country-level enabling environments. The GEF has already identified these barriers and will address them, relying as it does on a large subsidy that will be entirely withdrawn at programme end, does not seem optimal for achieving sustained demand or long-term market transformation, as discussed in section 1.3.


### Enabling environment for scaling up Component 3 of the programme seeks to create an enabling environment for scaling up F-RI-S by working with governments and other stakeholders on new policies, such as stimulating tax exemptions, subsidies or reduced interest rates for F-RI investments. RE-GAIN will provide technical assistance to local and national government entities in developing and implementing policies that support the adoption of F-RI-S practices, and support on tools, guidelines and monitoring. RE-GAIN will also work with governments and other decision-makers to ensure that F-RI-S are included in relevant agricultural infrastructure, including storage facilities, processing units and cold chains, as well as helping regulate markets for F-RI-S products and services. Support will also be provided to enhance the capacities of extension services and other stakeholders to provide technical assistance on climate conditions and the impact of F-RI-S in reducing loss. Although the RE-GAIN investments aim to prolong grain shelf life in the absence of good rural road access and renewable-powered cooling systems, they are not intended as a substitute for such infrastructure, which would enable complementarity in this regard.


### Impact on international markets A welcome innovation is the focus on building smallholder farmers' market access by linking them to institutional markets for staple crops – such as schools, universities, armies, hospitals and prisons – as well as to technical and financial partners. This will be achieved through a combination of direct sales, long-term or short-term purchase agreements with farmer cooperatives or producer groups, and, where no such organizations exist, by using aggregators. Furthermore, the signed purchase agreements can be


---
**Page 136**
---

used as necessary to obtain credit from financial institutions.* Technical assistance includes support by village-based advisers to wheat flour millers, enabling them to avoid unnecessary food loss from overprocessing to refined flour, and instead to switch to producing healthier wholegrain flour. The GCF also supports the development of a market for FLS in international markets through government procurement processes and work with partners such as HarvestPlus, the Partnership for Child Development, Save the Children and the World Food Programme.

### Sharing lessons beyond the target countries. Evidence generated and lessons learned in FLS projects can be shared more broadly to inform policy and practice. Activity 3.14 has set aside a network of regional experts who will share their experiences with other regions and countries through extensive networks of AGRA. AGRA will facilitate collaboration with continental and regional bodies and national governments, including African region climate institutions and regional economic communities. The programme will also engage with the private sector, civil society, government officials, farmers from non-programme areas, and development partners to share lessons learned on how to scale up FLS production and consumption in a sustainable way.

Exit strategy for subsidies: The funding proposal states: "Long-term sustainability is at the core of the programme design, with GCF investments seen as catalysts of change within local systems. This means that while the programme will provide support through creation of an enabling environment to shift away from the smart-subsidies, catalyzing further investment in FLS production and consumption, it does not envisage a complete exit strategy; however, there will be nothing in place to manage this transition away from the subsidy. Prices are likely to fall as the programme ends, and the market may not be able to absorb the fall-off in purchasing and thus lower adoption rates for the FLRS. The GCF wonders whether a subsidy that was smaller, more gradually phased out, or both, would be less disruptive, and easily could be scaled up again if needed (e.g., as part of a broader agricultural stimulus stimulation). AGRA made no mention of other similar price subsidy programmes. Arguably, support for the market is better achieved by providing a small subsidy that is designed to stimulate long-term sustainable production of inputs, rather than providing a one-time subsidy of inputs.

Local manufacturing not prioritized: The programme does not prioritize direct support to local manufacturing of FLSR, which could have been an alternative focus for subsidizing the production and consumption of FLS. Although business development support is part of the programme (Activity 2.1.2), this consists of training workshops on how to use the equipment, how to maintain and repair machinery and operate it as a fee-for-service basis. Unlike some of the other AGRA programmes cited in the response to the ITAP questions, the GCF programme does not include any such direct support to local manufacturing.

The response to a question from ITAP about import substitution was that while "AGRA fully agrees with the importance of import substitution as part of a broader strategy for reducing the impact of the programme...for the first years of programme implementation, RE-GAIN will prioritize domestic production and consumption of FLSR. This is in line with the overall programme delivery and to stimulate and prime the market for local manufacturing capacity."


---
**Page 137**
---

The ITAP notes that AGRA has completed a mapping of local suppliers and manufacturers of the technologies (15 to one-up sequels to annexes 2 of the funding proposal) and that the programme "will process as much domestic input as possible" where this is "comparably priced for the same quality as imported inputs." The ITAP believes that a more proactive approach could have gone further to promote market transformation.

Taking all these factors into account, paradigm shift potential is rated as medium.


### Sustainable development potential

#### Scale: Medium to high

The programme is expected to have a number of social, economic and environmental co-benefits. Socioeconomic and health or benefits. In targeting highly vulnerable smallholder farming communities, the proposed programme will yield multiple socioeconomic co-benefits. Reducing post-harvest losses through improved storage facilities and processing technologies will help to protect both producers and consumers from price volatility. Promoting post-harvest processing of produce will also increase the shelf life of perishable goods, thereby enhancing their nutritional value and reducing the risk of food-related illnesses resulting from consumption of spoiled or contaminated produce. In addition to improving access for smallholder individuals but also a more stable and safe food supply will reduce public health costs. Working with local suppliers and agrodealers, and supporting business development of MSMEs as service providers in post-harvest processing will create new jobs and income opportunities that can extend beyond the initial investment, with the intention for these opportunities to be of particular benefit to women.

Gender and youth or benefits. On the customer side, the fee-for-service business model of the programme has significant potential for breaking down barriers to access by women farmers and young people in rural communities. Women are often responsible for family chores, including female-headed households, to rent equipment, labour services, or both, that they would otherwise be unable to afford. The programme will also build on lessons learned from similar programmes, where AGRA has tested and proven the deployment of "Uber for tractors" digital solutions in post-harvest processing. In this model, customers submit their service requests are anonymous, and the service provider deploys the equipment on a first-come, first-served basis.

On the operator side, although AGRA hopes to prioritize women- and youth-led enterprises in supporting 2000 MSMEs and cooperatives to take loans and rent fee-for-service business models, it is unlikely that there will be sufficient demand for such services. This is noted in annexes 2 of the funding proposal. The qualifying groups must have a registration certificate and constitute at least one-third women or youth members. Given the nature of post-harvest aggregation of produce, selling of inputs or provision of mechanized services), and preferably qualified staff or members that have experience in operating, repairing and servicing the machinery, it is unlikely that there will be sufficient demand for such services. Consequently, these are commonly seen as men's areas of business; it seems unlikely that there will be many women-led enterprises to qualify under this criterion.

The funding proposal does not suggest any small steps towards engagement of women's cooperatives in post-harvest processing and mechanized services. It is recommended that the machinery clean and used. Such options could be explored through Activity 1.1.3 (on training for complementary services).


---
**Page 138**
---

20. On youth groups, however, AGRA has suggested an innovative sub-model which takes into consideration that the eligibility criteria will in practice likely also involve more youth groups (despite the characteristic of the target MSMEs and cooperatives in the funding proposal). The proposed model is to establish a formal youth group as a separate entity from MSMEs to partner with local youth groups. The established MSME will take the lead in entering into the main state-level agreement, while the youth group will be responsible for monitoring and ensuring accountability for the management of the agreement. The MSME will then recruit and work with an informal youth group from the community to operate the enterprise, sharing the income and its profits equally between them. This model is currently being piloted in Kenya. The informal youth groups will have the opportunity to build a track record, demonstrating their governance capacity and financial management skills.

21. Environmental co-benefits. The RE-GUN programme interventions to reduce post-harvest food loss will have some environmental co-benefits. Reducing post-harvest food loss will increase the availability of food for consumption in rural areas, which can help improve water use and preventing their wasteful use in producing food that is never eaten. The need to compensate for this reduction in food production by increasing land area under cultivation as a major environmental co-benefit - avoiding land degradation and biodiversity loss (also used in estimating the mitigation potential of the programme) - is not likely to be significant, since improved grain quality might in fact cause some farmers to expand the land under production to take advantage of higher yields. This will also help reduce pressure on forests from deforestation. So this co-benefit is not given, and there is nothing in place to test these assumptions. The reduction in food loss through better storage and handling of perishable foodstuffs "will" reduce the environmental impact of fertilizers and pesticides, and related energy demands. A clear benefit is the reduction in air pollutants from burning of waste food, and the associated health benefits.

21. Mitigation co-benefit: GHG emission reductions, as a co-benefit of the programme, will be achieved through three pathways (with the emphasis on the first two in funding proposal section 03): (a) reduction of emissions from land use change; (b) reduction of emissions from food produced for domestic consumption and exported; and (c) reduction of emissions from wasted food left to rot, causing methane emissions. The programme interventions will reduce post-harvest losses by 25%, which result in a total emission reduction of 111,275 tCO2e (tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent) over the programme period. This is calculated using the methodology described in section 03 and the breakdown in table 9 of the funding proposal. Given the doubt raised in the previous paragraph about the accuracy of assumptions (a) and (b), it is positive that this is being considered as a co-benefit only.

21. Mitigation methodology: The ITAP asked why AGRA chose not to utilize the most obvious method for estimating GHG emissions from food loss, which is the methodology for Reducing Food Loss and Waste, choosing instead to base the estimations on a single publication, and queried whether the resultant estimates could be considered as initially conservative. In a written response, AGRA explained that the methodology used in the ITAP report can only be effective in data-rich environments, which are not present in the programme context (see section 03 of funding proposal). The ITAP also noted that there was a big challenge in 14 value chains (two crops in each of the seven countries). The response explained: "In light of these challenges, the ITAP team worked with AGRA to develop and tested an alternative approach that would make optimal use of the data available. The calculations are considered conservative in that they: (i) were focused only on those portions


---
**Page 139**
---

The crop types of interest to the programme (i) did not consider additional emissions (such as those related to transport or storage/packing materials); (ii) made use of estimates for food loss published by studies that represent the average rates of loss, rather than very low or zero levels of loss; and (iii) assumed a 10% reduction in GHG emissions at a global level study level.
25 In the view of the ITAP, having the anticipated GHG emission reductions as a co-benefit justifies using a simplified approach to calculating their anticipated values. However, using average figures and not estimates at the lower end, and using only one literature source, is not deemed appropriate for this programme. The use of average figures in place of the proposed programme’s anticipated mitigation results could overstate the actual impact that can be achieved.
