### 1.4 Needs of the recipient
Scale: High

* **RE-GHN will primarily serve developing countries, six of which are classified as least developed countries (LDCs), all of which are recognized as being highly vulnerable to climate change.** Of particular concern is the highly affected agriculture sector, which employs between 60 and 80% of the population in these countries. The seven target countries produce a large share of global domestic product across the target countries. In addition to their sensitivity to climate impacts, all seven of these target countries are recognized as having low adaptive capacity and low resilience to climate change. These countries also have limited access to international markets due to high levels of sovereign indebtedness, and need grant funding to help address the challenges faced by farmers in these countries. Farmers in these countries are frequently smallholders and vulnerable to negative impacts of climate change.
* **Climate change-driven (or -exacerbated) food security and continued loss of post-production productivity is expected to reduce agricultural activity and yield losses already being experienced by these countries during cultivation, as a result of the changing climate.** This results in reduced farm income, increased vulnerability to poverty, higher temperatures, higher air moisture levels, more frequent or intense heavy rainfall events, and more frequent droughts. These factors are likely to lead to lower crop yields and output that farmers have left to sell, depress incomes and jeopardize smallholder livelihoods, perpetuating the cycle of poverty and food insecurity in these countries for years on end or persisting seasons. Farmers experiencing increasing losses during harvesting, field-drying and on-farm storage may choose to keep less of their reduced output for domestic consumption, worsening food security and leading to increased vulnerability to climate change and levels of undernourishment[1]. Of these reduce adaptive capacity and must be ameliorated to ensure the resilience of smallholder farmers in the seven countries.


1) According to the Bonn Dance Global Adaptation Initiative (GD-GAIN) Index for 2021, where “adaptive capacity” is the ability of society and its supporting sectors to adjust to reduce potential damage and to respond to the negative consequences of climate change, the seven target countries are among those with the lowest adaptive capacity for adaptation.

The responses in the ITAP question about whether these losses in the target countries have been quantified, the PSSA entity replied (in responses received by the ITAP on 18 January 2023 from AGRA to questions it received from the STAP on 4 December 2022) that the seven target countries are among those with the lowest adaptive capacity for adaptation. The current or projected impact of climate change on these countries is expected to lead to increased food storage and loss in post-production, which will further exacerbate poverty levels and undermine the body of knowledge required to build resilience against climate change. For example in Chapter 5 on Food Security of the 2019 IPCC Special Report on Climate Change and Land Use (https://www.ipcc.ch/report/ar6/wg3/-/IPCC_AR6_WG3_Chapter_5.pdf).

2) See funding proposal paragraph 27

10. Given the high levels of poverty, weak markets and low adaptive capacity experienced in smallholder farming communities, farmers are frequently unable to adopt climate-resilient FL-RS without initial external support.
11. Moreover, the national governments of each country, while prioritizing food security and rural development, have limited financial resources and technical capacity to establish sustainable markets for FL-RS with the majority of the limited resources being directed towards addressing the most vulnerable stage of the value chain, which, despite the urgent need, has remained relatively underdeveloped at the small scale—particularly when considering smallholder farmers.
12. By targeting the smallholder farmer as a key stakeholder in the development and implementation of MSMEs as service providers in the FL-RS value chain, RE-GAIN will build resilience directly within the communities themselves by solving the solutions into local systems that meet the individual needs of each community.

As a continental institution with a strong presence on the continent, AGRA will leverage its networks and partnerships on the continent, with strong country teams in the target countries. Using its continued engagement with governments, partners and other stakeholders to enhance its commitment to these key actors, knowledge and resources that are fundamental to take food loss innovation to scale.

This process of stakeholder engagement will continue throughout the programme implementation phase. The overall programme will be implemented under the overall guidance of a programme steering committee, which will meet quarterly with representatives from the GCF national designated authority and the AGRA country manager, with representatives of other key government counterparts (e.g., ministries of agriculture, environment, forestry, land resources conservation, crop development, agriculture extension services and agriculture planning services), the private sector and civil society organizations.

It is hoped that as AGRA moves into its next phase, there will be space for a multiplicity of voices from across the African continent and a vigorous debate about which approaches are best suited to address the challenges facing Africa’s smallholder farmers in achieving agricultural transformation across Africa, to reduce hunger, improve nutrition, and adapt to climate change.

Founded in 2004, AGRA is an African-led organization dedicated to sustainably growing the food systems of Africa. The organization operates in 12 countries, employing over 350 staff members across its headquarters in Nairobi (Kenya) and Ethiopia. As a result of this assessment (which led to an accredited entity for this funding proposal), the fiduciary function it can play (basic fiduciary standards for project execution) and its management and social risk category (category B).

### Efficiency and effectiveness: Scale: Medium to high

#### 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

The PSAs applicant AGRA, whose permitted fiduciary functions for this GCP programme are to invest in commercial projects, is seeking 100 per cent concessional funding for the programme. With a total budget of USD 105 million, AGRA is seeking USD 75.6 million, or 72 per cent of the total, and would itself provide USD 29.4 million, or 28 per cent of the total, in grant exchange.

The AGRA request for full concessionality is argued based on the nature of the investment programme, which seeks to: (1) catalyse commercial conditional procurements to catalyze a paradigm shift in the post-harvest value chain using innovative technologies and business models; (2) provide access to concessional grants for smallholder farmers and 2) catalytic grants for agricultural MSMEs - use concessional funding to incentivize private sector suppliers and financial institutions (FIs) to provide physical inputs, credit, and other services to smallholder farmers; (3) create a market for products that are otherwise commercially viable to private sector suppliers; and to provide tailored financial products that are offered by FIs to support the commercialization of new technologies. The intention is to leverage concessional funding in order to attract private sector investment.

In response to a query from ITAP about the risk of crowding out existing private sector suppliers, AGRA responded that "all interested and eligible suppliers (to be sourced through an open call following the eligibility criteria detailed in Annex 2 of the GCP) will have access to the programme." The programme seeks to build markets where they are non-existent and incentives all suppliers that enter into the market. It is expected that this will result in a significant increase over the current market failure of FL-RS being not available or affordable in specific targeted geographical areas due to factors such as lack of demand, limited production capacity, technology and finance. The intention is to grow the market and crowd in private sector players by driving the rollout of new technologies. RE-AGRA will thus:

- Drive the market through a combination of incentives for suppliers and consumers in their content.
- The "pay for two, get one free" type of investment will make sure the concessional funding ends up in farmers' pockets.
- Reduce the risk that FIs will not provide financing where demand is not yet confirmed (concessional funding will de-risk aggregators and distributors from remaining with unconfirmed demand).
- Reduce the risk for FL-RS service providers investing in equipment for which the business case is not yet proven. The manufacturers will be paid, and the service

### Overall Remarks from the Independent Technical Advisory Panel

In this proposed programme—RE-GAIN: Scaling solutions for food loss in Africa (brought to GCF by Green Climate Fund)—the IFAP notes that while the benefits of reducing the smallerholder farming sector in Burkina Faso, Ethiopia, Kenya, Malawi, Uganda, the United Republic of Tanzania, and Zambia are significant, the effects on staple crops such as maize and legume crop losses that are worsening is a result of intensifying climate change hazards.

The IFAP raises a concern that the significant subsidies to be provided to reduce the costs of FL-RS adoption may not have long-term benefits for markets. Farmers might not have a lasting effect, given the suppliers' large and short-term nature. There may be limited sustainability in the market if there is a sudden withdrawal. Nonetheless, the IFAP believes that the programme can have a major impact during the five-year period of implementation and beyond, as it will help to build the adaptive capacity of this highly affected sector in vulnerable priority regions of the participating countries.

# Introduction

## Programme Background

A great deal of attention has been paid in recent decades to the impacts of climate change on crop production. U., on growing risks to agricultural productivity. Scholarly investigations and public and private research have invested heavily in identifying and understanding these risks, as well as in developing strategies for adaptation or resilience on farms, yield stability over seasons, and in exploring plausible management options for the emerging challenges (CGIAR, 2023). As governments and societies look at how to minimize the risks of climate change, the impact of these changes on food production is increasing, fueling concerns about food security and livelihoods for current and future generations.

Food security, however, is affected not only by changes in crop production but by changes occurring throughout the crop value chain, including during post-harvest phases (Aldin, 2020). It is therefore crucial to examine the impacts of climate change on a crop's value chain, including production, aggregation, storage, transportation, processing, and distribution. Each stage comprises several sub-processes, and climate change may adversely affect many of these sub-processes too.

With the aim of using research and resources for resilience enhancement in the agricultural sector being focused on at the global level, this document aims to provide a comprehensive overview of the challenges, including those related to the changing production, transportation, and logistics. The document is structured as follows:

- Table 1.1: The International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) report highlights a range of climate change concerns in the post-production stage of value chain management and potential adaptation interventions that could increase resilience against such climate change concerns (IFAD, 2015).

| Value Chain Components | Climate Issues | Risk Management Interventions |
|---|---|---|
| Post-harvest management | Rising losses at harvest; volume declining due to increased temperatures, humidity, pests and diseases. | Improve knowledge sharing on harvesting techniques; develop and implement loss reduction measures and value addition for the harvested produce; ensure adequate cover changing requirements for cooling, drying, etc. |
| Siting of processing facilities | Extreme climate events such as floods, hurricanes, typhoons, droughts, landslides, processing facility shifts; shifting climate zones; reduced or increased transportation costs. | Build flood-resistant infrastructure and retrofit processing facilities with protective features to ensure their resilience against extreme climate events. |
| Energy in processing | High dependence on local bioenergy (wood, dung, etc.) for cooling, drying, etc.; high energy off-take with better soil management; rising water demands require more energy for cooling. | Provide renewable energy sources (such as solar and wind power); provide energy-efficient cooling systems (e.g., milling, heating, wind, bagging, etc.); use alternative energy technologies (e.g., solar, wind, solar chilling, electrical energy storage, etc.) to reduce energy consumption measures. |
| Water in processing | Declining and more irregular water supplies; growing competition with other domestic or industrial users. | Shift crops closer to more suitable water sources; increase water storage and distribution infrastructure; improve irrigation practices (e.g., groundwater recharge); introduce demand-side

# BRIEF PROGRAMME DESCRIPTION

There is a clear gap in knowledge, data and interventions designed to target the impacts of climate change at the harvest and post-harvest stages of the value chain, despite the mounting evidence of the ramifications on food loss and the impact


| Value Chain Components | Climate Risk Issues | Risk Management Interventions |
|---|---|---|
| Packaging materials and methods | Rising temperatures and humidity may increase or decrease post-harvest losses and damage to produce, particularly if current packaging materials are not suitable for the new climate. This can lead to product damage or poor quality. | Water conservation and contact solution for different water users (e.g., water groups). Develop dedicated packaging materials in parallel with waste and storage management strategies. |
| Processing infrastructure | Extreme weather events such as heavy rainfall, wind, and heat stress may occur during the peak rainfall, winds, and heat stress. | Introduce protective features and reinforcements into the design of critical infrastructure to withstand extreme temperatures, improve ventilation in buildings housing processing equipment, and use cooling systems and appliances; use early warning systems. |
| Transport hubs and routes | Routes may become seasonally or permanently impassable (or open up); extreme events will disrupt logistics. | Design transport infrastructure for road, rail, water, and air transport; co-design with local communities to ensure that the components avoid high-risk transport routes and are resilient against climate change risks. |
| Refrigeration and cold chains | Temperature rises increase requirements for costs of refrigeration, rising energy consumption, and increased greenhouse gas emissions. | Seek cost-benefit analyses of dependency on refrigerated cold chains to assess best route; introduce alternative cooling methods (e.g., solar-powered systems) and ventilation; optimize storage and transport management strategies. |
| Just-in-time logistics | Extreme climate events (floods, storms, etc.) may occur during the season and comply with "just in time" requirements. | Develop contingency plans for ultimate shocks to supply chains; identify alternative market opportunities; link into regional markets to avoid dependency on distant markets. |
| Demand from retail and consumers | Shifts in quality and quantity requirements due to climate change-induced disruptions in demand with climate change. | Assess market risks and opportunities before making commitments; monitor the impact of climatic impacts on high-value crops; diversify supply chains, including price fluctuations, diversively into "off season" crops. |
| Commodity labelling and certification | Increased consumer awareness as climate change may create new markets for commodities with a low carbon footprint. | Develop commodity-specific standards for procurement, labeling, and certification. |

AGRA is a continental institution working in 15 African countries addressing food systems focusing on smallholder farmers' production, marketing and nutrition. In the countries where AGRA operates, which are highly diverse in terms of climate, soils, crop choices and institutional capacity, neither all of these climate-related conditions may be expected, nor all of these problems will necessarily occur simultaneously; therefore, there is likely to be a wide range of risks that may be visible to address, and – given resource constraints – only a limited number of high-priority resilience interventions may be feasible to design and deploy. RE-GAN is an effort to identify the most salient risks, select the most impactful solutions, and implement the priority interventions through a well-structured, strategic, multi-country programme.

this has on land use changes and associated climate change mitigation. The majority of the current programmes designed to tackle climate-induced food loss focus on the pre-harvest stages of the value chain.

To address the pressing need for broader implementation of solutions aimed at reducing climate-related harvest and post- harvest food loss, the proposed programme is designed to raise awareness and build capacity to promote the adoption of Food Loss Reduction Solutions (FLRS). It will do this by creating enabling conditions, fostering the uptake of FLRS by end users and suppliers providing technical assistance for solution feasibility, and employing practices such as climate-smart forestry interventions. This will include enhanced financial access for farmers and Micro, Small, and Medium Enterprises (MSMEs), empowering them to invest in climate-friendly FLRS and incentivising vendors, manufacturers, and suppliers of climate-adapted FLRS, fostering a robust market ecosystem.

A key focus is on strengthening the capabilities of countries to develop climate-resilient post-harvest infrastructure, both through providing physical solutions alongside capacity building within the value chain. This includes investing in strategic financial instruments such as insurance, credit, and quality-based pricing systems and tax exemptions for reducing food loss. By enhancing access to markets, the programme will encourage farmers to adopt FLRS products and services, thereby boosting their climate and economic resilience.

Target Countries Overview

During the 2015-2027 period, AGRA plans to target 38 million farmers across 15 Sub-Saharan African countries, 40% of which will be women. The RE-GAIN Programme focuses on AGRA's activities in seven target countries, as shown in Figure 1-1 below. The RE-GAIN Programme is designed to combat food loss during the post-harvest stages and to boost climate resilience by fostering awareness and by building capacity for the adoption of Food Loss Reduction solutions (FLRS). The programme aims at increasing access to markets through strategic financial instruments such as insurance, credit, and quality-based pricing systems. It will also provide technical assistance for solution feasibility and employ practices such as climate-smart forestry interventions. This will include enhanced financial access for farmers and Micro, Small, and Medium Enterprises (MSMEs) to invest in climate-resilient FLRS. The programme plans to incentivize vendors, manufacturers, and suppliers to adopt these solutions and enhance the capacity of countries to develop climate-resilient post-harvest food handling infrastructure.

# Harvesting and Post-Harvest Definition

For the RE-GAN programme, the key value chain stages considered are shown in Figure 1-2.

| Harvesting process | Post-harvest handling and storage | Processing, transportation and logistics |
|---|---|---|
| Including: planting, sowing, and initial processing | Including: cleaning, sorting, and initial processing | Including: grading, packaging, and marketing of the crop. |

## Figure 1-2 Stages with value chain stages included in the RE-GAN Programme

The harvesting process within the RE-GAN Programme proposal is defined as the interval between the culmination of agricultural production, marked by the crop reaching maturity, and the initiation of post-harvest treatment. This process encompasses the identification of the optimal harvesting time and is further delineated into four distinct stages:

1. Removal of contaminated seeds, heads or cobs of mature crops at harvest.
2. Reaping, which involves cutting, pulling, or gathering the mature crop.
3. Threshing, which separates the grain from the stalks or other parts of the plant.
4. Cleaning, such as winnowing, to remove chaff and other impurities.
5. Hauling, which entails the transportation of the harvested produce to storage or processing facilities.

The post-harvest handling and storage stage commences once the crop exits the field and is typically conducted on the farm's:

- Threshing, which can be performed manually or with mechanical threshing machines.
- Drying, which may involve natural drying in the sun or artificial drying methods.
- Cleaning and sorting, such as through winnowing, to remove impurities.
- On-farm storage, which includes the use of grain bags, hermetic bags, ordinary bags, stacks, metal silos, and plastic silos.

In some instances, primary processing activities, such as grinding, hulling, pounding, milling, drying, and saving, are also conducted during this stage.

The processing, transportation, and logistics stage involves farmers selling their harvested crops either directly to traders, who collect the produce from the farm, or to collection centres and processors. These market participants then undertake the tasks of product accumulation, initial processing, quality control, grading, packaging, and transportation to wholesale buyers.

1 In this instance, a field is where the crops are grown, and a farm consists of the whole small holding including the small aggregation site.

1.3 REASONING FOR REQUESTED FUNDING

Africa’s food insecurity challenge has been exacerbated by climate change. Sub-Saharan Africa stands at a crossroads with an unprecedented opportunity for food systems transformation, driven by the demands of a rapidly growing population of 1.5 billion and the pressures of a changing climate (World Bank, 2023) (Worsterdorfer, n.d.). The continent faces significant development challenges including food insecurity, resource degradation, poverty, gender inequality, and social exclusion.

The vicious cycle of poverty and environmental degradation in Africa is evident in low crop productivity, deforestation, land degradation, desertification, and the loss of biodiversity. These factors are exacerbated by climate change, which has increased the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as floods, droughts, heatwaves, locust outbreaks, desertification, and sandstorms.

The impacts of climate change have increased over the past decades in Africa, manifesting in more frequent, intense, and prolonged extreme weather events, such as floods, droughts, heatwaves, locust outbreaks, desertification, and sandstorms. These extreme weather events have resulted in increased temperatures and humidity, shifts in precipitation patterns, water stress, and soil degradation. The impacts of climate change on agriculture are significant, with reduced yields for major crops grown under rainfed conditions, leading to short growing seasons reducing the viability of farming in marginal agricultural areas. Projected reductions in crop yields in some countries could reach as much as 50% by 2030, and crop net revenues may fall by up to 90% by 2100, with smallholder farmers being the most affected (IPCC, 2018).

Therefore, the RE-GAIN programme aims to enhance the climate resilience and adaptive capacity of smallholders by promoting the widespread adoption of IFI-RS in seven African countries. According to the World Bank estimates, a one percent reduction in post-harvest losses in Sub-Saharan Africa could lead to economic gains of $40 million each year, and most of the benefits would accrue to smallholder farmers (IFAD, 2013). The RE-GAIN programme will also promote more efficient use of resources and account for about 3.3 gigatonnes of greenhouse gas emissions globally (FAO, 2013). Large amounts of water and fertilizer also go into the production of food that never reaches human mouths. Reductions in post-harvest losses can help to reduce these inefficiencies while simultaneously strengthening livelihoods and improving food security—without imposing any additional environmental cost. Therefore, facilitated by the Green Climate Fund (GCF) investment, RE-GAIN will roll out a suite of physical interventions alongside capacity building and enhanced financial market and access tools. Not only will this benefit the respective countries as a whole, but it also has potential to benefit the region and even the wider planet.

1.4 PROGRAMME GOAL STATEMENT

If the capacity of the target countries and communities to respond to climate triggered food losses is strengthened through investment and incentives leading to learning, provision of context-specific and gender-responsive innovations to reduce food losses, and better enabling conditions for public and private investments, then smallholder farmers will have enhanced food security and livelihood resilience. Because of widespread use of food loss-reduction technologies will reduce food loss and reduce the carbon footprint of food systems, while increasing household income and building the resilience of smallholder farmers, MMEs and rural communities to climate shocks.

1. **PURPOSE AND STRUCTURE OF THE ANNEX**

This Annex seeks to ensure that the RE-GNIN Programme is designed, financed, implemented and monitored in a gender-responsive and socially inclusive manner. It has been developed in consultation with both the GCF’s Gender Policy, and its Gender Assessment and Action Plan Templates. The Annex adopts the GCF’s three-pronged approach to mainstreaming gender, using a robust gender assessment that considers the various factors (including those exacerbated by climate change) that influence gender inequality in each of RE-GNIN’s target countries’ agriculture sectors and identifies opportunities for promoting gender equality and youth empowerment, and reducing disparities. The findings from this gender assessment are translated into gendered actions that promote gender equality and youth empowerment, and reduce disparities in the sector, which are captured in a gender-sensitive monitoring and evaluation (M&E) framework (i.e., the Gender & Inclusion Action Plan). This Annex is informed by a review of existing literature and secondary databases, extensive stakeholder consultations and expert input.

This Annex sits within the context of the broader funding proposal and should be read as such:

* **Chapter 2** provides an overview of the RE-GNIN programme’s key components, outputs and outcomes. This framing document is intended to re-envisage Chapter 5 of this Annex, given the need to ensure that all Gender and Inclusion actions are appropriately embedded within the programme’s broader level of ambition and scope of delivery.
* **Chapter 3** presents an overview of the status of gender equality in each of RE-GNIN’s seven countries. It focuses specifically on: i) gender inequality in agriculture, including women’s access to land, credit, inputs, markets and agriculture; and ii) gender and climate change. It also outlines the legal, policy and stakeholder landscape as it relates to the intersection of gender, climate and agriculture in each country - providing an important framework within which the Programme can operate.
* **Chapter 4** describes the approach, findings, and recommendations derived from Programme-specific stakeholder engagement and literature review. It identifies preferences, notable barriers and potential opportunities to overcome adoption of GEF FLA/GEF-5 gender mainstreaming approaches, as well as potential solutions that should accompany such support.
* **Chapter 5** consolidates the recommendations from Chapter 4 into a Gender & Inclusion Action Plan, which contributes to - and aligns with – the Programme’s broader Theory of Change.
* **Chapter 6** concludes the Annex by outlining the overarching principles and approaches that will guide consistent, gender-responsive and inclusive Programme delivery, all of which are aligned to GEF’s existing institutional commitments and guidelines.

# Programme Overview

The RE-GAIN programme is designed to address food loss in Africa through an integrated approach, taking into account the growing need for climate adaptation in this space and the potential for mitigation of reducing food losses. The RE-GAIN programme's holistic approach to these growing challenges includes strengthening post-harvest services, particularly as it pertains to smallholder farmers. This document provides a summary of the programme’s components, focusing on the demand-side development component.

## 2.1 COMPONENT 1: FOOD LOSS-REDUCTION SOLUTIONS (FL-RS) DEMAND-SIDE DEVELOPMENT

This component focuses on increasing the demand for and adoption of FL-RS among smallholder farmers to reduce post-harvest food losses, enhance food security, and build resilience to climate change. The activities are centered around raising awareness, providing training, and improving market linkages.

### 2.1.1 Output 1.x: Support to Smallholder Farmers:

- **Awareness Raising:** Awareness campaigns educate farmers about the impacts of climate change on post-harvest losses and the benefits of adopting climate-resilient FL-RS.
- **Training Programs Through Extension Services:** Farmers receive training on best practices for harvesting, post-harvest handling, storage techniques, and the use of FL-RS. Training is tailored to local contexts and emphasizes practical skills.
- **Demonstrations of FL-RS:** Practical demonstrations showcase the effectiveness of FL-RS, helping farmers understand and adopt these solutions in their local environments.
- **Technical Assistance to Food Processors:** Assistance is provided to facilitate a shift towards whole-grain flour production, improve processing techniques, and enhance profitability.

### 2.1.2 Output 1.x: Improved Market Linkages:

- **Technical Assistance for Value Chain Structuring:** The programme helps establish more structured relationships between farmers, processors, and buyers, ensuring better quality compliance and reduced post-harvest losses.
- **Linkages with Institutional Markets:** Farmers are connected to institutional markets like school feeding programs, which provide reliable demand for high-quality produce, thereby encouraging the adoption of FL-RS.

### 2.1.3 Outcome for Component 1:

The main outcome of the component is an increased adoption of FL-RS by smallholder farmers.

## 2.2 COMPONENT 2: FL-RS SUPPLY-SIDE DEVELOPMENT

This component complements the first by improving the availability and affordability of FL-RS through support for local businesses and the introduction of innovative financial mechanisms to stimulate supply of FL-RS and improve the


---
**Page 160**
---

affordability of physical FL-RS solutions that can improve climate resilience for smallholder farmers and micro-, small- and medium enterprises (MSMEs).

### Output a: Business Development Support for FL-RS Providers

* **Through MSMEs and Cooperatives:** Local MSMEs and cooperatives, especially those led by women and youth, are trained to develop sustainable FL-RS service provision operations, with lease-to-own as an example. This includes technical training on equipment use and business management.
* **Market Intelligence and Networking:** AGRA supports the development of market intelligence and networks among MSMEs and cooperatives.

### Output a:2 Financial Mechanisms and Physical Solutions to Support the Adoption of FL-RS:

To stimulate supply and increase the affordability of FL-RS, the following can increase smallholder farmers' and community-led organisations' climate resilience; these physical FL-RS solutions have been selected given their impact on food loss and its climatic impact causes:

* **Hermetic Bags, Metal and Plastic Silos, Tarpaulins, and Plastic Sheets:** These are essential physical solutions aimed at reducing post-harvest losses. They protect crops from pests, moisture, and contamination, thus improving storage and prolonging the shelf life of produce.
* **Hermetic Bags (for storing grains):** Hermetic bags are designed to prevent grain loss by excluding oxygen. Using solar-powered fans, they are designed to reduce labor intensity and improve the efficiency of harvesting and post-harvest processing, minimizing losses due to improper handling.
* **Moisture Meters and Storage Protectors:** These tools help maintain optimal conditions for stored crops, reducing spoilage and increasing yields. Moisture meters are crucial for monitoring and managing crop moisture levels, which is vital in preventing mold growth and toxin production.
* **Common Storage Structures and Warehouses:** These facilities offer shared storage solutions that help smallholder farmers and cooperatives store their produce in controlled environments, reducing the risk of losses due to poor individual storage.

To increase the affordability of these solutions in the market, the following financial models will be leveraged:

Model 1 promotes the local supply of FL-RS interventions by using conditional procurements to effectively subsidise interventions at smallholder farmer level (referred to hereafter as "smart subsidies"). At its base, the smart subsidy model enables agro-dealers to provide FL-RS to smallholder farmers at a discounted rate by using GFG funds to procure one item every two years' procurement and sold out by agro-dealer. The subsidy is passed down to the smallholder farmer as a discount on the purchase of the FL-RS.

The interventions are typically focused on smallholder farmers, with lower individual ticket sizes to the end-users (i.e., for FL-RS such as tarpaulins and plastic sheets, metal and plastic silos, and hermetic bags. The five of flow funds will be facilitated through a trust/revenue account at a local financial institution (FI) and released once proof of off-take by eligible smallholder

* **Note:** the specific ratios will vary based on context of local markets in each country, with more developed markets receiving a smaller subsidy than those developed markets. A three-for-two model has been used as a base for discussion.

The maximum retail sales price and number of items per customer—as well as the eligibility criteria and geography where the sales are restricted can be made—will be included as part of the agreement between RE-GAN and suppliers as a condition for participation in the scheme.

# Women’s groups and local MSMEs

The aim of Model 2 is to create multi-stakeholder agreements with suppliers, youth groups, and financial institutions, with the goal of:

- Enhancing access - through repurchase assurances from the suppliers that lower the loss given default.
- Reducing borrowing costs - Through a combination of the lowered credit risk on low and above-borrowers and on the purchase price. The structure will ensure larger target FLRS become more affordable and thus accessible to youth groups who provide services to smallholder farmers.

At the national level, it is envisaged that two types of service-providing groups will be supported as act service providers for FLRS which require more expensive equipment than can service multiple farms – such as mechanical multi-crop threshers and shelters (preferably solar powered), moisture meters and communal storage structures. The establishment of the service operations will be supported through the business development under Output 2.1, ensuring that the youth groups have the necessary foundation to provide a reliable service. This approach leverages several key concepts to unlock the targeted benefits:

- Collective provision benefits to smallholder farmers in economies of scale through cost sharing and increased leveraging power with off-takes. These should provide further profitability and therefore additional demand for FLRS.
- Post-harvest handling will increase the quality and quantity of agricultural produce, allowing smallholder farmers to capture more value, hence increased incomes.

The inclusion of a subsidy is intended to finance in a typically underserved market. The structure aims to reduce credit risk by providing a partial subsidy that will lead to lower borrowing costs (thanks to smaller loan size and interest payments).

## 2.3 COMPONENT 3: ENABLING ENVIRONMENT FOR FL-RS MARKET SUSTAINABILITY

This component addresses policy-level constraints and builds institutional capacity to ensure the long-term sustainability and scalability of the FL-RS market, ensuring the long-term sustainability of the RE-GAIN programme.

### 2.3.1 Output 3.1: Enhanced Capacity of National Institutions

- **Policy Reform and Advocacy:** AGRA collaborates with governments to reform policies that currently hinder the development of the FL-RS sector, such as outdated regulations, reduced import duties, and the establishment of quality standards. AGRA also supports national governments in identifying gaps, enact and implement new policies and regulations where gaps exist.

The main outcome of this component 3 is strengthened enabling environment for the uptake of FL-RS.

# Country and sector-level analyses

This Chapter describes the status of gender equality in each of REGAN’s seven countries. It presents an overview of the progress, commitments, and impediments to closing gender gaps at a national scale, and then presents a high-level summary of two aspects of gender equality that are of primary relevance to this programme—namely: (i) gender agriculture; and (ii) the impacts of climate change on gender equality. This follows with an outline of the legal, policy and stakeholder landscape as it relates to the implementation of the gender-sensitive structure in each country—providing an important framework within which programme-specific gender interventions will sit.

## 3.1 KENYA

### 3.1.1 National overview status of gender equality

Kenya is a committed agency to numerous international agreements that promote gender equality, ensuring the protection and promotion of women’s rights. It ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 1984, and the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action (BPFA) in 1995 (UN Women Africa, 2023). Additionally, Kenya is a participant in the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), specifically SDG 5, committed to achieving gender equality and empowering all women and girls. Through these international commitments, such as the Maputo Protocol (Priorities for the African Charter on Human and Peoples’ Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa), Kenya has integrated international gender standards into its national legal and policy framework, working towards the promotion of women’s rights and gender parity (UN Women Africa, 2023).

## Box 1 | Kenya’s National Gender Law

Kenya has made significant progress in advancing gender equality. Through the enactment of laws, regulations and policies focused on increasing access to education for girls, their access to finance, and labour force participation (UN Women Africa, 2023). The Constitution incorporates international treaties including CEDAW and BPFA, ensuring equality is enshrined in national law with Article 27 guaranteeing equal treatment and opportunities for men and women. This has been complemented by gender-responsive budgeting efforts, where the Kenyan government is considered at the fore of pioneering and implementing such approaches at a federal level (HST, 2021).

Further to this, there are a number of gender-sensitive policies including the Child Law Policy (2009), which recognizes women’s equal rights in all aspects of life. This includes access to education, health care, and employment opportunities. The policy also addresses issues such as domestic violence and sexual harassment.

Kenya has ranked above regional averages for the World Bank’s Women, Business and the Law (WBL) Index and the World Economic Forum’s Global Gender Gap Index (GGGI). With a WBL score of 83.6/100, Kenya achieved a perfect score for mobility, workplace pay and market inclusion. Its GGGI score of 75 out of 146 countries reflects its efforts in education, health care, and parenthood, where there remains a need to enact legislation that supports women’s ability to start and run a business, and provide paid parental leave to allow women to more readily return to the workforce after having a child (World Bank, 2023). The country has also seen improvements in recent years, due to reforms that have moved in more stringent measures against violence, prohibition of gender discrimination, and equal pay for work of equal value. Contrary to this, Kenya’s GGGI rank of 75 out of 146 countries dropped by 2 places between 2023 and 2024 predominantly because of its subindex score for educational attainment, where Kenya ranks in the bottom 15 worldwide. Despite this, Kenya still ranks


---
**Page 165**
---

# Barriers to effective implementation of policies, legislation and commitments

The differences in measures of progress between the WIL and GGDI can largely be attributed to several key factors, including inadequate implementation of laws and policies for gender equality measures, poor accountability mechanisms and occupational gender gaps, all of which have hindered significant advancements in closing certain gender gaps (UN Women Africa, 2023). In particular, religious and customary laws, attitudes and practices are still considered key impediments to women's empowerment, as illustrated by data from the Kenya National Bureau of Statistics (KNBS) which indicates that only 15% of rural households in Kenya are headed by a woman. This is despite the fact that women make up nearly half of the agricultural workforce in Kenya (UN Women Africa, 2023). Nations around this concept of empowerment have led to several important differences between women and men with respect to higher education, asset ownership, and financial autonomy, where women trail behind men in each of these areas (Madarameeran, 2023). As a result, young women in rural areas are a particularly vulnerable group in Kenya. With limited access to resources and opportunities, they face significant challenges in accessing land, credit, and other inputs for agricultural production.

Women in agriculture

Agriculture is the primary source of income for rural households in Kenya, and women account for 75 percent of the labour force in the sector. This means that, in addition to their reproductive roles relating to water, energy and food security as well as household health and care, they undertake the bulk of agricultural work for home consumption and sale (UNDP, 2020). (ISD, 2018). Despite this, women earn significantly less than male counterparts, and operate largely in the informal agricultural sector. Women are also more likely to be involved in subsistence farming rather than commercial agriculture – including land. Cultural norms dictate that work is inherited by first-born males, meaning that women generally conduct their agricultural activities on the lands of men and are therefore beholden to the man's decisions on use of income and activities.

With women owning as little as 15-20% of agricultural land, and being an important form of collateral, they face major obstacles in accessing credit. Women also have limited access to extension services and other inputs for their farming operations – with a particular focus on securing women's land ownership rights (ISD, 2018). Women also lack access to information technologies, extension support, financial services and marketing support - all of which are critical to building resilience and scaling up agricultural production. This highlights several challenges in Kenya’s seed system. Collectively, these differences between women and men have resulted in women as well as a significant number of smallholder households and female managed agricultural enterprises. And poverty is particularly pervasive among low-income earning women and female headed households (UNDP, 2020).

### Box 2: Snapshot of findings from AGRA’s Gender Gap Analysis in Kenya’s National Seed System

AGRA's deep dive into the gender-differences within Kenya’s National Seed System was conducted using the SeedSAT. It highlights areas for improvement, requiring effort both by the government and private sector actors – for example (AGRA, 2022).

The findings from this analysis highlight several key challenges in Kenya’s national seed system through reforms to policy, legislation and regulation. Such reforms should be informed by greater engagement with seed producers - focusing specifically on small and micro producer units owned and managed by women.

# Climate change and gender equality

Both Kenya’s Updated Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) and National Adaptation Plan 2015-2030 acknowledge that climate impacts are not gender-neutral; women are disproportionately affected by extreme weather events due to their unequal access to key resources and services — such as land, finance, water, energy and education — which has major implications on the economy, particularly in rural areas.

As such, the impact of global warming and climate change was explored in detail as part of Kenya’s NDC review in 2020, which highlighted critical contextual factors existing to affect; agriculture and energy, and the impact that climate change offers an array of women due to their responsibilities and dependence on these sectors. Specifically, women's reliance on the natural resource base - both for productive and reproductive activities – makes them highly vulnerable to climate uncertainty. Protracted droughts have historically led to high rates of household food insecurity and malnutrition, while extreme flooding destroys crops and livestock, leading to loss of income and assets (e.g., housing, land, tools, seeds, animals, markets etc.). All these outcomes affect women more than men, given the traditional domestic and care-related responsibilities that Kenyan women shoulder. And within this broad social grouping, it is understood that pregnant women, children under five years old, and older women are particularly vulnerable to climate change (UNFPA, 2020).

Contrary to this, given men are tied to domestic responsibilities in the same rural context, they have increased mobility and often migrate to other areas in search of income in the aftermath of extreme climate events. While this additional income is important for their families, it does mean that women then need to take up roles that were previously allocated to the man in their family (IDRC, 2018).

## 3.1.2 Legal, policy and stakeholder landscape

Kenya’s commitments to gender equality are enshrined in its Constitution, legal and policy frameworks, and dedicated gender machinery mandates at national and subnational scales. (USADF, 2020) National instruments and key stakeholders working at the interface of climate change and agriculture in Kenya are captured in the following tables.

| Table 3-1 | Legal and policy landscape: Kenya |
| --- | --- |
| **National Policy on Gender and Development (2019)** | Agriculture is a key thematic area within the Policy, within which several key issues are flagged – relating to women being largely relegated to the informal sector and underrepresented in decision-making. The policy also highlights the need for the constant tension of domestic, social and economic responsibilities. Amongst the policy actions proposed to tackle these challenges, one focuses specifically on facilitating access to land and credit for women farmers. Given that traditional practices are resistant to modernist reform, women’s role in adaptation and mitigation strategies more broadly is also highlighted within the ENRM thematic area. |
| **Updated Nationally Determined Contributions (2020)** | The NDCs highlight the importance of gender equality as a key principle for such Kenya aims to implement its NDCs in a gender-responsive manner. As such, four adaptation programmes focused specifically on gender, youth and vulnerable groups are identified. |


---
**Page 167**
---

# National Action Plan (2015 - 2030)

Requires that all adaptation actions be implemented and monitored in a manner that reflects gender differences through the collection and analysis of gender disaggregated data. And within the Social Pillar: a set of short, medium- and long-term actions are described for Gender, Vulnerable Groups and Youth – aimed at strengthening resilience.

# National Climate Change Action Plan (WNCP) 2023-2027

Gender considerations are mainstreamed across all priority action areas within the NCCP. This includes the promotion of gender-responsive climate technologies and innovative financing mechanisms to support women’s access to resources, as well as encouraging a gender responsive approach to climate change. It also commits to the development of “Gender-aware agricultural services,” to promote greater inclusion of women in agriculture.

# Kenya Climate-Smart Agriculture Strategy 2017-2026

Includes a SWOT analysis of Kenya’s agriculture and climate change sectors, including the degree to which they tackle equity and inclusivity. Key issues highlighted include: lack of access to land, credit, inputs, markets, extension services, and financial resources and human resources for women and youth. In response to this, The Strategy identifies several gender-focused activities in its implementation framework, such as enhancing women and youth’s investment in the CSA value chain.

# National Climate Change Response Strategy (2015)

Within the Sectoral’s strategic adaptation and mitigation interventions, there is a focus on mainstreaming gender equality and addressing climate change through response strategies – including the provision of grants to support women led environment and climate efforts; training of gender focal points on environmental management and disseminating climate change information in a gender sensitive manner.

# Climate Change Act (Amendment 2023)

Aims to mainstream gender equity in all aspects of climate change responses. It states that the government will ensure that the design, implementation, and monitoring of national and county governments to mainstream intergenerational and gender equity in all aspects of climate change response.

# Kenya’s SDG5 Strategy 2020-2025

Reflects on the progress made in making gender gains; predominantly through policy reforms and affirmative action programmes – and highlights areas requiring more targeted attention, such as women’s representation in politics and decision-making. The strategy also identifies key themes, which cut across sectors and themes - including agriculture and climate change.

3.2 UGANDA

### 3.2.1 National overview: status of gender equality

The Government of Uganda acknowledges the importance of expanding opportunities for women, both as a fundamental human right and as a pathway to sustainable, inclusive development. This commitment is reflected in Uganda's gender-responsive law and policy framework, which aims to reduce gender inequalities and address vulnerabilities across social, political, and economic sectors. At the international level, Uganda aligns with key instruments such as the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), the Beijing Platform for Action (BPfA), and the Global Agenda 2030 Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) (Uganda Bureau of Statistics, 2019).

#### Box 3 Uganda's National Gender Policy

Uganda’s National Gender Policy was revised in 2007 to reflect important development imperatives, including the need for increased participation and representation of women, improvement in women’s literacy rates, and empowerment of women in business (Government of the Republic of Uganda, 2001). This Policy, alongside other related reforms such as the 2006 National Equal Opportunities Policy, have resulted in some progress in reducing gender inequalities and vulnerabilities, and promoting affirmative action in key sectors (Wahab et al., 2010) (Uganda Bureau of Statistics, 2019).

Additionally, in 2021, two key pieces of legislation were passed and enacted in 2022. The Sexual Offences Amendment Bill aims to address the sexual violence faced by women and girls following the rise of the #MeToo movement. It promotes gender equality in line with internationally recognized human rights standards. Meanwhile, the Employment Amendment Bill introduces provisions to address sexual violence and harassment in the workplace, ensuring safer and more equitable working conditions for all (MPO, 2021).


---
**Page 169**
---

### Uganda ranks top globally for the WFP’s GGGI indicator on health and survival

This is attributed to its high scores for “healthy life expectancy” and “sex ratio at birth.” Conversely, it sits in the bottom 20 countries for educational attainment due to low literacy rates, low tertiary education enrollment (which is a stark contrast to the gender parity achievement for primary education environments). Overall, the country ranks 83 out of 146 countries. (WFP, 2024)

### Barriers to effective implementation of policies, legislation and commitments

The overall prevailing influence of informal institutions, including patriarchal, religious and socio-cultural practices, are considered primary drivers of these gendered differences – making it particularly challenging to advance progress in key reproductive health areas. (Uganda Bureau of Statistics, 2019) Within more formal institutions, significant obstacles persist in terms of budget constraints, as well as limited human and institutional capacity to implement existing frameworks. There is a need to focus on closing the gap between legislation and practice by enhancing accountability mechanisms to monitor and assess the implementation of these laws and policies effectively (Uganda Bureau of Statistics, 2019).

### Women in agriculture

Ugandan women play a critical role in the country’s agricultural sector; they make up more than half of the agricultural workforce, and more women are primarily involved in agricultural practices than men (88% versus 78%) (IDH, 2021) (Bweny et al., 2021). However, despite this contribution to food security, women face significant challenges. The Pan-African Institute for Food Security found that there are substantial differences in male and female managed plots – determined notable disparities between 65% of all women in Uganda do not own land; male managed plots on average were on 60% greater than females’; and men earn 11 percentage points more likely to plant cash crops than women farmers. This means that they dominate high-value cash crop value chains and have larger livestock assets. Moreover, while use of improved seeds, fertilizers, herbicides, pesticides, and fungicides is low for women and male farmers, female farm managers are less likely to use them than men (Bweny et al., 2021) (IDH, 2020). Other studies have reaffirmed these findings and added further insight into why these disparities persist. Specifically, due to ingrained social norms, women continue to bear the burden of uncompensated time spent collecting water and firewood.


---
**Page 170**
---

# Uganda’s National Seed System

AGRA’s deep dive into the gender dynamics in Uganda’s National Seed System was conducted using the SeedSAT. This highlights evidence of inclusivity in the value chain, alongside areas for improvement. For example, women are well represented in agricultural research institutes and laboratories, as well as quality control departments within seed companies (where women in management tend to outnumber men).

However, as with many other countries reviewed using the SeedSAT, there is no evidence that government and private sector seed producers provide open and inclusive communication channels, external support or training. [AGRA, 2022]

## Climate change and gender equality

Uganda’s 2019 Human Development Report outlined three major, interconnected national challenges: climate change, gender inequality, and environmental degradation (Mwambe et al., 2020). Gendered differences in Uganda societies mean that women and female-headed households are poorer, and more vulnerable to poverty. They also have less agency and ability to participate in decision-making, governance and policy-oriented processes at all levels (from household through to national Government); all of which contribute to them being less resilient to the impacts of climate change and loss empowered [and] marginalized women are more likely to be affected by climate change than men. This is not only because Uganda’s agricultural sector (described above) but also in its service sector (where women are the primary providers of food) and waste disposal – it is clear that women should be central actors (as leaders and beneficiaries) in these three climate sensitive sectors.

As a result, Uganda has made considerable efforts to acknowledge the impact of climate change on women and elevate the role that they play in national climate action efforts. The National Climate Change Policy (2018) includes both a policy priority and a policy principle specifically addressing the needs of, and including, women, girls and other vulnerable groups in Uganda’s climate change strategies [Ministry of Water and Environment, 2018], and this has been translated into actionable measures through instruments such as the National Determined Contributions (both the original and updated versions) and related implementation plans.

Uganda’s Updated NDCs (2022) were informed by a gender analysis which served as a basis for determining how to meaningfully enhance women’s resilience and adaptation capacity within the context of existing societal dynamics that influence gendered differences in key sectors [Ministry of Water and Environment, 2022]. As a result, NDCs propose a series of actions to enhance gender-responsive NDC implementation – covering inclusive decision-making processes, specialist gender appointments to key ministries, gender-responsive budgeting, skills and capacity development and enhanced gender

3.3 ETHIOPIA

### Overview: Status of gender equality

Ethiopia is a signatory to a wide range of international conventions and protocols that champion gender equality and equity. These include the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW), the Beijing Platform for Action, and the Maputo Protocol (the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights on the Rights of the Woman). The country has also committed to achieving the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), including SDG 5, which focuses on gender equality. Recognizing the transformative potential of these frameworks in eradicating poverty, reducing inequality, and building a sustainable future, Ethiopia has proactively integrated and aligned the SDGs with its Ten-Year Development Plan (TYDP; 2021-2030), ensuring these goals are mainstreamed across all sectors of the economy (UN Women, 2024).

Box 5 (Ethiopia): National Legal Gender Framework

Since the enactment of Ethiopia's National Women Policy in 1989, the country has made consistent progress in advancing gender equality and equity. This progress is reflected in the establishment of key institutions (most notably, the Ministry of Women and Social Affairs), participation in international treaties, and the introduction of progressive legislative reforms to ensure that gender equality is carried through to all levels of decision-making and resource allocation. The National Women Policy requires all federal and regional public institutions to have a women's affairs unit or office. In addition, the country has made significant strides in increasing the representation and capacities on gender mainstreaming are also being prioritized at the highest levels of government - for example: the Ministry of Finance and Economic Development’s recent adoption of gender-responsive budgeting and reporting for key sectors, like health. (Gender Equity Unit, 2023).


---
**Page 173**
---

# Barriers to effective implementation of policies, legislation and commitments

Beyond these markers of progress and areas for growth, it is evident that gender disparities differ vastly across urban and rural settings, ethnic groups and regions. Despite progressive laws and regulatory reforms, patriarchal social norms and structures continue to perpetuate gender gaps in many contexts which have major economic and social implications.

Gender Equality Index (GEI) scores are a composite measure of gender equality based on 10 indicators covering education, health, political empowerment, economic participation and opportunity, and violence against women. The GEI is calculated by the World Bank Group using data from the bottom ten countries worldwide for educational attainment, largely due to notable gender differences in literacy rates and tertiary education enrolment (although small improvements are evident year on year). Progress toward parity in economic participation and opportunity is also low; while Ethiopia is one of six countries to have increased the share of women in technical and professional positions by a percentage point between 2012 and 2013, in real terms only 36.3% of technical positions are held by women (REF, 2024).

## Women in agriculture

In Ethiopia, women farmers perform up to 75% of farm labour, which equates to 70% of the country's horticultural food production (Chandler et al., 2022) [UNWomen, 2018]. Despite their significant contributions, women face serious constraints in accessing resources and opportunities on both their personal circumstances and the local environment. For example, they typically produce 35% less than male farmers and cultivate land that is on average 0.48 ha smaller than the land of their male counterparts. With less access to land, women farmers are inherently at a disadvantage given the critical role that land ownership plays in securing formal credit and other forms of collateral, along with other resources like water supply, improved seeds and fertilizers (Chandler et al., 2022). Women also face significant challenges related to lack of access to information and technologies all of which impact the effectiveness and sustainability of their farming practices, leading to less productive, profitable and diverse outputs. (Chandler et al., 2022) [UNWomen, 2018] [World Bank, n.d.]


---
**Page 174**
---

# Climate change and gender equality

The effects of climate change in Ethiopia are not gender neutral. Research shows that women and children are the highest risk groups, most vulnerable to extreme shocks and stresses (with sub-factors, like location, age and disability status further complicating their vulnerability). Women also have less access to information on climate change adaptation strategies, credit, time use and responsibilities at household and community levels, and differential treatment of women and men by formal institutions (which affects their ability to receive information, benefit from support, etc.) (World Bank, 2021) (N. Mehary, 2023). Climate change and environmental degradation have a significant impact on the agriculture sector, which has traditionally been gendered in Ethiopia. Women are more vulnerable than men because they lack access to climate-resilient technologies, tools and information (UNWomen, 2018). It is evident that climate change is further exacerbating inequalities which restrict Ethiopian women's ability to meaningfully participate in all spheres of life (UNDP, 2021).

Acknowledging this, as part of Ethiopia’s NDC review in 2021, a gender analysis was conducted to ensure key issues and entry points to promote gender equality through climate action were effectively integrated into the updated NDC. The study found that, while Ethiopia's climate change policies and strategies for agriculture, forestry and energy were generally women-responsive, there is still a need to address gender inequalities in agriculture and forest sector adaptation measures which reflected "limited genderization". The same was found to be the case for the NDC adaptation measures, where only four out of the 66 performance indicators stipulated the need for gender disaggregated data. This implies that, at the point of integrating these measures into implementation strategies and plans at the sectoral level, more needs to be done to ensure climate change policies are gender-sensitive (UNDP, 2021). In light of this, the study recommended that the introduction of green technologies and approaches in the agriculture sector need to focus on reducing existing burdens on women – where the division of labour at the farm level is highly gendered. (UNDP, 2021).


---
**Page 175**
---

Similarly to the updated NGOs, Ethiopia’s Climate Resilient Green Economy National Adaptation Plan (NAP) reflects a clear commitment to ensuring adaptation options are designed and prioritized through a gender-sensitive lens, whereby the needs of women are given due attention so that they benefit equitably from climate action [Federal Democratic Republic of Ethiopia, 2019]. However, beyond this principle on gender-sensitivity, little is stated in the NAP on gender-responsive approaches or sex disaggregated data collection which would indicate how this will be achieved in practice.

3-4 BURKINA FASO

### National overview: status of gender equality

Burkina Faso has demonstrated its commitment to international gender frameworks, aligning with global efforts to promote gender equality and eliminate discrimination against women (CEDAW) and the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action. The country has integrated gender equality principles into its national development policies. Burkina Faso has also embraced the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), particularly Goal 5, which seeks to achieve gender equality and empower all women and girls. Through these efforts, Burkina Faso aims to ensure equal access to education, health care, and decision-making while improving access to education and healthcare, addressing gender-based violence. Despite challenges related to poverty and security, Burkina Faso continues to work towards meeting its international obligations for gender equality (The Global Green Growth Institute, 2019).

Box 7 Burkina Faso's National Gender Legislation

Burkina Faso Constitution affirms equal gender rights, and the country has established a national institutional framework to promote the social and economic empowerment of women. The government has taken several steps to ensure equal opportunities for women while promoting gender mainstreaming, though its capacity remains limited. The Government adopted the National Gender Policy in 2005, aimed at ensuring equal participation, access to resources, and decision-making for both men and women. That same year, a law was introduced mandating a 30% quota for women's representation on electoral lists, with political parties facing a 50% funding cut for non-compliance (The Global Green Growth Institute, 2019).

Burkina Faso scored 82/100 under the World Bank’s Women, Business and the Law (WBL) index, above the regional average, achieving a perfect score in entrepreneurship, assets and pension. The main areas for improvement include pay, where there is no law that mandates equal remuneration for work of equal value for men and women; and where women are unable to work in jobs deemed hazardous including construction and factories (World Bank, 2023). Under the World Economic Forum’s Global Gender Gap Index (GGGI), it was ranked at 128 out of 146 countries, a drop of 13 places since 2022. The country has made significant progress in reducing gender gaps in education and health, but it was noted that it is one of seven countries in sub-Saharan Africa to have achieved equal gender representation in senior economic roles (WEF, 2024). Nevertheless, it still scored lower than the regional average, with further progress needed.

### Barriers to effective implementation of policies, legislation and commitments

Gender inequality remains a significant issue in Burkina Faso. Despite efforts to enhance women's participation and representation, women and girls continue to face disproportionate barriers across education, health care, and economic opportunities, exacerbated by numerous challenges such as the rise of Islamist extremist groups and the impact of the COVID-19 pandemic. These issues have exacerbated existing inequalities, making it even harder for women to access opportunities and services.


---
**Page 177**
---

resources. Furthermore, deeply entrenched patriarchal norms and cultural practices have impeded progress toward gender equality (CEPPS, 2021).

Although the Burkinabe government has introduced measures, including a gender quota for parliamentary candidates, women remain severely underrepresented at the national, regional, and community levels of politics, limiting their influence on decision-making processes (CEPPS, 2021). Additionally, a recent study affirms that there is a significant gender gap in wages driven by a notable difference between men’s and women’s earnings. Women are paid less than men for the same work due to occupational segregation across all sectors, with women typically being employed in lower productivity sectors. This gender gap is not associated with educational attainment. Instead, it stems from women experiencing lower economic benefits of their education because of young children or the household; meaning they are often employed as unpaid work in family businesses and farms. The fact that social norms, which now women’s primary role as caregivers, are the underlying cause of this disparity (Atheisa Donald, 2024).

### Women in agriculture

Across Burkina Faso, agriculture employs a significant portion of the workforce, accounting for 74% of the overall labour force. In total, 76% of women, compared with 72% of men (International Labour Organization, 2022) are engaged in the agricultural workforce. In rural regions of the country, the primary area for agricultural production, women assume responsibility for domestic tasks and childcare while also participating in farm activities. Women’s participation is often limited to seasonal work, as well as working on the farm during tasks from weeding to harvesting. Despite their numerous responsibilities, women have significantly less access to the resources and services necessary to enhance their productivity and income and to alleviate their domestic burdens (Dendiele et al., 2020).

In those instances where women own a farm, this reduced access to services translates into barriers for financial resources and credit. This has led to women primarily concentrating on lower value crops, as well as having reduced adoption of improved agricultural techniques. Where women are utilized in the form of labour, they are unlikely to be paid at all, largely down to them primarily being considered to work as family farms (World Bank Group, Africa Region, 2024).

### Climate change and gender equality

Burkina Faso submitted its updated Nationally Determined Contributions (NDC) in 2021. It emphasizes the need for gender analysis in the priority sectors of the NDC and the development of gender-sensitive NDC sector plans. It also states that any data collected will be disaggregated by sex where possible and to include more women (and young people) in agriculture (Government of Burkina Faso, 2021).

Burkina Faso has submitted a National Adaptation Plan (NAP) in 2015, which acknowledges that women are more vulnerable to the impacts of climate change and there is need to adapt for this when implementing the activities outlined in the NAP. It also states that women should be included as beneficiaries where possible, when accessing access to adaptation techniques, and to provide increased capacity building in leadership for women (Burkina Faso Ministry of Environment and Water Resources, 2015). It has not submitted a Climate Change Gender Action Plan (ccGAP).

## 3.5 MALAWI

### 3.5.1 National overview status of gender equality

The Government of Malawi has implemented robust measures to ensure that social inclusion and gender equality are integrated into national development frameworks, including the Constitution. Malawi ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 1987 and is a signatory to the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action (BDPA). Additionally, Malawi ratified the Southern African Development Community (SADC) Protocol on Gender and Development in 2008 and has committed to the UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), including SDG 5, which focuses on achieving gender equality. The Government has also taken steps such as increasing women's representation in parliament, promoting female participation in decision-making, and efforts to reduce gender-based violence. These actions demonstrate the Government’s commitment to advancing the legal status and protection of women and vulnerable groups (African Development Bank, 2020).

**Box 8 Malawi’s National Gender Charter**

The Government of Malawi's efforts to improve the current state of gender equality can be seen through its increasing number of policies on the matter. The Constitution of Malawi explicitly guarantees equal rights for women, prohibiting discrimination based on language, religion, or social status. Article 30 of the Constitution affirms the rights of all individuals and communities, giving special attention to women, children, and people with disabilities.

Additionally, the Government has enacted several gender-related laws to address the challenges faced by women and girls. These include the Prevention of Domestic Violence Act (2006), the Deceased Estates (Wills, Inheritance and Protection) Act (2011), the Gender Equality


---
**Page 179**
---

Adopted in 2013: The Trafficking in Persons Act (2013); the Child Justice Act (2013); and the Marriage, Divorce and Family Relations Act (2015).

In 2017, the Constitution was further amended to align the legal definitions of a child and marriage/marriage age at 18, aiming to eliminate child/early marriage in all forms. The Land Act was also revised in 2013 to enhance women’s access to control over, ownership of land, a critical productive asset (Economic Development Bank, 2020).

Efforts to integrate gender considerations into broader country strategies include the “Malawi Vision 2035” which acknowledges the negative impact gender inequality has across all sectors and commits to advancing gender equality at all levels through a multi-sectoral approach (Malawi National Planning Commission).

Malawi scored 80.0/100 under the World Bank’s Women, Business and the Law (WBL) Index, achieving a perfect score in personal status law, pay, marriage and assets. The main areas for improvement include mobility and parenthood, where women are unable to choose when they live or apply for a passport in the same way as a man, and there is no paid leave of at least 14 weeks for maternity leave (World Bank, 2023). Malawi’s gender equality index score was 58.7/100 under the World Economic Forum’s Global Gender Gap Index (GIGI) (WEF, 2020). However, it was included in the 2022 report, with a rank of 110 out of 146 countries, and an increase of 22 places from the previous year. This is largely due to a substantial shift in its sub-index score for political empowerment, due to the increasing number of women in ministerial positions (WEF, 2023).

**Barriers to effective implementation of policies, legislation and commitments**

The responsible gender issues have limited capacity and funding, weakening coordination efforts. Key structures like gender focal points exist but are under-resourced, limiting effective implementation of gender equality and mainstreaming projects. The majority of women in Malawi are engaged in subsistence farming and GBV laws are not widely enforced due to poor disarmament (African Development Bank, 2020). National data indicates that Malawi has a larger gender gap in secondary enrollment compared to the Sub-Saharan Africa average and lower overall enrollment levels. Girls face barriers such as lack of access to education, limited financial resources, early marriage, childbearing, fertility, skills, economic opportunities, and agency. These factors are interconnected in complex ways, but global evidence indicates that investments in keeping girls in school are particularly crucial. These investments have substantial impacts on their earnings, standard of living, child marriage and early childbearing, fertility, population growth, health and nutrition, access to decision-making, social capital and institutions, and future per capita investments in human capital (The World Bank, 2022).

**Women in agriculture**

Malawi’s economy is highly dependent on agriculture, contributing over a quarter of the country's GDP, and employing approximately 80% of the population with the majority being women. However, the sector's contribution to GDP is hampered by the large gender productivity gap. On average, plots managed by women are 25% less productive. This is largely attributed to lower levels of inputs (including seeds and fertilizers), reduced access to labour, technology and knowledge as well as cost constraints (African Development Bank, 2019).

A recent study in Malawi, funded by the World Bank, found that female farmers were much more likely to use poor quality seeds, reduced amounts of fertilizer and farm on lower quality soil compared to their male counterparts. Additionally, the study found that when accounting for the quality and quantity of these inputs, there was no gender gap in agricultural productivity (William J. Burke, 2021). Additionally, extension services are critical for ensuring women have equitable access to agricultural inputs and receive fair returns from those inputs. However, research indicates that female farmers are female-headed


---
**Page 180**
