# Climate change and gender equality

Malawi submitted its updated National Determined Contributions (NDCs) in 2021. As part of its social protection remit it now includes a focus on gender mainstreaming in policies, programmes and projects as well as a commitment to "actively integrate the mainstreaming of gender considerations within climate change measures and when relevant to track climate change issues and indicators according to gender and vulnerable groups." This is echoed below, highlighting the need to ensure climate change adaptation measures are planned with a gender lens and tracked to account for gender and other vulnerabilities.

Malawi has not submitted a National Adaptation Plan or a Climate Change Action Plan (ccAP) thus far. However, it does have a National Adaptation Plan Framework under its Ministry of Natural Resources, Energy and Mining Environmental Affairs Act (2019) and the NDCs are aligned with this framework. With regard to ECoG, Malawi has committed to linking to the NAP process including ensuring adaptation actions consider women's access and impact alongside the use of sex-disaggregated data and inputs from gender experts for planning purposes (Government of Malawi, 2020).

# National Climate Change Management Policy

It emphasizes integrating gender considerations, ensuring climate initiatives are inclusive and address women’s specific needs, promoting their participation in decision-making processes related to climate change adaptation, sustainable land management, and improved access to climate information and technology, aiming to boost productivity and resilience, particularly benefiting women who play a significant role in farming.

It has been delivering financial services to women in rural communities since 2002. It provides loans and ongoing business and financial literacy training to clients across its 17 branches and eight satellite offices, with a large proportion of clients engaging in farming.

NASFM is the largest independent smallholder farmer membership organization in Malawi. It has been providing support for women farmers through training on business management, marketing, quality control, literacy, and basic education and information services. It emphasizes a gender-inclusive approach and has developed a gender programme to increase participation of farming and extension leadership.

## Box 10 Tanzania's Gender Decade Legislation

Beginning in 1992, the Government of Tanzania has consistently pushed for the integration of gender equality into its ministries and policies, starting with the creation of the Ministry of Community Development, Women’s Affairs and Children (now known as The Ministry of Gender, Children and Social Welfare). In 1994, the government established a National Gender Commission. A further step was taken in this area, aimed at increasing women's participation in society and protecting their rights. This includes the 1999 Land and Village Lands Acts which equalize land ownership rights for men and women. The 1972 Law of Marriage Act grants women equal property rights within marriage, including the right to inherit property equally with her husband. However, despite these laws, progress has been slow. For example, despite a 2013 court ruling mandating the marriage age from 18 years to 16, 18 UN Women (2020). More recent gender considerations have been integrated into the Tanzania Development Vision 2050, which commits to redressing gender imbalances and empowering women (The United Republic of Tanzania, 1999).

Due to these efforts, Tanzania is ranked above regional averages for the World Bank’s Women, Business and the Law (WBL) Index and the World Economic Forum's Global Gender Gap Index (GGGI). Under the WBL Index, Tanzania scored 83.1/100 and received a perfect score for workplace, pay and mobility (World Bank, 2023). Its main areas for improvement include parental leave and assets, where there is no paid leave of at least 14 weeks available to mothers, and both sons and daughters are equally entitled to inheritances. However, Tanzania’s GGGI score has dropped from 58th place in 2016 to 73rd place for the GGGI. Tanzania is ranked 54 out of 146 countries, down six places from last year (WEF, 2024). This drop is largely due to a decrease in its sub-index scores for economic participation and opportunity, as well as educational attainment.

## Barriers to effective implementation of policies, legislation and commitments

Whilst the areas for improvement differ between the GGGI and WBL Indexes, they both fundamentally indicate a need to improve the opportunities available to women and girls from education through to work, including the restrictions on marriage and childbearing. The most significant barrier is the lack of enforcement of existing laws. For example, the Law of Marriage Act, which permits marriage for girls as young as 15, while the Law of the Child Act does not address the legal age of marriage or prohibit child marriage. Progress was made when Tanzania’s high court ruled in 2016 that marriage of girls under 18 is unconstitutional, a finding reaffirmed by the Court of Appeal in 2019 (The World Bank, 2022). However, this ruling has not been enforced and many women are still married before they reach the age of consent. In Tanzania, women who marry before turning 15, or over half married before turning 20, these rates are more than five times higher than those for men (The World Bank, 2022). Further barriers to successful enforcement include the limited representation of women in law enforcement and judicial positions, which hinders their participation in decision-making processes. This is compounded by traditional gender biases, along with violence and discrimination against women, perpetuating social norms that undermine their confidence and opportunities for leadership. This is then subsequently reflected through the ongoing systemic challenges, including discriminatory legal frameworks and sociocultural barriers rooted in traditional norms (Luki, 2024).

### Women in agriculture


---
**Page 183**
---

Across Tanzania, agriculture employs a significant portion of the workforce, accounting for 65% of the overall labour force. In total, 67% of women, compared with only 48% of men (International Labour Organization, 2022), are engaged in the agricultural sector. Despite women making up this majority, female farmers appear to experience a reduction in both quantity and quality of yields compared with their male counterparts. This appears to be because of reduced access to male farm labour and decreased returns from both labour and non-labour inputs, including pesticides and organic fertilizers (Vanya Stachevich, 2015).

Female plot managers, often widowed, divorced, or separated, lack male household labour and rely more on female family labour, which tends to be less productive. In instances where they can afford to hire male labourers, they tend to be less profitable than male farmers (The World Bank, 2015). Additionally, women's uneven return from non-labour inputs (i.e., pesticides and fertilizers) is likely related to either using lower quality products or insufficient knowledge on when and how to apply them (The World Bank, 2022).

Due to the significant role agriculture plays in the Tanzanian economy, in April 2022, President Samia Suluhu announced "Agenda 10/30", aimed at boosting agricultural output growth by 10% by 2030. Among other measures, the centre of this strategy is the expansion of tenure and land, with over 3 million women expected to be added to the sector by 2030, made up mostly of women and youth. It also aims to ensure each farmer is allocated a minimum of 50 acres of land (NCDP, 2021).

### Climate change and gender equality

The National Council for Gender Equality (NCGE) was established under the Constitutionally Determined Constitutions (CDCs) in 2018 and has since submitted an updated version in 2021. In its most recent NCGE, under adaptation measures to be pursued, there is a brief mention of gender mainstreaming, highlighting the need for enhanced gender equity in climate change adaptation actions as well as promoting measures to address the negative impacts of climate change on women. Beyond this, there is no further mention of gender issues including anything related to mainstreaming or equity (The United Republic of Tanzania Vice President's Office, 2021).

Tanzania has not submitted a National Adaptation Plan (NAP), though it does have a National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) which makes no mention of gender equality or mainstreaming (United Republic of Tanzania, 2007). It does however have a Gender Policy Framework (GPF) and the National Guidelines for Mainstreaming Gender into Environment (NGME) (The National Guidelines for Mainstreaming Gender into Environment (2014)).

3.7 ZAMBIA

### 3.7.1 National overview: status of gender equality

Zambia has shown strong dedication to promoting gender equality by engaging in various international agreements. The country ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) in 1985, affirming its commitment to protect women's rights and promote gender equality. Zambia is also a signatory to the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action (1995). Zambia has pledged to address issues such as education, health, and employment. Additionally, Zambia is committed to the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), with a specific focus on Goal 5, which aims to achieve gender equality and empower all women and girls by 2030. On a regional level, Zambia is a signatory to the African Union Agenda 2063, which emphasizes gender equality as essential for sustainable development across Africa (The World Bank, 2023).

Box 17 Zambia's National Gender Equality Strategy

Zambia has developed and enacted policy and legal frameworks to promote and foster gender equality. Legislation on gender equality covers several sectors including health and education, as well as economic opportunities and agency. This includes the National Gender Policy which promotes gender equality and addresses gender-based violence through equal opportunity initiatives, anti-discrimination measures, and support for women's empowerment. The Zambia Gender Equality Act (ZEGA) of 2015 aligns Zambia’s legal framework with international and regional gender equality standards, including CEDAW, and prohibits sexual harassment and discriminatory practices. It also mandates the creation of a Gender Equity and Equality Commission to oversee gender issues (The World Bank, 2023).

This commitment to gender equality is mirrored in its development agenda, where as part of the Zambia Vision 2030, the Government of Zambia has pledged to “promote gender equality for sustainable economic development” and improve “educational attainment and eliminate gender gaps at all levels of human and social economic development.” (Republic of Zambia, 2005). As part of its National Action Plan, it has committed to integrating both climate change and gender into the annual planning and budgeting process, with a set of gender equality indicators that will be used for monitoring and evaluation. These indicators are expected to be finalized in light of their use as global governance indicators on this matter (Ministry of Gross Economy and Environment, 2023).

Zambia is ranked above regional averages for the World Bank’s Women’s Business and the Law (WBL) Index and the World Economic Forum’s Global Gender Gap Index (GGGI). Under the WBL Index, Zambia scores 82.1/100 and received a perfect score for workplace, job, and entrepreneurship (World Bank, 2023). Its weak areas for improvement are pensions and mobility, which do not account for periods of absences due to childcare in pension benefits, and insufficient legislation that would allow women to work for a passport in the same manner as men (World Bank, 2023). Zambia’s GGGI rank currently sits at 154 out of 146 countries, with a score of 7.2/100 (WEF, 2024).

Zambia has made significant progress in education and attainment. However, Zambia was one of the 26 countries that came out first under the subindex ranking health and survival (WEF, 2024).


---
**Page 186**
---

# Barriers to effective implementation of policies, legislature and commitments

Zambia’s progress in closing the gender gap has been challenging, with gender imbalance persisting in multiple aspects of society. In particular, the effectiveness of gender equality norms is significantly hindered by various complex barriers. One of the most pervasive challenges stems from deeply entrenched cultural and traditional norms that uphold patriarchal values. These norms often limit women’s rights and perpetuate gender stereotypes, undermining the effectiveness of gender equality policies. Additionally, there are institutional constraints, such as early marriage and lack of access to education, which can further exacerbate these issues over statutory laws. Making it hard to implement and enforce laws designed to protect women's rights (Ochukwa, 24). Additionally, the capacity of institutions responsible for implementing gender equality policies is often limited by a lack of resources, funding, and trained personnel. Many gender-related programs are underfunded, which leads to weak implementation and enforcement mechanisms.

The persistence of these barriers has led to slow progress in enforcing them (The World Bank, 2023). Finally, low levels of public awareness, particularly in rural areas, further complicates efforts to enforce gender equality commitments. Many women and men are unaware of the legal protections and rights guaranteed by international agreements and national legislation. This lack of knowledge hinders their ability to demand accountability from authorities and advocate for gender equality. As a result, enforcement of gender equality commitments remains a challenge, requiring not only legal reforms but also cultural shifts, resource allocation, and increased awareness at all levels of society (The World Bank, 2023).

## Women in agriculture

In Zambia, agriculture is a vital sector for the economy, providing food, employment, and income. It accounts for a significant portion of the country's GDP and employs over half of its workforce (Zambia National Bureau of Statistics, 2021). The sector plays a critical role in poverty alleviation and economic growth, with various government initiatives in place to support agricultural development and resilience (International Monetary Fund; African Development Bank, 2023). Of those women in employment, approximately 62% (International Labour Organisation, 2022) are engaged in the agricultural sector. However, women face significant barriers when it comes to accessing land for agriculture and rain-fed farming, as many face barriers when it comes to accessing and/or controlling resources that would allow them to expand their capacity. This includes both customary and statutory land tenure being heavily gendered and limited financial inclusion with an estimated 68% of women not having access to any form of financial services (The World Bank, 2023). This is exacerbated by the lack of access to social protection schemes such as Social Cash Transfers (SCTs), which limits their ability to access and engage in formal training and business opportunities (The World Bank, 2023).

## Climate change and gender equality

In 2018, the Government of Zambia launched its Climate Change Gender Action Plan (ccGAP) which is designed to ensure that Zambia’s climate change processes incorporate gender considerations, ensuring that both women and men have equal access to participate in and benefit from climate change initiatives. It recognizes the disproportionate impact climate change will have on different groups of women, as well as the pivotal role women can play in adaptation measures. It advocates for policy reform, education, increased awareness, academic research, use of disaggregated data and greater capacity-building and training (IUCN, 2018).

The implementation of ccGAP is referenced in both Zambia’s National Adaptation Plan (NAP) (Ministry of Green Economy and Environment, 2023); and its updated National Determined Contributions (NDCs) (Government of Zambia, 2021), though the NDCs neglect to go into further detail. Zambia's NAP does however raise the need to integrate both climate change and gender into its annual planning and budgeting process. This includes consideration for the different needs and priorities of

**Page 187**
---

3.7.2 Legal policy and stakeholder landscape

National instruments and key stakeholders working at the intersection of gender, climate and agriculture in Zambia are captured in the following tables.

Table 3-12 Legal and policy landscape: Zambia Instrument

| Relevant content |
| --- |

* National Gender Policy (2013): Agriculture is identified as a priority sector for fostering sustainable economic growth with women supplying 50% of labor. Their involvement is deemed key. It flags key issues including poor access to land, as well as women often being engaged in informal and low-productive work with limited decision-making power. The policy also highlights that influence women have in decision-making as well as access and control over resources. The policy further seeks to address this issue facilitating women's access to resources and services including land and finance alongside mainstreaming gender into policy and agricultural practices.
* Updated: Nationally Determined Contributions (2021): Mentions Zambia’s NDC, but apart from this there is no further mention of gender.

National Adaptation Plan (2015 – 2036): Reaffirms the need to integrate both climate and gender into its annual planning and budgeting processes. The plan also highlights that it will be covering this, and a training programme focused on the implementation of GWP.
* National Policy on Climate Change: Launched in 2017. The new National Policy on Climate Change is designed to stem the impact of climate change and includes a focus on restoring our future and its impact on economic growth.

Climate Change Gender Action Plan (GAP): Addresses the disproportionate impact climate change will have on the livelihoods of women, particularly smallholder farmers. It also highlights that it will be covering gender mainstreaming in climate research, sex-disaggregated data and greater capacity building and training.
* The Gender Equity and Equality Act (GEQA) No. 23 of 2015: Introduces key international and regional human rights instruments on gender, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW), the Protocol, and the SADC Protocol on Gender and Development. It also prohibits sexual harassment and harmful practices while aiming to eliminate all forms of discrimination against women.

Key stakeholders

| --- | --- |
* Gender Division (formerly known as Ministry of Gender): It is responsible for “coordinating and monitoring the effective implementation of gender policies with other government Line Ministries and cooperating partners.”
* Ministry of Agriculture: The mandate of the Ministry of Agriculture covers the adoption of smart and innovative measures to improve productivity as well as supporting agricultural trade and marketing. The ministry also has a specific responsibility in the area of land tenure, and it is a mainstreaming of gender within the sector as one of its priorities.
* Zambia Agricultural Research Institute (ZARI): ZARI sits under the Ministry of Agriculture and focuses its work on generating agricultural technologies, with a focus on those used by small-scale farmers.

# 4 RE-GAIN programme-level gender analysis

Building on the country-level analyses above, this Chapter describes RE-GAIN’s approach to understanding the programme-specific gender dynamics within the seven countries, the way in which these dynamics influence key gender domains, and the impact that these domains may have on programme activities. The Chapter then describes the findings from applying this stakeholder-driven approach, serving as a basis for developing the Programme’s Gender & Inclusion Action Plan (Chapter 5).

## 4.1 APPROACH

National and local stakeholder workshops were facilitated in each of the seven RE-GAIN countries, serving as key informants of this programme design process. The first round of workshop discussions (comprising 14 workshops) focused on the pressing climate challenges faced during harvesting, post-harvesting and processing, transportation and logistics processes – considering these challenges from a gender perspective per country – and potential solutions that could be provided by the RE-GAIN programme to overcome these. These workshops were attended by a range of stakeholders, with an average 50% representation of male and female participants.

Building on these workshops, a second round of engagements was facilitated at national and local levels (comprising 14 workshops in total), to garner feedback and recommendations on the solutions and barriers to accessing them, with a particular focus on the gender, environmental and broader social considerations that may influence the sustainability and impact of the proposed solutions on different subgroups of the target communities. A core focus of these workshops therefore explored:

- The environmental and social risks and impacts of the implementation of solutions, informing the development of Annex 6.
- Potential gender and youth-specific barriers that may impact women and youth’s adoption of solutions and identifying opportunities to overcome these, as a guide to determining key recommendations and actions proposed in this Annex.

The dedicated gender and inclusion session was structured in three parts:

- The first element focused on describing the role that women play in harvest and post-harvest value chains – and whether these differ for male counterparts or youth.
- The second element of discussion considered various domains that influence women, men and youth engagement in agriculture, and specifically the harvest and post-harvest activities for the select country crops. These domains included:

  - Practices and participation;
  - Knowledge, beliefs, and perceptions;
  - Access to assets;
  - Legal rights and status;
  - Gender roles, responsibilities and time-use; and


---
**Page 190**
---

### FINDINGS

As with many of the findings presented in Chapter 2 on the disparities (and underlying drivers) between women and men within the context of agriculture and climate change - many clear, common and consistent themes on gender and inclusion emerge from the fourteenth workshop reports. This sheds light on the fact that, while the seven RE-GAIN countries differ in terms of gender equity considerations across key sectors and markets / indicators of progress, they experience many similar challenges related to women's access to resources, power dynamics, and push to agricultural value chains.

As such, findings from the workshops are presented in summary format below. Given the interrelated struggles between the domains listed below, many findings are interlinked or cut across multiple domains.


| Gender domain: the underlying drivers that influence the roles, voice, choice and control that women, men and youth have in agricultural value chains |
| --- |
| Patterns in the practices of women, men and youth involved in the agricultural sector are largely reflective of how these groups are socially constructed in terms of their contribution to, and position within, society as a whole. During the workshops most participants concluded discussions on “practices” with the typically assigned "roles and responsibilities" of these different groups. The graphic below presents a snapshot of the most common delineation of roles, indicating differences and areas of overlap in the responsibilities of women, men and youth at various stages along agricultural value chains. This delineation reflects the underlying patriarchal power dynamic that governs how land is used: men take a lead role and operate at the meta-scale of land preparation activities while women and youth take on supportive, more menial tasks. This delineation is similarly echoed across the post-harvest value chain stages and thus of significance to the planning and design of the RE-GAIN programme, which focuses on these latter stages. |
| The narrative that follows provides more depth into the implications of these roles in terms of other key gender domains. |


---
**Page 191**
---

# Socio-cultural and gender relations were noted as the significant underlying reasons for food value chain inefficiencies, which in turn directly cause food loss.

The more productive and soccer roles of men and women, as prescribed by socio-cultural structures affect their access to and control over assets, knowledge and services, and their participation in productive activities and decision-making. This impacts the efficiency of the food value chain and hence is an underlying reason for food loss. Women and men have different preferences, constraints and opportunities. They also have different needs, conditions and preferences when carrying out their activities along the agricultural value chains.

The gender roles are particularly relevant in determining the response of a specific food value chain to food loss reduction approaches and interventions, and consequently determining their effectiveness and impact of F.L.R.S.


---
**Page 192**
---

Both men and women attend agricultural-related groups or meetings and at times women’s level of participation exceeds that of men. Where women empowerment has been higher than that of men, this has led to differential effects by development programs to reach more women. In some cases they play a dominant role in the household due to patriarchal norms; women and youth are more reserved in meetings, while men take on primary speaking and decision-making roles. Youth’s views in particular are often overlooked by elders. However, the creation of dedicated savings groups and farmers associations for women and / or youth shows positive signs of empowerment where women and youth can participate equally with men. In some cases, women have been able to gain more influence due to their level of engagement during mixed meetings as women and youth grow in confidence and men’s perspective on the benefits of meaningfully including women and youth evolve.

Men, and to a lesser extent women, are primary beneficiaries of extension support and agricultural training programs. This results in women having less access to agricultural inputs and technologies, and knowledge on new / more efficient or profitable practices, which limits their ability to upscale or diversify farming operations. The gaps women face in access to training, skills, and technology was reported to be due to a combination of gender norms and social factors such as lack of education, low levels of education, lack of decision-making power, and limited time due to their high burden of care work. However, where women are included in trainings, it appears that they are generally more receptive to such support.

While youth provide significant labour across the agricultural value chain, they have limited authority and generally participate under the guidance of older family members, including working on labour-intensive activities alongside older people.


### Knowledge, beliefs and perceptions

Traditional and cultural beliefs play a significant role in agricultural practices, definition of roles and responsibilities, and food security. This perpetuates beliefs that certain tasks are inherently more suitable for men, women or youth which can reinforce stereotypes, patriarchal norms and inequalities. In many cases across the RE-GAN countries, these beliefs include:

- Women are caretakers, rather than agricultural producers and as such their work on farm families is often unpaid.
They perceive themselves to have knowledge about advanced farming techniques and have lower literacy rates, and therefore receive less exposure to agricultural education opportunities. Tasks like winnowing, sorting and food processing for household consumption, as well as cleaning tasks in agriculture settings, are performed by women as these activities are associated with the domestic realm.

- In some cases, women are seen as producers within households and community spheres - asserting their dominance through ownership / control of assets, higher levels of literacy, seniority within traditional structures, and control over higher value cash crops and livestock. For example, selling produce, even if produced or processed by women and youth is assumed as a man’s role while the money accrued from these sales is managed to men.
- In some cases, women are seen as producers in agriculture settings but their involvement in decision-making processes is limited. In turn, youth tend to associate farming with poverty, and it thus is not considered a desirable form of employment by many.

In some communities, polygamy is perceived as a source of labour, with women and children working on farm families where they are expected to implement traditional practices. Literacy levels, school enrolment rates, and early marriages.


### Access to assets & legal rights and status

Access to assets is inherently connected to the patriarchal socio-cultural norms that dominate many rural communities in each of RE-GAN’s focus countries. This means that the presence of gender equitable legal rights – particularly those relating to ownership or inheritance of land and other productive assets (such laws are present in majority of the RE-GAN countries)


---
**Page 193**
---

- does not equate to enforcement or progress. In instances where traditional customs govern decision-making, this means that:
    * Property acquired in a household is automatically considered the man's, and thus legally registered in the name of a man.
        This implies that in most communities and families, women and youth do not own land and other valuable assets (like livestock, infrastructure, etc.) and therefore have limited or no control over how such assets are used. This applies to inheritance as well, where customary laws still tend to favour boy children over girls and women. Such legal bias restricts women's economic empowerment.
    * Even in matrilineal societies where women do own land, there is still evidence that men-control decisions relating to the use of such assets, limiting women's ability to fully participate in and benefit from their own resources. A knock-on effect of this is that some women also have limited control over access and use of agricultural inputs like seeds, fertilizers and equipment.
    * Land ownership is a primary enabler of accessing credit and other financial services, making it particularly challenging for women who do not have access to such collateral to secure financial support. This undermines their agricultural productivity and economic empowerment.

- Men and older women generally have more access to land than youth, making it difficult for youth to engage in entrepreneurial activities within the agricultural sector. However, youth tend to have greater access to information through the internet and social media platforms, which is increasingly being viewed by farmers as beneficial and useful.
    * Women are often excluded from decision-making processes related to land use and management.
        This extends to issues relating to property disputes, and domestic abuse or gender-based violence. This stems, in part, from very limited representation of women in local government and related legal support services.

### Power and decision-making

As evidenced through the above domain descriptions, clear power dynamics exist between women, men and youth within rural communities.

- Men hold primary decision-making power within households, on farms and broader community structures. This extends to choices around land use, technology adoption, crop choice, marketing and sale of produce, and related financial matters such as loan uptake or credit access.
    * Women are often excluded from decision-making processes related to land use and management.
        This extends to issues relating to property disputes, and domestic abuse or gender-based violence. This stems, in part, from very limited representation of women in local government and related legal support services.

- Women make decisions that tie in with their domestic responsibilities, such as food security and nutrition, water and firewood collection and storage, health and hygiene, and small-scale business activities. At times, a husband and wife may jointly decide on these matters, but this is not always the case.
    * Youth hold no significant power in domestic or productive spheres, both at household and community levels. This partly stems from a common perception that they lack experience; meaning their opinions are often overruled purely due to their age. However, youth who have gained greater education will hold greater power than decision on issues in the household.
        Youth-targeted agricultural programmes are showing some potential to help empower youth and influence the responsiveness of older relatives to their opinions.

### Adopting RE-GAIN's solutions: barriers and opportunities for women and youth

The gender dynamics of these communities, and specifically how they relate to local agriculture sectors, provides an important foundation upon which RE-GAIN's proposed solutions can be explicated in terms of uptake and suitability. Gender relations are also highly relevant in determining the effectiveness of food loss reduction strategies and interventions. The findings from

# Recommendations

As per Table A-1, almost all solutions were considered of medium-to-high preference for women, but several notable barriers were consistently highlighted in discussion – flagging the importance of ensuring targeted gender-responsive support is provided as part of the RE-GARN project. Such support includes:

- Improved, inclusive access to finance for women and youth.
- The upfront cost of many of the solutions proposed was highlighted as a primary barrier to adoption by women and youth. It is therefore critical that inclusive and diverse

## 4.3 Recommendations


---
**Page 195**
---

### Inclusive and accessible capacity building and awareness raising for women and youth

Women were aware of each of the solutions proposed, they consistently acknowledged the need for capacity building / awareness raising: to ensure that tools, equipment and structures would be used correctly; on the benefits of changing current practices or preferences; and on the importance of using locally sourced, durable materials in production. Women’s relatively lower endowment of resources represents one way in which gender can influence FL RS. Contact with extension agents is seemingly lower among women farmers, and this further gives them less access to the information and skills training that facilitate the adoption of new technologies

#### Sensitization of male community members
In many communities, women’s participation in training workshops, their share of resources, and their ability to make decisions about finance options, are controlled by their husbands. It is therefore critical to ensure that men are included in discussions that centre around the importance of such opportunities, to garner their support and understanding of the broader family / community wide benefits of such.

#### Local production of sustainable, high-quality products made using local materials
Women voiced concerns around the quality and durability of solutions like tarponius and hermetic bags. This stems from past experiences where they have purchased or been provided with such options and have been let down by the delicateness of materials. It is therefore important to both ensure that improved designs made with locally sourced, durable materials are made available, and that information on these improvements is shared widely so that women are sufficiently aware of quality of such products and are encouraged to invest in and utilise them.

Based on the above, and in keeping with the GAIN’s programmematic approach, and the fact that common gender related barriers and opportunities exist in all seven countries, a set of programme wide gender and inclusion actions should be prioritised during subsequent design, implementation and M&E processes. These recommendations serve to guide the Gender & Inclusion Action Plan that follows.


---
**Page 196**
---

# Gender & Inclusion Action Plan

## 5.1 KEY ASSUMPTIONS AND CONSIDERATIONS

To ensure that the goals and recommendations highlighted in Chapters 2 and 4 are integrated and implemented into the broader RE-GAN Programme, a Gender & Inclusion Action Plan (GAP) has been developed. The Gender & Inclusion Action Plan will be responsible for ensuring that gender equality is mainstreamed into all aspects of the programme’s broader design and budget. The Gender & Inclusion Action Plan should be adopted based on the following key assumptions and considerations:

- **Experts:** The Expert makes an allowance for the appointment of a dedicated full-time programme-level Gender Expert who will also be responsible for overseeing the planning, delivery, and reporting on all indicators; this expert will also serve as the lead technical specialist responsible for all process-related gender activities such as policy revision, establishment of relevant systems and processes, awareness raising campaigns etc. The Gender Expert will also be responsible for ensuring equitable representation across AGRA’s Senior Executive Committee and Programme Advisory Board and provide the required capacity building expertise for all regional offices and country programmes.
- **AGRA Governance:** AGRA’s Group Director will oversee the Gender Expert's delivery and outputs. The Gender Expert will be based at AGRA HQ in Kenya. In addition to this full-time Gender Expert, two part-time Gender Officers have also been budgeted for; who will sit in other RE-GAN countries and regions.
- **Action Plan Staffing:** The Gender & Inclusion Action Plan has its own dedicated team of experts. This staffing structure ensures that the Gender & Inclusion Action Plan has the necessary resources and presence across the continent, so that each region can be readily serviced and risks can be effectively managed and escalated accordingly.

- **Alignment with Programme Strategy:** Given the contribution of this Gender & Inclusion Action Plan to the Programme’s broader Theory of Change (ToC), it is Impact Statement. Outcomes and Outputs directly link into those in the ToC. This ensures that all proposed Activities clearly align with the Programme's primary activities and are thus embedded within the 'workplan' – as opposed to being considered "additive" or of peripheral importance.
- **Multi-country Programme Start Up:** A standardised process for the start-up phase of a multi-country programme of this nature, several preliminary steps must be carried out prior to implementation of this Gender & Inclusion Action Plan. These include:
  - Framing AGRA’s gender and inclusion institutional arrangements, which will serve as the programme's broader organisational framework.
  - Establishing country-level Gender Focal Points in each Country Office, whose roles should be captured in Terms of Reference. Guided by this Gender & Inclusion Action Plan and aligned with AGRA relevant staffing policies. Lines of engagement between the Gender Expert and AGRA’s country offices (notably AGRA's embedded gender personnel) will also be established, with roles and responsibilities between each group clearly defined.
  - Developing country-specific Gender & Inclusion Action Plans that directly feed into this programme-level Gender & Inclusion Action Plan. These will be informed by baseline gender assessments in each country, to ensure that the results are relevant and specific to the context of each country. Country-level processes and outputs will be delivered during the first six months of the programme, with the country-level GAP submitted to the GOF for approval in Q1 Year 3. This is a critical step which will inform the establishment of more specific disaggregated targets for women, men, youth and other

# Programme application

To ensure consistent application of gender-responsive and socially inclusive planning and implementation, per component and across all countries, several important principles and approaches are outlined below. These comply with AGRA's organizational commitments and globally accepted E&S best practice.

## 6.1 GENDER INTEGRATION APPROACH

In keeping with AGRA’s Five-Year Gender and Inclusion Strategy (2022-2027), the RE-GAIN Programme will adopt the Reach-Benefit Empower Framework across each of its Three Outcome areas:

* **Women in decision-making:** The number of women that are included as core Programme participants (measured through quotas at meetings and trainings, and number of women / youth only events) and through targeted effort to identify and deliver benefits that women and youth value explicit price on (be these time savings, workload reduction, income generation, investment opportunities etc.)
* **Linked to the aims, benefits sought, and youth will ensure:** Ensuring Programme interventions tackle specific needs and constraints to their productivity in the agriculture sector, through tangible actions designed to meet their preferences.
    * Empowerment of women and youth will focus on strengthening their voice, choice and control over the various gender domains that they value. This aligns with the Programme’s approach to utilising community-driven feedback and evidence-based interventions to effectively tackle drivers of disempowerment and power imbalances between women and men in post-harvest value chains.
    * These outcomes will be achieved through transformative change alongside the creation of an enabling environment that addresses gender norms, through for example, interaction with powerholders in the community to critically examine gender norms, inequalities and influence change.

## 6.2 GRIEVANCE MECHANISM, SAFEGUARDING TOOLS AND STAKEHOLDER ENGAGEMENT

### 6.2.1 Grievance Mechanism

Stakeholder engagement measures will work pro-actively towards identifying and addressing issues before they become grievances. However, when grievances are reported they need to be addressed in a consistent and verifiable manner as part of the Programme’s commitment to accountability.

AGRA will ensure through the E&S Due Diligence that the grievants will establish a mechanism to receive and facilitate resolution of stakeholders’ concerns, complaints, and grievances about the project’s performance. Procedures stipulated by AGRA’s Grievance Mechanism (Annex 9 of AGRA’s ESMS Manual) will be followed and stakeholders will be informed about the process in advance. The grievance mechanism will be designed to ensure that it is transparent, easily understandable, appropriate, and readily accessible to all segments of the stakeholders at no costs and without retribution. Furthermore, the mechanism can be verified by AGRA’s E&S Manager, who will then communicate with the Team of E&S Officers. Those actions are applicable for IFC’s risk Category A, B and C projects.


---
**Page 208**
---

### 6.2.2 Safeguarding Approach

AGRA is committed to the protection and safeguarding of children and vulnerable adults (together referred to as Vulnerable Persons) and The Youth. AGRA acknowledges that it has a duty to ensure that its programs and operations are conducted in a manner that is safe for Vulnerable Persons and Youth. AGRA recognizes that there is potential for abuse to take place within the context of its work with children, youth, and vulnerable adults.

AGRA’s Safeguarding of Vulnerable Persons and Youth Policy sets out AGRA’s commitment to protect the Vulnerable Persons including women and youth it works with either directly or indirectly, from any form of Abuse. It provides mandatory guidelines on preventing any form of Abuse and Exploitation of Vulnerable Persons and sets out codes of conduct that are applicable to AGRA’s Staff and Partners in this respect. It also sets out the mechanisms AGRA will follow in the event any abuse is reported.

The policy is in conjunction with other AGRA policies, including but not limited to the following: (i) Ethics Policy (ii) Protection of Children Policy (iii) Child Protection Policy (iv) Gender Equality and Women’s Empowerment Policy. It also sets out the disciplinary processes & procedures (v) Data Protection Policy. To operationalize AGRA’s Safeguarding of Vulnerable Persons and Youth Policy, the HR, Legal and Gender, Youth and Inclusiveness units have developed the following tools for use in program implementation with partners, and are thus all applicable and will be utilized within the context of RE-GAIN:

- Safeguarding Implementation Plan
- Safeguarding and PSRER Risk Assessment Form
- Travel Risk Assessment Form
- Safeguarding Incident Reporting Form
- Youth travel information Table

### 6.2.3 SEAH and GBV Risk Management

AGRA’s commitment to the Do-Humanity principle is embedded in all its programmes. This commitment is operationalized through the aforementioned Safeguarding Policy, designed to prevent and respond to any risks of abuse or exploitation.

This includes mandatory gender and safeguarding training for all staff, partners, and community leaders involved in programme implementation. For example, in Rwanda, AGRA has implemented Community-Based Safeguarding Committees composed of both male and female representatives who serve as the first point of contact for reporting SEAH and GBV concerns.

At the programmatic level, AGRA takes a proactive stance by integrating a GBV/SEAH Risk Assessment as part of its Gender Analysis Toolkit. This forward-thinking approach is applied before launching interventions in new contexts, ensuring that potential risks are identified early on. The risk assessment includes an analysis of existing laws and regulations, social norms, and patterns related to GBV/SEAH. For instance, in Malawi, this risk assessment revealed that women engaging in higher-income agricultural roles faced heightened risks of domestic violence. AGRA responded by designing Household Gender Balance Training that worked with men and women to reduce tensions around shifting financial roles.

RE-GAIN will therefore adopt AGRA’s SEAH Policy and Risk Monitoring and Mitigation Frameworks, which entails:

- Country-Specific Safeguarding Risk Audits: Conducted annually to identify program-specific risks related to SEAH and GBV.


---
**Page 209**
---

# 6.2.4 Stakeholder Engagement:

Stakeholders are persons or groups who are directly or indirectly affected by a project, as well as those who may have interests in a project and/or the ability to influence its outcome, either positively or negatively. They can include affected communities located near a project, particularly those subject to actual or potential project-related risk and/or adverse impacts on their physical environment, health or livelihoods.

How stakeholders are engaged will determine the success of a project implementation, as it forms the basis for managing a project's environmental and social impacts. A detailed consultation process is required and all engagement with stakeholders must be conducted in line with AGRA’s Environmental & Social Safeguard Policy. A Stakeholder Engagement Plan (SEP) is required to be developed and implemented for each project on an organisational scale. The SEP should be reviewed, updated as necessary, and be tailored to the characteristics of the affected persons or communities. Template of a SEP can be found in Annex 9.

The outcomes of the overall and country-level strategic risk assessments should be formally taken into consideration in the updating and planning of AGRA’s overall guidelines and strategy for engagement and documented through minutes, and in any engagement undertaken on organisational scale.

# APPENDIX A Workshop Meeting Notes from Gender and Inclusion Sessions

The following subsections summarise the key gender aspects captured in each of the national and local workshop reports. These workshops were held in discussions that pertained specifically to the RE-GAIN programme. Each gender session was prefaced by presentations, groupwork and feedback sessions on the targeted agricultural value chains selected per country as well as a discussion on the gender dimensions of climate change. The sessions focused on issues such as specific issues, opportunities and needs were discussed.

While every effort was made to ensure that the gender sessions were delivered consistently across countries, the facilitators also ensured that each session was tailored to focus on issues considered most pertinent to the stakeholders in the room. As such, the focus and granularity of the findings from each workshop differ somewhat.

Key takeaways are combined for each country's national and local workshops.


## A 1. KENYA

### Table 7-3 Key gender takeaways from Kenya’s national and local stakeholder engagement
**Discussion topic:** Gender domains
**Key takeaways:**
* Women in small-scale units have limited access to land, and no decision-making power. Both from a cultural and (presumed) legal perspective. In instances where legal rights exist for women, they are not necessarily commonly practiced.
* Interestingly, most farmer groups are either predominantly composed of women or have a majority of women who are interested but not able to attend trainings, accept aid and services work than men and youth.
* Youth often lack collateral.
* Women and youth primarily engage in harvesting, threshing and processing within the value chain. Women also seem to be more focused on nutrition and the overall welfare of the home. Men, traditionally, are seen as the primary producers and decision-makers for the farm, with a greater use of mechanised equipment. Youth may also be employed alongside men for anything very labour-intensive.

### FLR preferences, barriers & solutions
* On-farm storage solutions for Kenya: tyres/old tires and plastic sheets have the highest preference amongst women, followed by hermetic bags, sacks and metal containers. Barriers to adoption of storage structures were included amongst the lowest.
* Financial solutions were seen as the main barrier required to improve opportunities for adoption of agroforestry practices in Kenya in the form of credit or improved access. Current credit interest rates are very high (up to 10-25% per month). Second to financial solutions was the need for greater training and capacity building, that would need to be inclusive and accessible.

# A.3. ETHIOPIA

## Key Key policy takeaways from Ethiopia (continued and finalizable segment)

### Discussion topic: Key Takeaways

#### Gender domains
- Women are responsible for food security of the household, as they are the ones that are saving production for household consumption.
- Women are involved in harvesting, drying, storing, threshing beans, cleaning the grain, etc. They are responsible for the food security, safety of produce under storage, etc.
- Men are involved in land acquisition, land preparation, planting maize, processing grain. They make decisions on enterprises, methods of threshing, modes of transport, types of markets where produce is sold. Men have more opportunities to access credit or access to credit from finance loans than women and youth, because they have greater access and ownership of land (most communities and families are patriarchal in terms of owning land). They are also given platforms during meetings – overshadowing women and youths’ views.
- Women are involved in harvesting but have no significant power or decision-making capacity. They have less access to information due accessibility to social media platforms.
- Women fear of being ashamed of reporting their husbands to legal structures when husbands do wrongings.
- Attendance of meetings and workshops is more by men, who in most cases do not pass on the knowledge to women.

#### FL-RS preferences, barriers & solutions
Highest preference for specific types of inputs includes access to finance for women farmers through microfinance schemes from banks. The use of improved seeds, women training, irrigation and plastic mulch which requires access to affordable funding, could be linked to existing programs, and requires improvements in the current poor quality of bags that are available). Mechanical multi-crop threshers are also needed, but these will require subsidies for women farmers due to high upfront costs.

Even when women do own assets, cultural norms frequently prevent them from having control over these assets. | * There are gender differences in responsibilities for agricultural activities such as planting, preparation and threshing, while women are responsible for weeding, transplanting, and harvesting. # A.6. TANZANIA

## Key policy pathways for female smallholder and local stakeholder engagement

### Discussion topic: Key takeaways

#### Gender domains

- **Women tend to participate in labour-intensive activities such as planting, weeding, and harvesting.** They have limited participation in decision-making roles or higher-value activities like marketing and financial management of the farm business.
- **Men are more likely to engage in land preparation, ploughing, and activities that involve machinery.** They are more likely to control financial aspects and decision-making in farming operations. Also participate in marketing and selling of produce, typically seen as the face of the farming business.
- **Youth tend to have limited access to resources and training opportunities.** Their participation is usually under the guidance of older family members, with limited autonomy.
- **Women are perceived as caretakers rather than main agricultural producers.** They have limited access to agricultural education and training, perceived as less knowledgeable about advanced farming techniques. Cultural norms may limit their involvement in certain agricultural practices considered "male tasks."

FL-RS preferences, barriers & solutions

Focus of discussions was on gender domains only.

**Page 219**

# A7. ZAMBIA

## Key takeaways from Zambia national and local stakeholder engagement

### Discussion topic: Key takeaways

#### Gender domains
- **Women** are responsible for household chores, field labour, harvesting, threshing, and packaging.
  - This holds some household decision-making power over how money is spent on domestic purposes.
  - Men are more likely to make decisions about land use.

- The general inclination is that men use mechanized equipment, and women use traditional methods for farming and post-harvest processing. The customary thinking is that men understand the equipment better.

- Men are also responsible for benchmarking for the next season, constructing storage facilities, operating machinery, and other value addition activities. Men handle the field pesticide spraying, sales, machinery, and livestock health monitoring.
  - They are also involved in on-ploughing the fields, hedging, and harvesting.

- **Youths** are involved in herding livestock, field labour and household chores. They also help men with transporting and marketing produce. Youth have no power and depend on their parents in this region.

#### Access to productive assets is limited too.
  - Property acquired in the household is automatically the man's. As such, men have more access to assets than women and youths; not only land but also livestock. When a woman gets married, she forfeits the rights to family property – although more


---
**Page 220**
---

FL-RS preferences, barriers & solutions

- **Mechanical multi-crop threshers** are a high preference for women but have high capital costs, literacy levels and access to credit are barriers.
- Some examples of solutions include rent-to-use schemes, agricultural leasing teams, savings groups, and skills development.
- Metal and plastic lids were also noted as a high preference. However, materials used can be costly if there is a need to simplify designs and use local materials.

The availability of hermetic bags and polypropylene plastic bags was considered a barrier to adoption – learning should be drawn from the ERPD program which distributed some bags.
- Tarpsaulin and plastic sheets are available; these solutions are not new, and therefore can be easily adopted. They offer flexibility, flexible payment options, and other financial solutions.

Storage structures (storage sheds, huts, baskets) were of high preference to women, who stated that there is a need for increased durability of such products.
- Cost of construction was another barrier; most respondents preferred community-based storage solutions through aggregation as a suggested potential solution.
- Storage protectants and control agents are of high preference to women, but access to these needs to be accompanied by awareness raising on the effects of chemicals on women, alternative options, and safe ways to use and dispose of chemicals. Artificially was also flagged as a potential concern.
